Declaration  The undersigned declares that this EIS: 
▪ has been prepared in accordance with the Environmental Planning and 
Assessment Regulation 2021; 
▪ contains all available information relevant to the environmental 
assessment of the development, activity, or infrastructure to which the 
EIS relates; 
▪ does not contain information that is false or misleading; 
▪ addresses the Planning Secretary’s environmental assessment 
requirements (SEARs) for the project; 
▪ identifies and addresses the relevant statutory requirements for the 
project, including any relevant matters for consideration in 
environmental planning instruments; 
▪ has been prepared having regard to the DPHI’s State Significant 
Development Guidelines - Preparing an Environmental Impact 
Statement; 
▪ contains a simple and easy to understand summary of the project as a 
whole, having regard to the economic, environmental, and social 
impacts of the project and the principles of ecologically sustainable 
development; 
▪ contains a consolidated description of the project in a single chapter of 
the EIS; 
▪ contains an accurate summary of the findings of any community 
engagement; and 
▪ contains an accurate summary of the detailed technical assessment of 
the impacts of the project as a whole. 
Signature 
 
Date 2 February 2026

P a g e  11 
PART 1: SUMMARY  
1.1 Overview & Project Description 
This State Significant Development Application (SSDA) is for the staged construction and operation of 
a 1.2-Gigawatt (GW) Data Centre Campus (the proposal) at 706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (the site). 
This Environmental Impact Statement (EIS) has been prepared by Willowtree Planning on behalf of Plan 
Project Management Pty Ltd (the Proponent) to support the proposed development and consider all 
relevant statutory and environmental matters . In short, the proposal involves the staged construction 
and operation of a Data Centre Campus, comprising:  
 
▪ Site preparation works, including tree clearing;  
▪ Dam dewatering;  
▪ Site remediation;  
▪ Bulk earthworks across the site and retaining structures;  
▪ Battering to adjoining properties to the south and east;  
▪ Infrastructure comprising civil works, stormwater, and utilities servicing, including:  
o Lead-in power infrastructure from TransGrid Sydney West  Substation (three lead-in 
connections proposed).  
o Sediment basins and interim stormwater management measures in advance of 
connection to regional stormwater infrastructure.  
▪ Construction of future local roads through the site, including: 
o North-south local road (Road 1); 
o East-west local road (Road 2);  
o Temporary access road from Mamre Road (for construction purposes); and  
o Roundabout at the southern site boundary; 
▪ Land allocation for: 
o Mamre Road Stage 2 upgrade widening delivered by TfNSW; and 
o Southern Link Road (SLR) ultimate scenario delivered by TfNSW; and  
o Future automated vehicle route running north to south through the site; 
▪ Staged construction and 24/7 operation of a Data Centre Campus containing the following:  
o 172,640m2 of technical data hall floor space, designed to accommodate 26 shells 
spread across 6 purpose-built (buildings) sub parcels A-F (maximum building height 
of 40m measured from proposed ground level); 
o 54,854 m2 of ancillary office & corridor floor space; 
o Plant equipment including 728 cooling units;  
o 846 backup generators  
o Fuel storage on Site is 18,056 kilolitres, per Shell configuration comprises of diesel 
storage via 5 x 132 kL Bulk Tanks and 34 x 1 kL Day Tanks, with additional 2 x 1 kL Day 
Tanks for shells with tech space ;  
o Total of 2,652,000 Kg of PTU Lithium-ion battery storage; 
o 2 x 433,800L water tanks for fire protection;  
o 619 at-grade car parking spaces;  
o Wayfinding signage; 
o One (1) incoming electrical switching station and campus electrical substation;  
o One (1) static synchronous compensator (STATCOM); and  
o Six (6) sub-parcel electrical substations.  
▪ Acoustic and visual screening along the western and northern boundaries; 
▪ Complementary landscaping and offset planting.  
▪ Combined sprinkler and hydrant system with dual water supply meeting FM Global and 
Australian Standard requirements.  
▪ Total estimated construction period of approximately 10 years from commencement, subject 
to market demand, tenant requirements, and regulatory approvals: 
 
[[page 18]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  12  
The proposed development also incorporates land beyond the main development site to 
accommodate electrical infrastructure and earthworks, as discussed in Section 4. 
 
The proposed development is to be constructed in the following phases: 
 
▪ Phase 0 * - Conduct western bulk earthworks  within Stage 1 area , establish temporary 
construction access, and construct boundary retaining walls. 
▪ Phase 1 - Deliver core campus infrastructure, services, access roads, and full Parcel A facilities. 
▪ Phase 2 - Construct Parcel B comprising four data halls with associated support infrastructure 
and access. 
▪ Phase 3 - Develop Parcel C with data halls, support infrastructure, and a northern extension of 
the campus access road. 
▪ Phase 4 - Deliver Parcel D with data halls, utilities, an eastern road extension, and a temporary 
emergency access route. 
▪ Phase 5  - Complete eastern earthworks and construct Parcel E, Road 2, drainage works, 
perimeter access, and landscaping. 
▪ Phase 6  - Construct Parcel F with five data halls, support facilities, utilities, and access 
infrastructure.  
▪ Phase 7** - Construct Road 1 from the roundabout to the Southern Link Road corridor with a 
temporary turning head. 
 
*Phases 0 and 7 not shown on plan as these are civil related only 
**Stage 3 shown is by others and not part of this proposal 
 
Figure 1 below identifies the proposed phasing across the site and Figure 2 shows the development as 
viewed from the corner of Mamre Road and the future SLR.

The proposed development is a Sate Significant Development (SSD) pursuant to Chapter 2, Schedule 1, 
Clause 25 of State Environmental Planning Policy (Planning Systems) 2021  (Planning Systems SEPP), 
as it is development for the purpose of a data centre with a total power consumption of 1.2 GW, 
exceeding the 15-megawatt threshold. The Secretary of the Department of Planning, Housing and 
Infrastructure (DPHI) issued project -specific Secretary's Environmental Assessment Requirements 
(SEARs) (reference: SSD -92743706) on 30 September 2025, in accordance with Section 4.39 of the 
Environmental Planning and Assessment Act 1979 (EP&A Act). 
 
 
Figure 2: Photomontage of Mamre Road and Future SLR (15-year scenario) 
Source: Geoscapes, 2025  
1.2 The Site 
The site is legally described as Lot 10 Deposited Plan (DP) 1280592 and is located at 706 -752 Mamre 
Road, Kemps Creek, within the Penrith Local Government Area (LGA). Notable attributes include: 
 
▪ It forms part of the Mamre Road Precinct (MRP) within the Western Sydney Employment Area 
(WSEA), which was rezoned by the NSW Government in June 2020 to facilitate 850 hectares of 
industrial land.  
▪ It is zoned IN1 General Industrial under State Environmental Planning Policy (Industry and 
Employment) 2021 (Industry and Employment SEPP), with a portion of land adjoining Mamre 
Road zoned SP2 Infrastructure for future road acquisition and upgrades.  
▪ The overall area is approximately 52 hectares, is located at the intersection of Mamre Road and 
Bakers Lane, with frontages of 1.1 kilometres to Bakers Lane (north) and 490 metres to Mamre 
Road (east).  Bakers Lane and surrounding land areas have been identified for the future 
Southern Link Road to be delivered by Transport for NSW (TfNSW). 
▪ The site is currently vacant and contains six dams, with predominantly cleared grassland and 
small patches of remnant native vegetation.  
▪ Most of the site is classified as urban -capable certified land under the Cumberland Plain 
Conservation Plan, with only a small northeastern portion containing biodiversity values. 
▪ The site slopes from northeast to southwest, falling approximately 43 metres across the site. 
▪ The site is surrounded by industrial and employment land uses consistent with the precinct's 
character. Directly north across Bakers Lane are Mamre Anglican School, Trinity Catholic 
Primary School, Emmaus Catholic College, and Emmaus Retirement Village. 
▪ Surrounding development includes warehouses, logistics facilities and emerging data centres, 
reflecting the precinct's evolution as a major industrial and technology hub. The site is located 

[[page 20]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  14  
approximately 8.5 kilometres northeast of Western Sydney Airport, with excellent connectivity 
to the M4 and M7 motorways via Mamre Road. 
1.3 Planning and Legislative Framework 
All relevant Federal and State legislation and Environmental Planning Instruments (EPIs) have been 
considered in the preparation of this EIS. The proposed development  is satisfactory in terms of its 
legislative context, on the basis that:  
 
▪ The proposed development is wholly permissible with consent in the IN1 General Industrial 
zone under State Environmental Planning Policy (Industry and Employment) 2021  (Industry 
and Employment SEPP);  
▪ The proposed development is considered consistent with the objectives of the zone  by 
facilitating employment-generating development;  
▪ All applicable State Environmental Planning Policies (SEPPs) have been considered  and are 
satisfied; 
▪ The Mamre Road Development Control Plan 2021 (MRP DCP 2021) has been considered, and 
the proposed development  generally complies with the relevant controls , with detailed 
justification provided for any departures; 
▪ Strategic policies that apply to the locality and wider region have been considered and the 
proposed development is consistent with the strategic context of the area, supporting NSW to 
become a leader in adoption and use of digital technology as envisaged by the State 
Infrastructure Strategy and the delivery of critical digital infrastructure for the Western Sydney 
Aerotropolis; and 
▪ The proposed development  can satisfy the relevant provisions of the National Construction 
Code (NCC) and applicable Australian Standards. 
 
The proposed development  has been assessed against the relevant statutory requirements, 
preconditions and mandatory considerations as addressed within Section 2.2 of this EIS and the 
supporting technical reports. The matters identified in the SEARs issued for the proposed development 
have been assessed in detail, with specialist reports underpinning the key findings and 
recommendations to address the  requirements (refer to PART 7: ASSESSMENT AND MITAGATION OF 
IMPACTS of this EIS for details).  
1.4 Engagement 
The Proponent and broader project team have undertaken extensive community and stakeholder 
engagement in the preparation of this EIS. This has included consultation with various stakeholders, 
including relevant government agencies (such as NSW EPA, Transpo rt for NSW, and Penrith City 
Council), utility providers, Aboriginal community representatives, surrounding industrial landowners 
and businesses, and local residents. 
 
Targeted and ongoing consultation has been carried out with sensitive receivers to the north of the site 
along Bakers Lane, including Mamre Anglican School, Emmaus Catholic College, Trinity Catholic 
Primary School, and Catholic Healthcare's Emmaus Retireme nt Village and Residential Aged Care 
Home. Initial meetings were held in October 2025 to brief these facilities on the proposed development 
and receive feedback, with follow -up meetings in November 2025 where a revised scheme was 
presented incorporating de sign changes in response to their concerns. Key design refinements 
included relocating buildings C and D to provide greater setbacks from the schools, and minimising 
construction traffic movements on Bakers Lane by shifting primary access to Berri -Werri Drive to the 
south. These consultations also informed the preparation of technical assessments, including noise and 
vibration, air quality, and visual impact studies specifically addressing impacts on these northern 
receivers.

The proposed development incorporates feedback received through community consultation, as 
detailed in PART 6: COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT of this EIS and the Community Engagement Report 
located at Appendix 32  Community Engagement Report.  
1.5 Environmental Impact Assessment  
An assessment of environmental impact has been undertaken against the relevant planning controls 
and policies in accordance with the requirements of the issued SEARs. Additionally, a number of expert 
consultants have been engaged to specifically consider r elevant aspects of the proposal. The 
environmental impact assessment has found that the proposed development satisfies with the relevant 
controls, and it is considered that appropriate mitigation measures can be put in place to minimise any 
identified risks. 
1.6 Justification for the Proposed Development 
The proposed development is justified for the following reasons:  
 
▪ The proposal represents $9 billion investment in NSW's digital infrastructure . The proposed 
development responds to Australia's rapidly expanding technology sector and strengthening 
position as a critical digital hub within the APAC region. 
▪ During construction , it is estimated to result in a significant ( over a fixed period ) increase in 
economic activity across Greater Sydney, through a mix of direct and indirect (flow -on) activity, 
including: • 
- $13.7 billion in output (including $6.7 billion in direct activity).  
- $5 billion in contributions to Gross Regional Product (GRP) (including $1.6 billion in direct 
activity).  
- $2.8 billion in incomes and salaries paid to households (including $1 billion in direct 
income). 29,959 ongoing FTE jobs (including 10,569 FTE directly related to activity at the 
site). 
▪ When operational, the proposal is estimated to result in an increase in economic activity across 
Greater Sydney:  
- $1.4 billion in output (including $661.2 million in direct activity).  
- $633.8 million in contributions to GRP (including $274.3 million in direct activity).  
- $326.9 million in incomes and salaries paid to households (including $135.1 million in direct 
income). – 
- 2,775 ongoing FTE jobs (including 800 FTE directly related to activity at the Site). 
▪ The proposed development is crucial component of the continued growth in Australia’s data 
storage sector, which businesses, households, and public authorities are reliant on for day -to-
day operation.  
▪ Engagement with relevant community, government, and agency stakeholders has been 
undertaken and all comments have been carefully considered when designing the proposal. 
▪ The EIS appropriately addresses all strategic and statutory considerations, including the items 
raised in the SEARs.  
▪ The proposed development  will not result in any unacceptable social, economic, or 
environmental impacts. 
▪ Suitable mitigation measures and conditions of consent can be implemented during the 
construction and operation of the development.

PART 2: INTRODUCTION 
2.1 Introduction  
This EIS has been prepared by Willowtree Planning on behalf of Plan Project Management Pty Ltd (the 
Proponent) and is submitted to the NSW Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI), 
in support of a State Significant Development Application (SSDA) for the Mamre Road Data Centre 
Campus, involving the staged construction and operation of a 1.2 -GW Data Centre Campus at 706-752 
Mamre Road, Kemps Creek. 
 
This EIS describes the site and proposed development, responds to the SEARs, and assesses the 
proposed development against all relevant legislative requirements, EPIs, and planning policies.

and Appendix 33  Mamre Road Precinct Development Control Plan 2021 Assessment of this EIS.

development is development for the purpose of a data centre that has a total power consumption of

4.39 of the EP&A Act.  
2.9 Objectives of the Proposed Development 
The objectives of the proposed development are to: 
 
▪ Optimise proximity to existing power and telecommunications infrastructure to support NSW's 
digital economy growth and Australia's digital sovereignty  and enable Western Sydney's 
transformation into a technology and innovation hub. 
▪ Provide sovereign data storage capacity and low -latency data processing for Australian 
businesses and government, addressing data infrastructure gaps identified in the National Data 
Infrastructure Strategy. 
▪ Deliver critical digital infrastructure that supports NSW to become a leader in adoption and use 
of digital technology, consistent with the State Infrastructure Strategy 2018-2028. 
[[page 25]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  19  
▪ Support Australia's digital sovereignty and data security priorities as recognised in the National 
Digital Economy Strategy, enabling compliance with data sovereignty requirements across 
financial services, healthcare, government and defence sectors. 
▪ Deliver a state -of-the-art hyperscale data centre campus that provides enterprise and 
government cloud services with network redundancy and data backup capabilities. 
▪ Leverage the strategic location within the Mamre Road Precinct to support the Western Sydney 
Employment Area transformation, contributing to the precinct's target of 17,000 jobs and 
catalysing broader digital economy growth. 
▪ Achieve a high -quality design outcome appropriate to the evolving industrial context of the 
site, incorporating sustainability measures that minimise environmental and amenity impacts. 
▪ Increase global resilience by distributing data within a stable location with minimal natural 
disaster risk and strong governance. 
▪ Provide secure data storage in a suitable location, increasing digital access speed and reducing 
latency to Greater Sydney customers. 
▪ To provide opportunities to s upport network redundancy and data backup requirements for 
enterprise and government cloud services while avoiding offshore hosting. 
2.10 Development History 
ISPT Summit   
 
A Concept Masterplan (SSD -30628110) has been lodged by ISPT Pty for the development of eight (8) 
industrial warehouse buildings with ancillary office space and a café, totalling an indicative Gross Floor 
Area (GFA) of 244,413m². The proposal was publicly exhibited from 19 March 2024 to 15 April 2024 and

PART 3: STRATEGIC CONTEXT 
This section of the EIS identifies the key issues that are relevant to the proposal’s locational and strategic 
context and provides a justification based on the key strategic themes , guidelines and directions that 
are relevant to the locality. This section also provides an analysis of alternatives considered. 
3.1 Site Description and Context  
The site is located at 706 -752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek and is legally described as Lot 1 0 in DP 
1280592 and comprises a total area of approximately 522,477m² (52.2ha) and is rectangular in shape. 
The site has a primary frontage to Bakers Lane at the northern boundary, spanning approximately 1.1km. 
A secondary frontage to Mamre Road is located along the western boundary, spanning approximately 
495m. 
 
The site is undeveloped rural land, one residential dwelling, interspersed vegetation, livestock, and a 
number of farm dams. The site is characterised by undulating topography, with natural ground levels 
ranging from RL 85.5 AHD at the highest point in the southeastern corner, to RL 42.5 AHD at the lowest 
point in the southwestern corner. A ridgeline runs across the site, generally aligned from southeast to 
northwest, with ground slopes either side of the ridgeline generally between 6% and 15%. Elsewhere 
across the site, the ground slope is generally 2-3%. 
 
The site  is generally urban capable  and includes 13.24ha of native vegetation, with the remainder 
classified as exotic vegetation, cleared land and dams. The native vegetation comprises two Plant 
Community Types (PCTs): Grey Box – Forest Red Gum grassy woodland on shale of the southern 
Cumberland Plain (PCT 850, covering 13.02ha) and Swamp Oak open forest on riverflats of the 
Cumberland Plain and Hunter Valley (PCT 1800, covering 0.22ha). 
 
The entirety of the site is identified as 'Vegetation Category 3' bushfire prone land under the Penrith 
Bush Fire Prone Land map. There are no state or local heritage items located on or in close proximity 
to the site. 
 
The site is located within the MRP, which sits within both the Western Sydney Aerotropolis and the 
Western Sydney Employment Area (WSEA)  Structure Plan . The site is surrounded by industrial 
development to the north (refer to the site context map in  Figure 11. Surrounding land uses in the 
immediate vicinity of the site include: 
 
▪ North – To the north of the site, is: 
o The Mamre Anglican School 
o Trinity Catholic Primary School  
o Emmaus Catholic College 
o Emmaus Retirement Village.  
o Erskine Park Industrial Area  
 
▪ South – The site's southern surrounds comprise the GPT Yiribana West estate  which has 
substantially pro gressed construction of initial warehouses  and Mirvac Aspect estate , with 
operational warehouses and further warehouses under construction. 
 
▪ East – Industrial estates under construction, including Stockland/Fife with Oakdale West and 
South Industrial estates, approximately 1-2km of the site. 
▪ West – The Yards Estat e (approved in 202 and approximately 70% occupie d) is directly 
opposite the site on the adjacent side of Mamre Road, with rural agricultural land also located 
in the broader vicinity. 
 
[[page 35]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  29  
The Western Sydney International (Nancy-Bird Walton) Airport site is located approximately 8km to the 
southwest of the site.

Current Use The site is currently vacant and contains six dams, with vegetation comprising 
predominantly cleared grassland and small patches of remnant native vegetation.  
Topography  The site exhibits a natural fall from northeast to southwest, with existing levels 
ranging from RL 85.5 AHD at the highest point in the southeastern corner, to RL 
42.5 AHD at the lowest point in the southwestern corner near the intersection of 
Mamre Road and the western boundary. This represents an overall fall of 
approximately 43 metres across the site. The to pography generally slopes in a 
south-westerly direction toward Mamre Road, with intermediate levels of 
approximately RL 81.49 AHD recorded across the central and northern portions of 
the site along the Bakers Lane frontage.

TABLE 5: SITE CHARACTERISTICS

Figure 18. Existing Site Topography 
Source: Greenbox Architecture, 2025 
Access The site has a primary frontage to Bakers Lane of approximately 1.1 kilometres 
along the northern boundary and a secondary frontage to Mamre Road of 
approximately 490 metres along the eastern boundary. TfNSW has proposed a 
new Southern Link road which will occupy Bakers Lane and a portion of the site 
providing an east -west connection between Mamre Road , Kemps Creek and 
Wallgrove Road, Horsley Park. 
Biodiversity  Much of the site is classified as urban -capable certified land under the 
Cumberland Plain Conservation Plan, with only a small portion at the northeastern 
boundary identified as containing biodiversity values requiring management. The 
site contains fragmented native vegetation including two Plant Community Types: 
PCT 3320 (Cumberland Shale Plains Woodland) and PCT 3975 (Southern Lower 
Floodplain Freshwater Wetland), representing two Threatened Ecological 
Communities (TEC) under the BC Act. Cumberland Plain Woodland is also listed 
as a Critically Endangered Ecological Community under the EPBC Act and meets 
Serious and Irreversible Impact principles.  Direct impacts include the removal of 
1.36 hectares of fragmented, disturbed native vegetation within non-certified land, 
including 0.94 hectares of Cumberland Plain Woodland CEEC.  As outlined in the 
Biodiversity Development Assessment Report (Appendix 15  Biodiversity 
Development Assessment Report ) the proposal is not likely to impact an 
ecological MNES and therefore a referral to the Commonwealth is not required. 
Geology  The site is underlain by Quaternary deposits comprising fine-grained sand, silt and 
clay. The north-western and south-western portions of the site are mapped in the 
Bringelly Shale outcrop zone, consisting of shale, carbonaceous claystone, 
claystone, laminate, fine to medium grained lithic sandstone and rare coal and 
tuff. 
Salinity 
Potential 
Soils are classified as non-saline to slightly saline with electrical conductivity below 
AS3600-2018 A2 exposure classification limits. 
Acid Sulfate 
Soils 
The site has low acid sulfate  soil risk, being above 40m elevation with Bringelly 
Shale geology and not mapped on NSW Acid Sulfate Soil Risk Maps.  
Bushfire  The site is mapped as Category 3 Vegetation and Vegetation Buffer bushfire prone 
land.  
Watercourses The site contains six existing dams requiring dewatering as part of site preparation 
works. The site is located outside the extent of mainstream flooding from Kemps 
Creek and Wianamatta-South Creek, above Probable Maximum Flood levels, and 
outside the Penrith Local Environmental Plan 2010 (PLEP) flood planning area.  
Wetlands  The site contains Southern Lower Floodplain Freshwater Wetland (PCT 3975) 
vegetation community, classified as a Threatened Ecological Community under 
the BC Act. 
Aboriginal 
Heritage 
There are six recorded Aboriginal Heritage Information Management System 
(AHIMSs) on the southern portion of the subject site.

3.2 Strategic Planning Context 
A summary of the proposal’s consistency with the relevant strategic context, as established by 
government plans, policies and guidelines set out below:   
3.2.1 National Data Infrastructure Strategy & National Digital Economy Strategy 
The National Digital Economy Strategy identifies data centres as critical digital infrastructure and 
prioritises meeting the rapidly growing demand for domestic data processing capacity to support 
digital sovereignty and data security. The National Data In frastructure Strategy further emphasises 
Australia's data infrastructure gaps and the need for sovereign data storage capacity, low-latency data 
processing for Australian businesses and government, and enhanced support for cloud computing and 
digital services.  
 
The proposed development directly aligns with this national policy guidance by providing essential 
digital infrastructure to support Australia's growing data processing requirements and strengthening 
the nation's digital sovereignty and economic resilience. 
3.2.2 National AI Plan 2025 
The National AI Plan 2025 establishes the Australian Government's strategic framework for artificial 
intelligence development, adoption and governance across the economy. The Plan identifies AI as a 
transformative technology reshaping how Australians work,  learn and connect, with the ambition to 
create a fairer, stronger Australia where every person benefits from technological change. 
The Plan is structured around three core goals that guide government action: 
 
• Capturing the opportunities  – fostering investment in world -class digital and physical 
infrastructure, supporting local AI capability and attracting global partnerships to position 
Australia as a leader in AI innovation and applications. 

[[page 43]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  37  
• Spreading the benefits – ensuring all Australians, regardless of background or location, share 
in AI advantages through support for small and medium enterprises, regional communities, 
digital skills development, workforce transitions and improved public services. 
• Keeping Australians safe – establishing robust legal, regulatory and ethical frameworks that 
protect rights and build trust, including the establishment of an AI Safety Institute, ongoing 
review and adaptation of laws, and engagement with international partners to manage risks 
and promote responsible innovation. 
 
The proposed development directly aligns with this national policy guidance by providing essential 
digital infrastructure to support Australia's AI ecosystem. Data centres are explicitly identified in the 
Plan as critical infrastructure for realising AI op portunities, with Australia attracting over $10 billion in 
data centre investment during 2024 and plans announced for investments that could scale to more 
than $100 billion. The proposed development would contribute to the Plan's objectives by enabling 
local AI capability, supporting sovereign data processing capacity, and positioning Australia as a 
leading destination for AI investment in the Indo-Pacific region. 
3.2.3 Greater Sydney Region Plan – A Metropolis of Three Cities 
The Greater Sydney Region Plan – A Metropolis of Three Cities (A Metropolis of Three Cities) established 
the strategic framework focusing o n three (3) interconnected cities within the greater Sydney 
metropolitan area. This strategic framework was further strengthen by the Western Sydney City Deal, 
signed on 4 March 20218, which established a partnership between the Australian and NSW 
governments and eight local councils that made up the Western Parkland City to drive transformative 
change over 20 years. 
 
The Metropolis of Three Cities aims to anticipate the housing and employment needs of a growing and 
vastly changing population. The overall vision pursues an objective of transforming ‘Greater Sydney’ 
into A Metropolis of Three Cities, including:  
  
▪ The Western Parkland City;  
▪ The Central River City; and   
▪ The Eastern Harbour City. 
 
The proposal contributes to the four (4) standardised elements as follows:  
 
▪ Infrastructure and collaboration  – the proposal would facilitate the provision of services to 
support the wider locality and region. 
▪ Liveability – the proposal encourages employment -generating opportunities and economic 
prosperity, which has positive influences on the wider locality and supports the growth of the 
Western Parkland City. 
▪ Productivity – the proposal provides critical digital infrastructure that underpins Australia's AI-
enabled economy, supporting national productivity gains and enabling businesses across all 
sectors to leverage AI technologies as outlined in the National AI Plan 2025. 
▪ Sustainability – the proposal will provide sustainable design measures that align with the 
principles established for the precinct. 
 
The proposed development contributes to the objectives set out by promoting sustainable 
development and economic opportunities to the wider locality and community, positioned within the 
strategically important Mamre Road Precinct of the Western Sydney Aerotropolis precinct plan in 
Figure 20.

3.2.4 Western City District Plan  
The site is located within the Western City District, which forms the majority of the Western Parkland 
City. The Western City District Plan (the District Plan) provides a 20-year strategic framework to manage 
growth and achieve the 40 -year vision for Great er Sydney established in A Metropolis of Three Cities.  
As outlined in Figure 21, the volume of released land for ‘Potential Future Industrial/ Employment Land’ 
is vast and encircles the site.  
 
 
Figure 21: Western City District Industrial and Urban Service Land and Freight Assets 
Source: Greater Sydney Commission, March 2018 
Central to the District Plan is the transformation of Western Sydney through the Western Sydney 
Airport and Badgerys Creek Aerotropolis, supported by the first stage of the North South Rail Link. This 
infrastructure investment is designed to create a Weste rn Economic Corridor , a spine of knowledge -
intensive employment that will diversify the economy and attract a skilled workforce. The District Plan 
identifies that at full operation, the airport will create at least 60,000 jobs across logistics, trade, 
aerospace and defence, advanced manufacturing, and tourism, with this agglomeration of knowledge 
jobs anchoring the Western Economic Corridor's development. 
The Site 
[[page 46]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  40  
 
The Mamre Road Precinct, where the site is located, plays a critical role in this transformation. Rezoned 
on 11 June 2020, the precinct is envisioned as a world-class industrial area providing approximately 850 
hectares of industrial land with capacity for around 17,000 jobs. The precinct is strategically positioned 
to support the Western Sydney Airport through warehousing, logistics, and industrial uses, with 
planned access to the Western Sydney Freight Line, intermodal terminal, M12 Motorway, and Elizabeth 
Drive. 
 
The District Plan establishes Planning Priorities that guide development outcomes. The following 
priorities are directly relevant to the proposed development: 
 
▪ Planning Priority W1: Planning for a city supported by infrastructure.

advanced manufacturing, logistics, and knowledge -intensive servic es. The proposed development 
provides essential infrastructure capacity that must be delivered to meet demand to enable continued 
business investment and employment growth.

road infrastructure. The  proposed developments compatibility with 24/7 operations and freight -

supporting rather than competing with traditional logistics and warehousing functions.

defence, advanced manufacturing, and logistics sectors identified as priorities. The $ 9 billion capital

Western Sydney.

development cre ates the digital foundation necessary for Liverpool, Greater Penrith, and

the industrial and logistics precincts that serve them.

3.2.5 Future Transport Strategy 2056 
The Future Transport Strategy 2056 is a 40 -year strategic framework supported by complementary 
plans for regional NSW and Greater Sydney. The Strategy delivers integrated movement and place 
outcomes by aligning land use and transport planning to achieve pr oductive economic growth, 
liveable communities, and sustainable transport solutions. 
 
The Strategy specifically prioritises the development of the Western Parkland City, including the 
Western Sydney Aerotropolis and surrounding employment lands where the site is located. This 
strategic focus supports the transformational economic and social opportunities generated by Western 
Sydney International Airport and the emerging Aerotropolis, positioning the region as a key growth 
area for Greater Sydney's future development. 
 
The Strategy recognises that an efficient transport system results in greater economic performance by 
enabling businesses to reach new markets, attract investment, and access job opportunities. Transport 
infrastructure that supports economic growth is prio ritised, particularly where it reduces network 
inefficiency and supports productivity  and have committed to delivering the following transport 
corridors:  
 
Future Transport 2056 promotes technology -enabled mobility services and data -driven transport 
models that respond flexibly to demand. The Strategy emphasises the role of transport in unlocking 
new commercial developments and supporting economic investment through long-term, place-based 
planning that provides certainty for industry location and investment decisions. 
 
The proposal aligns with the strategies and objectives of the Future Transport Strategy 2056 on the 
following basis: 
 
▪ The site has access to regular public transport services, supporting worker accessibility 
and reducing reliance on private vehicles; 
▪ The site is accessible by active transport modes and provides adequate bicycle parking 
facilities to encourage sustainable transport choices;

3.2.9 Mamre Road Development Control Plan (2021) 
The Mamre Road Precinct Development Control Plan 2021 was adopted on 17 November 2021 and 
commenced on 19 November 2021. The DCP provides the framework for coordinated and orderly 
planning and delivery of infrastructure, land uses, supporting facilities an d environmental protection 
within the Precinct. The proposed development is consistent with the relevant controls across the 
DCP's five chapters, including: 
 
▪ Chapter 2 Precinct Planning Outcomes (biodiversity conservation, integrated water cycle 
management, flood prone land, heritage, aviation safeguarding and utilities services); 
▪ Chapter 3 Precinct and Subdivision Design (subdivision design, views and visual impacts, 
interface considerations, and transport network); and  
▪ Chapter 4 General Requirements for Industrial Development (building height, setbacks, 
landscaping, building design, amenity considerations, earthworks, waste management, and 
access and parking). 
 
The proposal demonstrates consistency with the DCP's overarching objectives to ensure development 
occurs in an orderly manner with sufficient infrastructure, services and amenities to support growth 
while protecting environmental, heritage and amenity values.  
 
The proposed height of the built form of the proposed development is required due to the nature of 
data centre operations as is evident in the approved design (including height) of other data centres in 
the precinct. This proposal demonstrates that the proposed built form would not create unacceptable 
solar, wind and visual impacts on surrounding sensitive uses, including the nearby schools and 
retirement village. 
 
A full and comprehensive assessment of the DCP provisions is contained at Appendix 33  Mamre 
Road Precinct Development Control Plan 2021 Assessment. 
3.3 Site Suitability 
The site is considered suitable for the proposed hyperscale data centre due to its permissible IN1 zoning, 
strategic location within the Mamre Road Precinct, access to essential power and telecommunications 
infrastructure, proximity to end users and skilled labour, and appropria te management of 
environmental constraints.  In summary, the site characteristics and location are suitable because: 
▪ The site is strategically located to serve Greater Sydney and the emerging Western Parkland 
City, within optimal distance to major business and employment centres including Parramatta, 
Liverpool, Penrith, Norwest Business Park, and the future Western Sydne y Aerotropolis 
including Bradfield City Centre. The benefit of being located close to major markets is 
improved response times and reduced latency to the end user, which is an important success 
factor for any hyperscale data centre facility.  
▪ The site is positioned to serve customer demand within a 10km radius while contributing to 
the geographic distribution of critical digital infrastructure across the Sydney Metropolitan 
area.  
▪ The site is well supported by the high -power outputs and water infrastructure provided by 
Sydney Water required to operate a successful hyperscale data centre campus. 
▪ High-voltage transmission infrastructure is available via the TransGrid planning network 
augmentation. 
▪ No off-site road upgrades required for the proposed development to operate. 
▪ Due to the size of the site, visual impacts can be suitably managed by way of building setbacks, 
materiality, landscaping zones with substantial tree plantings reaching heights similar to the 
built form and finished building pad levels.

▪ The overall character of the  proposed development is consistent with that intended for the 
850ha Mamre Road Precinct and broader Western Sydney Aerotropolis , that will also include 
an intermodal opposite the site in the future.   
3.4 Consideration of Feasible Alternatives 
Section 192 of the EP&A Regulation requires an analysis of any feasible alternatives to the proposed 
development, including the consequences of not carrying out the development. The Proponent 
identified project alternatives with respect to the need for dat a centre infrastructure that maximises 
economic and social benefits. Design options were considered to address the strategic need, project 
objectives, and site constraints and opportunities. Each of the options considered and the analysis that 
led to the preferred concept are summarised below. 
 
Option 1 - Do Nothing 
 
'Doing nothing' would be inconsistent with the envisaged outcomes for the site in accordance with the 
Western Sydney Employment Area and Mamre Road Precinct planning priorities. Not carrying out the 
proposal would result in a missed opportunity to realise the vision for the site and implement an 
internationally competitive data centre campus. 'Doing nothing' would result in minimal economic 
benefit, reduced employment generation, and would not support the growing data centre sector in 
Greater Sydney or contribute to the precinct's target of 17,000 jobs. 
 
Option 2 - Design Alternatives 
 
A master planning process was conducted to assess the feasibility of a data centre campus with a 
maximum building height of 20 metres. Given the significantly lower IT capacity achieved, this 
outcome would not optimise colocation of data storage facilities  and was not considered an efficient 
use of the site. Hyperscale data centres are significantly more efficient than smaller -scale enterprises 
and public sector organisations, using less energy to perform the same unit of work, with hyperscale 
facilities being up to five times more efficient than on-premises servers and smaller-scale data centres. 
If an alternative design was pursued at a lower scale, the proposed development would not achieve co-
location efficiencies and instead would result in multiple data centre buildings having to be built at 
other sites in Western Sydney, consuming valuable and strategically identified employment lands.  This 
is considered an inferior outcome as it does not allow for efficiencies of colocation within a single 
campus.  
 
Option 3 - The Proposal 
 
Following consideration of the alternatives, the proposed design was determined to be the most 
successful in facilitating the best built form and amenity outcome for the site. The proposal is the 
outcome of extensive planning, urban design, and environmental considerations by the Proponent and 
lead design team, informed by consultation with key stakeholders detailed at Section 6.1 of this EIS. 
 
The proposal demonstrates suitability as: 
 
▪ It is strategically located within  the Mamre Road Precinct  that is rapidly transitioning to 
accommodate a range of large scale warehousing and industrial land uses that define the 
emerging character; thus the proposed development will be consistent with the desired future 
character of the precinct; 
▪ It can be mitigated in terms of traffic, biodiversity, noise, visual amenity or air quality  ,subject 
to standard management and mitigation measures; 
▪ It can accommodate all planned road infrastructure corridor (SLR and Stage 2 Mamre Road) 
along with the internal local road network. 
 
[[page 53]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  47  
The selected option reflects the key strategic objectives of the locality and best utilises the site to its 
available capacity, being the most viable outcome.  
3.5 Project Need  
The proposal is for a data centre campus which would provide secure, reliable and scalable solutions 
for cloud, content and large enterprise customers to house their high and rapidly growing volumes of 
data and information, while also providing opportunities for AI. Increasing the capacity and capability 
of secure data storage and cloud infrastructure is of regional and national significance , as if the need 
to provide AI training. 
 
The proposal is a ‘new economy’ use which facilitates a rapidly growing demand for data storage and 
processing. The Australian Government has identified cyber security as one of the key growth industry 
sectors. The cyber security sector is forecast to have an annual cyber security reven ue growth of 5.5% 
by 2026. Cyber Security Growth Centre, AustCyber, has prepared Australia’s Cyber Security Sector 
Competitiveness Plan 2022 (Sector Competitiveness Plan). The Sector Competitiveness Plan notes 
three key actions to grow revenue for the Australian cyber security sector. The proposed development 
is aligned with Key Action 2: Bolster domestic procurement and export capacity . The proposed 
development would assist in growing the domestic cyber security market and facilitating increased 
export capacity. 
 
Data centres are critical infrastructure for supporting secure regional and national government and 
business. The proposed development is aligned with the Greater Sydney Commission’s standardised 
elements, objectives, and priorities. 
 
Additionally, the proposed development would generate the following benefits:  
 
▪ Supporting Australia's digital sovereignty  – the proposed development addresses critical 
national infrastructure gaps identified in the National Data Infrastructure Strategy and National 
AI Plan 2025 by providing sovereign data storage capacity and low -latency data processing 
essential for Australian businesses and government operations. 
▪ Enabling AI capabilities – the proposed development provides essential digital infrastructure 
to support Australia's AI ecosystem, facilitating AI model training, deployment and innovation 
identified as national priorities in the National AI Plan 2025. 
▪ Strengthening digital connectivity  – the proposed development contributes to the digital 
backbone necessary for businesses across Western Sydney to access cloud computing and data 
processing services that underpin modern business operations and economic competitiveness. 
▪ Job creation and economic impacts  – construction and operational employment, combined 
with the enabling effect of digital infrastructure on other industries, creates direct and indirect 
economic benefits that strengthen the local and regional economy.

PART 4: PROJECT DESCRIPTION  
4.1 Detailed Description of Development 
The proposed Data Centre Campus serves as a critical hub for storing, processing, and managing large 
volumes of digital information. It  consists of multiple interconnected data shell buildings housing 
technical data halls, support infrastructure, and operational facilities. The Campus comprises separable 
portions east to wes t, divided by the future Road 1 , however will be under single operation and 
management.  
 
The proposed development particulars are as follows:  
 
▪ Site preparation works, including tree clearing;  
▪ Dam dewatering;  
▪ Site remediation;  
▪ Bulk earthworks across the site and retaining structures;  
▪ Battering to adjoining properties to the south and east;  
▪ Infrastructure comprising civil works, stormwater, and utilities servicing, including:  
o Lead-in power infrastructure from TransGrid Sydney West Substation.  
o Sediment basins and interim stormwater management measures in advance of 
connection to regional stormwater infrastructure.  
▪ Construction of future local roads through the site, including: 
o North-south local road (Road 1); 
o East-west local road (Road 2);  
o Temporary access road from Mamre Road (for construction purposes); and  
o Roundabout at the southern site boundary; 
▪ Land allocation for: 
o Mamre Road Stage 2 upgrade widening delivered by TfNSW;  
o Southern Link Road (SLR) ultimate scenario delivered by TfNSW; and  
o Future automated vehicle route running north to south through the site; 
▪ Staged construction and 24/7 operation of a Data Centre Campus containing the following:  
o 172,640m2 of technical data hall floor space, designed to accommodate 26 shells 
spread across 6 purpose -built buildings referred to as sub parcels A -F (maximum 
building height of 40m measured from proposed ground level); 
o 54,944 m2 of ancillary office & corridor floor space; 
o Plant equipment including 728 cooling units;  
o 846 backup generators;  
o Fuel storage on Site is 18,056 kilolitres, per Shell configuration comprises of diesel 
storage via 5 x 132 kL Bulk Tanks and 34 x 1 kL Day Tanks, with additional 2 x 1 kL 
Day Tanks for shells with tech space ;  
o Total of 2,652,000 Kg of PTU Lithium-Iron battery storage; 
o 2 x 433,800L water tanks for fire protection;  
o 619 at-grade car parking spaces;  
o Wayfinding signage; 
o One (1) incoming electrical switching station and campus electrical substation;  
o One (1) static synchronous compensator (STATCOM); and  
o Six (6) sub-parcel electrical substations.  
▪ Acoustic and visual screening along the western and northern boundaries; 
▪ Complementary landscaping and offset planting  including substantial planted buffer areas at 
the boundaries of the site to screen built form and create shade and habitat.  
▪ Combined sprinkler and hydrant system , with dual water supply meeting FM Global and 
Australian Standard requirements.  
▪ Total estimated construction period of approximately 10 years from commencement, subject 
to market demand, tenant requirements, and regulatory approvals:

4.4 Use and Operations 
Hours of Operation  
 
The site will operate for 24 -hours a day, seven days a week. This allows for the critical nature of their 
operations and includes loading and unloading, data centre and office operations.  Typical operations 
during the day and night include: 
 
Typical Daytime Activities:  
 
▪ Facility Operations & Monitoring : Continuous monitoring of IT systems, cooling, and power 
infrastructure by operations teams.  
▪ Maintenance & Servicing: Scheduled preventive maintenance on electrical, mechanical, and 
IT equipment.  
▪ Customer Access & Support: Client representatives may visit for equipment installation, audits, 
or technical consultations.  
▪ Logistics & Deliveries : Movement of hardware, spare parts, and consumables into secure 
areas.  
▪ Administrative Functions: Staff meetings, compliance checks, and reporting activities.  
  
Typical Nighttime Activities:  
 
▪ 24/7 System Monitoring: Network operations and security teams oversee system performance 
and respond to alerts.  
▪ Critical Updates & Backups : Software patches, data replication, and backup processes often 
occur during off-peak hours.  
▪ Reduced On -Site Staff: Skeleton crews manage essential operations, supported by remote 
monitoring.  
▪ Emergency Response: Teams remain on standby for power, cooling, or security incidents.

Data Centre Buildings 
 
The proposal includes 172,640m2 of technical data hall floor space, designed to accommodate 26 shells 
spread across six purpose -built buildings. Each building is made up of server data halls, plant and 
equipment areas, operations spaces, offices, and storage to support the efficient functioning of the 
building. The proposed development is staged to deliver the six purpose-built buildings and associated 
support facilities and infrastructure in phases.   
 
[[page 59]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  53  
The buildings are engineered to accommodate up to 1.04 GW of IT compute capacity, providing 
significant scalability to meet future demand. The modular configuration allows adaptable fit-out and 
phased deployment, ensuring rapid response to evolving technology requirements a nd customer 
specifications. Server hardware will be installed progressively by tenants and is expected to include a 
combination of cloud and AI compute, with final configurations determined by the operational needs 
of individual hyperscale tenants. This design approach ensures the facility remains adaptable, resilient, 
and capable of supporting next-generation digital infrastructure.  
 
COSH and Technology Building (Technical Offices)  
 
The proposal includes an additional 54,944m2 of ancillary office & corridor floor space. This is split with 
a range of 7,968 m2 to 9,428m2 in each sub-parcel technology buildings, including corridors and 4,126  
m2 as the Central Operations and Support Hub (COSH).   
 
The technology buildings in each sub-parcel will include a variety of open plan office space inclusive of 
meeting rooms, security operations rooms, network operations rooms, building and technical storage, 
loading docks and amenities for staff and visitors .  These technology buildings will be used as the 
primary personnel entry for the data centres on each sub-parcel and provides a comfortable ergonomic 
workplace for staff, contract personnel, customers and visitors alike, engaged the operations and 
maintenance of the data centre buildings on each sub -parcel.  Each technology building will have a 
high level of material finish quality and maintain views for passive surveillance to the front and rear of 
the data centres in each sub-parcel.   
 
The COSH building will be located centrally within the campus and co-located with the entry and exit 
roads.  This building will provide a comfortable, high -quality workplace with high levels of natural 
daylight and views across campus for staff, customers, operational contract staff and authorised 
visitors.  
 
The COSH includes the primary campus check-in point for our security team vetting personnel in and 
out of the campus as well as providing a range of other primary security functions.   On ground floor a 
series of meeting and board rooms, work points, end of  trip facilities, campus central first aid point, 
food and beverage and pedestrian entry are all provided for campus staff.   A warehouse for storage of 
critical spares and outdoor dining for those using the F&B offerings.   Further up the building, 
emergency accommodation, additional office workspace with a range of flexible work settings 
alongside high quality staff amenities provide high grade within the industry and market office 
accommodation for the campus centrally managed functions to be run from.

Canteen and Café 
 
A dual food and beverage offering integrated into the ground floor of the COSH is set to be provided 
for the benefit of all personnel on campus.   This consists of a café and a canteen with 288m2 front of 
house areas and 450m 2 in total, including back of house. The café and the canteen provide different 
offerings to customers, catering for a different level of service time and food offering, where the café is 
a traditional ‘grab & go’ and canteen offering both dine in and take out offerings.   Outdoor and indoor 
settings for a variety of group sizes provided across both giving customers an opportunity to relax, dine 
and break from the regular rhythm of the working campus.

4.6 Physical Design and Layout  
The proposal seeks development consent for the construction and operation of a Data Centre Campus 
with a total power consumption of up to 1.2 GW and a maximum building height of 40 metres  from 
finished ground level , supported by ancillary office space, electrical substations, back -up generators, 
diesel storage, lithium -ion batteries, bulk earthworks, associated supporting infrastructure (including 
road construction), car parking and landscaping.  
 
It is noted that the site comprises six (6) sub -parcels, identified as Parcels A -F, which collectively 
accommodate each building. Each parcel will contain either four or five data centre ‘shells’ together 
with a shared technical building.  The size and configuration of the proposed data centre parcels are 
based on an optimised ‘kit -of-parts’ design, developed to enhance construction and operational 
efficiency and to maximise spatial utilisation for data generation and storage within each shell.

4.7 Built Form  
The proposal comprises six (6)  shells spread across six (6) purpose-built buildings with associated 
external plant and equipment, security fencing, internal access roads, carparking, infrastructure and 
landscaping. The proposed development is organised into an east and west campus containing sub-
parcels A -F, with sub -parcel A positioned at the western extent of the site and sub -parcel E at the 
eastern extent. Road 1, running north to south through the site, delineates the boundary between the 
east and west campus areas. 
 
Each of the six (6) buildings comprises four storeys with a maximum building height of 40 metres from 
proposed ground level, inclusive of mechanical roof plant and lift overruns. The internal configuration 
provides front of house administration and office areas at ground floor level, with data halls, plant and 
services areas distributed across all floors. Generators are strategically positioned at the corners of each 
building, accompanied by steel flues for ventilation and exhaust purposes.

4.7.1 Building Height  
The site is subject to a maximum (non-statutory) height of 20m in accordance with the MRP DCP 2021 
which is sought to be varied given that the proposed development is seeking a maximum building 
height of 40 metres from the proposed pad level  (53.5m from the lowest point of the sites existing 
ground level). This exceedance is due to the site's natural landform , the operational needs to provide 
slender buildings that optimise energy usage and cooling and the opportunity to increase setbacks to 
the northern boundary to minimise interface impacts with the schools and aged care facility.  
 
The maximum finished roof level (RL) of Building E in the eastern portion of the site is RL 107.5 metres, 
whilst Sub-parcel A in the western portion achieves a maximum level of RL 93.5 metres. This 14 metre 
variation reflects the site’s existing gradient a nd allows the built form to respond sympathetically to 
topography, minimising unnecessary earthworks and retaining a stepped profile when viewed 
externally, thereby reducing the visual impact of the proposed development through integration with 
the landscape and surrounding topography.

▪ South - 25m to 99m

a visual perception of separation and ensure meaningful landscape zones can be provided  to screen 
the bulk and scale of the proposed development. Within the setbacks to these frontages, there will be 
various forms of incidental related development such as rods, electrical infrastructure and some plant 
and equipment.

4.8 Visual and Acoustic Screening  
Consideration has been given to how the proposed development will appear to visual receivers in both 
the public and private domains, especially during the early establishment phase of landscape planting. 
Each building has been designed to present a distinct yet cohesive architectural expression, 
responding to the surrounding landscape through the use of natural tones, muted colours, and low -
reflectivity materials. Façade modulation, horizontal and vertical articulation, and varied cladding 
textures are employed to visua lly break down the apparent bulk and mass of the structures. These 
design treatments aim to reduce perceived scale, improve integration with the evolving industrial -
technological character of the Mamre Road Precinct, and maintain visual harmony with future  
adjoining developments. 
 
The design team recognised early that building form, façade treatment and material selection would 
be key considerations in addressing potential visual impacts on surrounding receivers. Façades facing 
north and west, which are most visible from the public domain and nearby educational facilities, 
incorporate articulation and variation in tone and texture to provide visual interest and reduce 
perceived bulk. Combined with the site’s generous setbacks and landscape buffers, these treatments 
will contribute to  a visually balanced, contemporary industrial form that sits comfortably within the 
wider Mamre Road Precinct setting. 
 
A 7m high screen is proposed along northern and western boundaries shown in the below. The screen 
is to be constructed of perforated metal , allowing partial transparency and will allow for reduction of 
visual glare and wind load. The design of the screen allows for partial air flow and also reduces the visual 
exposure of buildings and ancillary structures and infrastructure.  
 
 
Figure 50. Proposed Photomontage Inclusive of Screening 
Source: Greenbox Architecture, 2025

4.8.1 Materials and Finishes  
The architectural language of the facility emerges from a dialogue between durability and contextual 
harmony. Neutral grey metal cladding wraps the structure like a protective shell, grounding it against 
the industrial backdrop while reflecting the muted tones of the surrounding landscape. Ribbed precast 
concrete panels, left in their raw finish, introduce a tactile honesty that speaks to permanence and 
resilience, anchoring the building to its site. Vertical and horizontal aluminium modular blades 
articulate the façades, creating rhythmic shadows that shift with the sun, a subtle nod to the dynamic 
nature of the environment.  
 

[[page 77]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  71  
Perforated aluminium cladding punctuates key elevations, allowing light and air to permeate while 
softening the building’s mass. Grey -tinted glazing offers transparency without glare, balancing 
openness with privacy. Around the perimeter, galvanized steel anti-climb mesh and a 2.4 -meter-high 
fence ensure security without compromising visual continuity.  
 
Stainless steel flues rise like sculptural elements, their polished surfaces catching the sky, while metal 
blades in parcel -matched hues weave a sense of cohesion across the site. Together, these materials 
narrate a story of strength and adaptability —an ar chitecture that respects its industrial setting yet 
aspires to refinement through detail and texture.

4.8.2 Signage  
External wayfinding plays a vital role in guiding visitors and staff across large campuses efficiently and 
safely. For this project, signage will remain neutral, focusing on clarity and functionality. Each campus 
will be identified with simple markers such  as “Campus A” and “Campus B” at main entry points. Key 
areas like visitor parking, delivery zones, and security checkpoints will feature clear directional signs 
with universal icons, ensuring visibility for both vehicles and pedestrians. 

[[page 81]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  75  
 
 
Figure 60. Wayfinding Strategy  
Source: Greenbox Architecture, 2025 
 
Traffic flow will be managed through reflective vehicle signage indicating internal roads, loading docks, 
and speed limits, while pedestrian pathways will be marked with arrows and zone indicators. Safety 
reminders will be placed at intersections to reduce  risks. To enhance navigation, buildings will be 
distinguished using a color -coded system applied consistently across façades, external signs, and 
campus maps. 
• Campus Identification: 
o Neutral markers (e.g., “Campus A” / “Campus B”) at entrances 
o Directional signs for visitor parking, delivery zones, and security checkpoints 
• Traffic Flow: 
o Vehicle directional signs (arrows, speed limits, loading dock directions) 
o Pedestrian pathways clearly marked 
• Building Identification: 
o External facade colour finishes differentiate each building and help campus users to 
orient themselves within the site 
The application of distinct external façade colour finishes across individual buildings serves a dual 
purpose: it visually differentiates structures and enhances wayfinding for campus users. This approach 
minimizes reliance on additional signage by embeddi ng orientation cues within the architectural 
language itself. At this stage of development, the façade design strategy is intended to establish a 
coherent and legible narrative for external identification, ensuring clarity and consistency throughout 
the site. 

[[page 82]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  76  
4.8.3 Landscape Design  
External wayfinding plays a vital role in guiding visitors and staff across large campuses efficiently and 
safely. For this project, signage will remain neutral, focusing on clarity and functionality. Each campus 
will be identified with simple markers such  as “Campus A” and “Campus B” at main entry points. Key 
areas like visitor parking, delivery zones, and security checkpoints will feature clear directional signs 
with universal icons, ensuring visibility for both vehicles and pedestrians. 
 
The proposed landscaping for  Mamre Road Data Centre Campus creates a cohesive, resilient, and 
locally responsive environment that integrates ecological planting with functional and security 
requirements across the proposed development. Overall, the design provides 77,637m 2 (17.8%) of 
permeable area on lot and achieves a projected canopy cover of 45,668m 2 (10.4%), supporting both 
environmental performance and visual amenity. 
 
The landscape treatment features tiered sandstone retaining walls complemented by carefully 
selected planting. Cascading greenery will drape over the walls to soften their appearance while 
allowing the natural sandstone to remain visible through strategic gaps.  
 
On the first tier, tall Eucalyptus trees are proposed to provide visual screening without impacting the 
secure zones adjacent to the security fence. To meet bushfire requirements, tree planting will occur in 
grouped formations with adequate separation, and  no large shrubs will be planted beneath tree 
canopies. 
 
In compliance with security requirements, no trees will overhang the 3 -metre offset on either side of 
the anti-climb fence. Despite these restrictions, canopy trees are still proposed along the major north, 
east, and west boundaries to provide effective visual screening —particularly towards the sensitive 
receivers to the north. 
 
The planting palette focuses on species characteristic of the Cumberland Plain Woodland plant 
community, which is indigenous to the area. Species have been selected in accordance with the Mamre 
Road Development Control Plan appendix. This approach supports  local biodiversity and creates 
habitat for non -human kin, aligning with the proposed development’s intent as a non -public access 
development. 
An outdoor amenity area is also proposed adjacent to the COSH building, providing a pleasant space 
for future workers. Planting in this area will include ornamental species to enhance visual appeal and 
assist with wayfinding throughout the site. 
 
Street trees are proposed in line with the Mamre Road Penrith Council’s Street Tree Masterplan, 
creating a leafy outlook to the public domain. Trunk drainage corridors will be enhanced with native 
grasses and trees , consistent with the Aerotropolis Regional Stormwater Infrastructure Guideline, 
contributing to a cohesive and ecologically responsive landscape character. 
 
Landscaping plans propose a mixture of large and medium evergreen indigenous and native trees, 
shrubs, and groundcovers consistent with the Mamre Road DCP 2021. Specifically, the proposal 
includes: 
 
▪ A landscape setback to Mamre Road and Bakers Lane with a typical width of 10m. This 
comprises planting of new native trees capable of growing to between 20-25m in height.  
▪ Along to the eastern and southern boundaries, it is proposed to provide landscaping to 
interface with the future and planned industrial estates. 
▪ The area surrounding the proposed switching station and STATCOM in the north -west corner 
of the site is afforded a higher density of landscaping with substantial landscaped setback 
zones of 35m provided. In combination with the setbacks provided, this area equates to 
77,637m2 of landscaping that serves to provide a visual buffer and enhance the natural setting 
of the site. 
[[page 83]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  77  
▪ It is proposed to provide landscaping that varies in height at maturity, ranging from  0.3m to 
25m.  Species will typically comprise natives that are adaptive to the climatic conditions of the 
locality.  
 
The resulting landscape characteristics of the proposed development include:  
 
▪ A landscaped area of 77,637m2, representing 17.8% site coverage and comprising: 
o Landscaped area of 45,597m2 for the West Campus 
o Landscaped area of 32,040m2 for the East Campus 
▪ A total of 846 new trees to be planted; and 
▪ A total tree canopy cover of 45,668m2, representing 10.4% site coverage and comprising: 
o Canopy cover of 27,233m2 for the West Campus; 
o Canopy cover of 18,382m2 for the East Campus 
 
The Landscape Plans are attached as Appendix 11  Landscape Plan.

Source: Geoscapes, 2025 
4.9 Site Preparation and Establishment Works  
Site preparation activities are proposed to facilitate the proposed development. This includes 
demolition, tree removal, and earthworks.  
4.9.1 Demolition and Tree Removal  
The ultimate development involves the demolition of all existing buildings and structures, including 
existing hardstand and infrastructure.  
 
A total of 215 trees and groups of trees within and adjoining the site boundaries be removed due to tree 
locations being in direct conflict with the proposed bulk earthworks and roadways 
 
The removal requirements result from the following impacts: 
 

[[page 84]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  78  
▪ 156 trees and groups of trees fall directly within the footprint of the proposed bulk earthworks; 
▪ 56 trees and groups of trees fall directly within the footprint of the proposed roadways, 
including the future Southern Link Road, Road No. 1 and Mamre Road widening works; and 
▪ Three (3) neighbouring trees.  
4.9.2 Dam De-watering  
As part of the site preparation works, the six (6) farm dams on the site will be required to be dewatered, 
in accordance with a Dam Decommissioning Plan.  The Dam Decommissioning Plan is to provide 
guidelines for the dewatering of dams and the translocation of aquatic fauna species. This plan will 
need to be in place prior to the commencement of the decommissioning works. 
4.9.3 Remediation 
The remediation strategy follows NSW EPA guidelines and industry best practice. Contaminated soil 
containing asbestos will be excavated and disposed at licensed facilities that can legally accept such 
materials. This includes approximately 5 cubic metres o f soil with loose asbestos fibres and 
approximately 1,150 cubic metres of soil containing asbestos fragments. Petroleum-stained soils will be 
excavated and relocated to areas beneath future buildings or paved areas where they will not pose any 
risk or aest hetic concern. Waste materials unsuitable for retention will be removed and disposed 
appropriately, while inert materials such as bricks and concrete may be retained in suitable locations. 
Building rubble around dam embankments will be manually inspected a nd any asbestos materials 
removed before the rubble can be reused or disposed. 
 
Following remediation, the site will undergo comprehensive validation to confirm all contamination 
has been adequately addressed, including soil sampling, laboratory testing, visual inspection, air 
monitoring, and documentation of waste disposal. Subsequen tly, the site will be suitable for the 
proposed data centre without requiring any ongoing management of contamination. 
4.9.4 Earthworks  
Bulk earthworks are required to provide six (6) large flat building pads  to facilitate the  proposed 
development of data centre parcels, in conjunction with associated  hardstand areas, and carparking 
areas around the site.  
 
The cut and fill requirements within the site have been defined through multiple design iterations and 
careful consideration of various factors within and surrounding the  proposed development site , 
including but not limited to: 
 
▪ Undulating topography within the Mamre Road Precinct; 
▪ Provision for connectivity to and interfaces with adjoining lands; 
▪ Achieving as close as possible to a balance of cut to fill to minimise the volume of material that 
needs to be imported or exported, allowing for management of topsoil and over -excavation 
(desilting) required within existing farm dams; 
▪ Minimising the height and extent of retaining walls fronting  the public domain including the 
future Southern Link Road, Mamre Road and internal estate roads; 
▪ Avoiding extensive cut in bedrock sub-surface units; 
▪ Meeting the requirements for the end-use of the site as a data centre campus; and 
▪ Provision of a final design solution that addresses water management requirements.  
 
A detailed earthworks plan has been prepared and is provided within the Civil Engineering Report at  
Appendix 21  Infrastructure Delivery Strategy Report and Civil Infrastructure Report.  
 
[[page 85]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  79  
 
Figure 62. Bulk Earthworks Plan 
Source: AT&L, 2025

4.9.5 Retaining Walls 
The proposal incorporates retaining walls where level changes cannot be accommodated through 
batter slopes. Retaining walls shall be constructed adjacent to the road reserve and between data 
centre parcels within the site boundaries. All retaining walls sh all utilise keystone block products or 
similar systems as detailed in the civil engineering drawings (Appendix 21  Infrastructure 
Delivery Strategy Report and Civil Infrastructure Report). 
 
In accordance with Section 4.4 of the MRP DCP 2021, retaining walls exceeding 2 metres in height shall 
be constructed as tiered structures. All retaining walls shall be designed and constructed in accordance 
with standard industry practices, relevant Australian Standards and applicable engineering guidelines. 
 
Retaining walls shall be constructed on a staged basis corresponding with the proposed development 
earthworks programme. Where retaining walls are not immediately constructed, temporary batters at 
a gradient of 1:4 shall be maintained for stability purposes. Any batter exceeding a gradient of 1:5 shall 
be vegetated to prevent erosion and ensure stability.
