4.10 Stormwater Management  
The proposed stormwater drainage infrastructure for the data centre campus has been designed to 
manage and convey stormwater flows in accordance with relevant engineering standards prior to 
controlled discharge to receiving waters north of Bakers Lane and west of Mamre Road. The drainage 
system incorporates the following design criteria: 
 
Estate road drainage infrastructure: 
 
▪ Major drainage system (comprising pit and pipe networks, overland flow paths and channels): 
designed to accommodate the 1% Annual Exceedance Probability (AEP) storm event 
▪ Minor drainage system (pit and pipe network): designed to accommodate the 5% AEP storm 
event as a minimum standard, with increased capacity where necessary to satisfy major system 
requirements 
 
Data centre campus drainage infrastructure: 
 
▪ Major and minor drainage systems: designed to capture and convey stormwater flows up to 
and including the 1% AEP design storm event, ensuring operational continuity of the Data 
Centre Campus whilst mitigating potential flood risk within the site 
 
Further detail is provided in Section 7.9 of this EIS and Appendix 37  Water and Stormwater 
Management Plan. 
4.10.1 Erosion and Sediment Control  
A comprehensive Erosion and Sediment Control Plan (Appendix 41) has been prepared in accordance 
with relevant guidelines to manage potential impacts during construction activities. 
 
The proposed erosion and sediment control measures are detailed within the Civil Engineering 
Drawings (Appendix 39  Earthworks Strategy Report ), which provide comprehensive layouts and 
technical specifications for all control infrastructure to be implemented across the subject site through 
to completion of construction works. 
 
The following methodology and control measures will be implemented to mitigate potential 
sedimentation impacts during the construction phase: 
 
▪ Minimisation of the spatial extent and temporal duration of soil disturbance activities 
▪ Diversion of clean water runoff from undisturbed catchments around disturbed areas, with 
discharge via appropriate scour protection measures 

[[page 87]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  81  
▪ Installation of flow diversion structures to intercept surface flows and redirect clean water via 
designated drainage pathways 
▪ Direction of sediment-laden runoff to appropriately sized temporary sediment basins designed 
to accommodate design storm volumes, with provision for chemical flocculation where 
required 
▪ Implementation of stabilised construction access points incorporating vehicle shaker grids and 
wash-down facilities to prevent sediment tracking onto the adjacent road network 
▪ Construction of catch drains to convey sediment-laden water to designated sediment control 
structures 
▪ Installation of sediment fencing to provide source control through filtration and retention of 
sediments 
▪ Progressive stabilisation of disturbed surfaces through hydroseeding or equivalent measures 
in areas not subject to immediate construction activities 
 
All temporary sediment control basins will be positioned outside the 1% Annual Exceedance 
Probability (AEP) flood extent from both local overland flow paths and South Creek flooding." 
4.11 Road Design and Parking  
4.11.1 External Road Network 
The site is bounded to the north by Bakers Lane, a local road under the ownership and maintenance of 
Penrith City Council, comprising a 6-8 metre sealed pavement with table drains within a 20-metre road 
reserve. 
 
Notwithstanding the site's direct frontage to Bakers Lane along the entirety of its northern boundary, 
only emergency vehicular access from Bakers Lane is proposed as part of the proposal. The proposed 
development does not propose any external road upgrades or modifications to the existing road 
network.  
 
The future SLR is planned to run parallel to Bakers Lane and will ultimately provide the primary access 
point to the site. The Southern Link Road is intended to function as an east -west arterial connection 
between Mamre Road and Wallgrove Road. However, funding for the delivery of this infrastructure has 
not been committed by TfNSW at the time of this EIS.  
 
The primary point of vehicular access to the site will be via the north -south collector road that is 
currently under construction as part of the Yiribana Logistics Estate development  (SSD-10272349). No 
vehicular access to the site off Mamre Road is proposed once operations have commenced. 
4.11.2 Internal Road Network  
The internal road network has been designed to accommodate the operational requirements of the 
proposal and the broader Mamre Road Precinct. The network anticipates heavy vehicle movements 
including semi -trailers (up to 19m) and A -Doubles (up to 30m), whic h require specific design 
considerations for lane widths and turning movements 
 
The internal network comprises two road typologies in accordance with the Mamre Road Precinct 
Development Control Plan: 
 
▪ Collector Road (Road No. 1): 25.6m road reserve with 15.4m carriageway width 
▪ Industrial Road (Road No. 2): 24.0m road reserve with 15.0m carriageway width 
 
Both road types are designed for 60km/h speeds (signposted 50km/h) and accommodate PBS Level 2 
Type B vehicles as the design vehicle, with PBS Level 3 Type A vehicles as the check vehicle. Each road 
provides two 3.5m through traffic lanes and kerbside lanes of 4.2m and 4.0m respectively.

4.11.3 On-Site Parking 
Car-parking areas are located at -grade and directly adjacent to the buildings, meaning adequate 
parking is provided for each stage of the proposed development.  
 
A total of 619 car parks is provided, including 124 Electric Vehicle (EV spaces). Six motorcycle parks are 
also proposed as part of the  proposed development. All staff and customer/contractor car spaces 
would be designed to meet the minimum requirements of User Class 1/1A car parking (low turnover) to 
align with AS2890.1:2004 for staff parking and AS2890.6:2009 for accessible parking. 
 
Parking spaces for the COSH building have been designed to user Class 3 standards within the campus 
(suitable for medium/high turnover) with publicly accessible spaces (for the café) provided in an angled 
configuration that is well suited to high turnover traffic. The internal manoeuvring area to and from the 
internal car park areas have been designed for light vehicles up to B99 with dimensions 
1,940mm×5,200mm, as prescribed within AS2890.1: 2004.

4.11.5 Service and Loading  
A loading dock comprising 1 loading bay (suitable for an HRV) for each sub-parcel (6 total) is considered 
sufficient and appropriate. Furthermore, the COSH building incorporating a café has been provided 
with a dedicated loading space suitable to accommodate  an HRV which will provide a high level of 
servicing amenity. It is anticipated that service area design compliance with AS 2890.2:2018 would form 
a standard Condition of Consent further to approval. 
4.12 Utilities  
The proposed development will include the provision of the following required utilities: 
Disel Fuel Tanks 
The generator engines on site will use diesel fuel to generate power. All 2.2 MW generators enclosures 
will be located within the gantry  areas. Each generator enclosure contains a fuel day tank. These 
generator day tanks will be supplied with fuel from bulk fuel tanks located in tank chambers on the 
ground floor. The fuel system in each shell provides sufficient fuel to run the generators for 26  hours. 
Fuel tanks will be designed to comply with AS 1692 and AS 1940 requirements.

Industrial Water 
The industrial water site infrastructure comprises with dual connections from recycled water mains 
from 2 different recycled water utilities with a set of water storage tanks per building which holds 24 
hours of peak mechanical demand. Separate A and B mec hanical makeup water system will be 
provided from each set of tanks for a N+N redundancy arrangement.  Each A and B system will have a 
separate pump set, which each pump set can boost 100% of mechanical demand. 
The volume of industrial water storage tanks is designed to have sufficient capacity to provide flow to 
N+R cooling plant for the worst-case 24-hour sequence of ambient conditions measured at the nearest 
ASHRAE weather station based on 20 years of recent historical data. The total tank volume also 
includes 10% of the total tank effective c apacity allowing buffer zone for rainwater reuse and 
wastewater reuse top up.

own 33kV reticulation system to supply the shells with electricity.  
4.13 Back-up Power System  
The proposed development has opted for diesel -generator sets as the backup power source for the 
proposed site. This is based on the most appropriate technology for a mission critical data centre and 
the market readiness for this technology in New South Wales. Alternatives have been considered for 
this site, but none are considered viable except for alternative fuel for the generator engines. 
 
The proposed site will use approximately 846 low voltage 2MW generators for sub-parcels A-F. These 
generators will only operate in the event of a mains utility failure to the respective electrical block or 
for testing purposes. These generators provide a total capacity of 1861.2MW backup power. This capacity 
exceeds the total consumption of the site because of redundant power strings that provide resiliency 
to the operation of the facility. The quantity of generators is outlined in the Plant and Equipment 
Section of this report. 
 
The following table details generator distribution across the site: 
 
TABLE 14: GENERATOR QUANTITIES BY SUB-PARCEL AND LEVEL

Level 3 29 29 29 29 36 36 
Level 2 29 29 29 29 36 36 
Level 1 24 24 24 24 30 30 
Ground 24 24 24 24 30 30 
Total 130 130 130 130 162 162 
 
Total site capacity: 846 generators providing 1,861.2MW backup power (including redundancy). 
4.13.1 Maintenance Testing 
To ensure operational readiness for unexpected mains power interruptions, generators will undergo 
regular maintenance testing. Quarterly testing occurs with generators connected to mobile load banks 
at each sub -parcel. Only one generator per sub -parcel is tested at any time during daytime hours 
between 7:00am and 6:00pm. Testing procedures will not exceed the 200 -hour exemption lim it 
prescribed under Schedule 1, Clause 17 of the POEO Act.

5.2 Commonwealth Planning Context 
Under the Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act 1999  (EPBC Act), any action 
(which includes a development, project, or activity) that is considered likely to have a significant impact 
on Matters of National Environmental Significance (MNES) (including nationally threatened ecological 
communities and species and listed migratory species), must be referred to the Commonw ealth 
Minister for the Environment. The purpose of the referral is to allow a decision to be made about 
whether an action requires approval on a Commonwealth level. If an action is considered likely to have 
a significant impact on MNES, it is declared a ‘Controlled Action’ for which formal Commonwealth 
approval is required.  Currently, bilateral agreements allow the Commonwealth  Minister for the 
Environment to rely on the NSW environmental assessment process when a ssessing a Controlled 
Action under the EPBC Act.  
 
[[page 96]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  90  
As outlined in the Biodiversity Development Assessment Report (Appendix 15  Biodiversity 
Development Assessment Report), the proposal is not likely to impact an ecological MNES.  
 
Two threatened fauna species listed under the EPBC Act were assessed due to the presence of some 
suitable habitat within the site. Significant Impact Criteria (SIC) assessments as part of the Biodiversity 
Development Assessment Report have been prepared for both species as follows:  
 
▪ Swift Parrot 
▪ Australian Painted Snipe 
 
Detailed assessment of the impacts to these species is undertaken in Section 7.16. It is noted that there 
is a low likelihood of impact to Swift Parrot  and the Australian Painted Snipe due to the degraded 
nature of the site, limited suitable habitat and a small area of habitat removal of 1.35 hectares of native 
vegetation. It has therefore been determined that a Commonwealth referral is not required for impacts 
to these species.  
 
It is acknowledged that 0.63 hectares of the site is consistent with a critically endangered ecological 
community (CEEC) listed under the EPBC Act , identified as the Cumberland Plains Shale Woodlands 
CEEC, will be impacted by the proposal. Notwithstanding, there is a low likelihood of significant impact 
as the CEEC occurs in a highly fragmented and isolated patch, subject to historical disturbance and 
current weed ingress.  
 
Migratory species are unlikely to occur at the site, given its location within an industrial setting and a  
lack of suitable habitat features. No other threatened species are likely to be impacted by the proposed 
development. 
 
There are no National Heritage Places within the subject site. There are no wetlands of international 
importance within or in close proximity to the subject site.  
  
Given the above, the proposal will not result in any significant impacts having regard to the EPBC Act.  
Therefore, a referral to the Commonwealth is not considered necessary.

Declaration of SSD Development consent is sought under Division 4.7 - State Significant Development’ of the Environmental Planning and Assessment

A State environmental planning policy may declare any development, or any class or description of development, to be State 
significant development. 
 
Schedule 1 of Planning Systems SEPP lists development that is declared SSD. Schedule 1, Clause 25, states the following:  
 
25   Data Centres 
Development for the purposes of data centres that has a total power consumption of more than the relevant amount. 
(1A)  Subsection (1) does not apply to development on land within the area of City of Sydney 
In this section –  
Relevant amount means –  
(a)  for development in relation to which the relevant environmental assessment requirements are notified under the 
Act on or before 31 May 2023—10 megawatts, or 
(b)    for any other development—15 megawatts 
 
The proposed development is anticipated to have a total power consumption of up to 1.2 GW. Accordingly, the proposal is classified as 
SSD. 
Consent Authority Section 4.5 of the EP&A Act and Section 2.7 of the Planning Systems SEPP stipulate that the consent authority is the Minister  for 
Planning (or the Department as their delegate) unless the proposed development triggers one of the matters set out in Section 2.7(1) 
in which case the consent authority will be with the Independent Planning Commission IPC). The Proponent has not made a reportable 
political donation in connection with the proposed development application.  
 
In accordance with Schedule 1 of the Planning Systems SEPP, development for data centres with a total power consumption of mo re 
than 15 megawatts constitutes SSD. As noted above, the total power consumption of the proposed development is in excess of 15 
megawatts. The power to grant approval lies with the Minister for Planning (NSW DPHI) as the consent authority for SSD, pursu ant to 
Section 4.5 of the EP&A Act.

Permissibility  The site is located within the IN1 General Industrial zone pursuant to the Industry and Employment SEPP. The proposed development 
constitutes a ‘data centre’ which is defined as following:  
 
data centre means a building or place the principal purpose of which is to collect, distribute, process or store electronic data 
using information technology. 
 
Data centres are a type of ‘high technology industry’ which in turn, is considered a type of ‘light industry’ which sits unde r the group 
term of ‘industry’. Industries (other than offensive or hazardous industries) are permitted with consent in the IN1 General Industrial zone 
pursuant to the Industry and Employment SEPP and Section 2.31 of the Transport and Infrastructure SEPP.  
 
The proposal is consistent with the zone objectives as it provides for a typology of industry that will create employment opp ortunities 
and will encourage a high standard of development that does not prejudice the sustainability of other enterprises or the environment.

5.3.3 Mandatory Matters for Consideration 
TABLE 18 identifies the matters that the consent authority must consider in deciding to grant consent to the proposal. It also identifies the section(s) of this EIS 
and the appended documents that address these mandatory matters. 
 
TABLE 17: PRE-CONDITIONS OVERVIEW 
Statutory Reference Pre-condition  Assessment  Addressed in EIS 
Pursuant to Clause 3.2(1) of the Sustainable Buildings SEPP, in 
deciding whether to grant development consent, the consent 
authority must consider whether the development is designed to 
enable the following:  
(a) the minimisation of waste from associated demolition 
and construction, including by the choice and reuse of 
building materials,  
(b) a reduction in peak demand for electricity, including 
through the use of energy efficient technology,  
(c) a reduction in the reliance on artificial lighting and 
mechanical heating and cooling through passive 
design,  
(d) the generation and storage of renewable energy,  
(e) the metering and monitoring of energy consumption,  
(f) the minimisation of the consumption of potable water. 
 
Pursuant to Clause 3.2(2) of the Sustainable Buildings SEPP, 
development consent must not be granted to non -residential 
development unless the consent authority is satisfied the 
embodied emissions attributable to the development have been 
quantified.

design) and material resources.

Chapter 3 – 
Advertising and 
Signage 
Schedule 5 of the Industry and Employment SEPP includes signage provisions that are required to 
be considered as part of applications that propose to include signage. The proposed signage as 
outlined in Section 3.8 includes Estate Signage and external faça de signage zones. Pursuant to 
Clause 3.6 of the Industry and Employment SEPP:  
 
A consent authority must not grant development consent to an application to display signage unless 
the consent authority is satisfied–  
(a) that the signage is consistent with the objectives of this Chapter as set out in section 3.1(1)(a), 
and  
(b) that the signage the subject of the application satisfies the assessment criteria specified in 
Schedule 5.

C. It is noted that content within the proposed signage zones would be subject to future tenant 
requirements. The proposed signage is also consistent with the requirements of the MRP DCP 2021, 
as demonstrated in Appendix 33  Mamre Road Precinct Development Control Plan  2021 
Assessment.

thresholds established under the Department of Planning’s guideline, ‘Applying SEPP 33’.

Appendix 33  Mamre Road

Cumberland Plain Conservation Plan 
Cumberland Plain 
Conservation Plan 
The CPCP is part of the NSW Government’s commitment to delivering the Western Parkland City, 
consistent with the Greater Sydney Commission’s strategic vision described in its Greater Sydney 
Region Plan: A Metropolis of Three Cities and Western City District Plan. This includes providing the 
biodiversity approvals for development in Western Sydney’s four (4) nominated areas and four (4) 
major transport corridors. The CPCP has been developed to meet requirements for strategic 
biodiversity certification under the BC Act and strategic assessment under the EPBC Act 
 
The majority of the site has been identified as urban capable  under the CPCP. As such, the majority 
of the site does not require further biodiversity assessment with the BDAR only applying to the areas 
of ‘excluded land’ within the site.

This section is a summary of the preliminary community and stakeholder engagement undertaken to 
inform the preparation of the Environmental Impact Statement (EIS). The engagement process was 
undertaken in accordance with the Secretary's Environmental Assessment Requirements (SEARs) and 
the Department’s Undertaking Engagement Guidelines for State Significant Projects (DPHI, 2024). 
Further details are provided in the Community and Stakeholder Engagement Report prepared by 
Willowtree Communications, located in Appendix 32.

6.2 Summary Of Community and Stakeholder Engagement Program 
The engagement approach utilised a range of tools and methods designed to ensure accessibility, 
provide multiple opportunities for participation, and accommodate the diverse stakeholder groups 
identified for the proposed development . The selection of engagement tools was informed by the 
stakeholder groups identified, the IAP2 levels of engagement, and the Undertaking Engagement 
Guidelines for State Significant Projects. 
 
TABLE 20: ENGAGEMENT TOOLS 
Engagement Tool Description 
DPHI Request for 
SEARs  
DPHI issued a request for agency and stakeholder input to inform the 
Secretary's Environmental Assessment Requirements (SEARs) for the 
proposed development. 
Response to SEARs Government agencies and stakeholders provided submissions identifying 
matters to be addressed in the Environmental Impact Statement. 
Planning Focus 
Meeting 
Interagency, authority and Council meeting to discuss SEARs responses 
and detailed approach to key issues raised 
Individual Meetings One-on-one meetings with key stakeholders including nearby sensitive 
receivers (schools, childcare centre, aged care facility), government 
agencies, and utility providers to discuss the proposal and address specific 
concerns. 
Community 
Newsletter 
A project newsletter distributed to 524 stakeholders, including nearby 
residents, businesses and sensitive receivers as well as various 
organisations, providing an overview of the proposal, engagement 
opportunities, and key project information. Council and several agencies 
were also provided with a copy of the Newsletter as a courtesy. 
 
The distribution area was determined based on proximity to the site, 
potential to experience impacts from the proposal, including areas likely 
to experience construction and operation-related impacts. In this context, 
the area was enlarged to include residential areas to the west and south 
of the precinct, following feedback from DPHI during the Planning Focus 
Meeting. 
Direct 
Correspondence 
Direct letters and emails to nearby property owners, sensitive receivers, 
and key stakeholders providing project information and inviting feedback. 
Community Survey An online survey enabling stakeholders to provide feedback on the 
proposal and raise any concerns or issues for consideration. 
Online Information 
Session 
Virtual briefing sessions providing an overview of the proposal, technical 
information, and opportunities for questions and feedback. 
Direct Phone Line A dedicated phone number established for community members and 
stakeholders to contact the proposed development team with enquiries. 
Project Email A dedicated email address for written enquiries, feedback and ongoing 
correspondence throughout the engagement period.

The following engagement activities were undertaken to ensure comprehensive consultation across all 
identified groups, with outcomes documented to demonstrate how community and stakeholder 
feedback influenced the proposed development design. For further details, refer to the Community 
and Stakeholder Engagement Report.

6.7 Community Engagement – Local Resident Feedback   
A local resident from Aldington Road provided feedback on 11 November supporting the proposed 
development, recognising its potential to create jobs, boost the local economy, and ensure Australian 
digital infrastructure is world -leading. The resident has worked in the digital sector and respects its 
importance. 
 
The primary concern raised related to the preservation of a very large, old Eucalyptus tree (likely E. 
Amplifolia or E. Tereticornis, both indigenous to the area) located at the back of the site near the 
proposed "Tech SYD 6.5" building location. The tree provides critical habitat for native birds through 
hollow branches and represents both nostalgic and ecological value. The resident noted that many 
ancient trees have been removed with recent industrial development, resulting in habitat loss for local 
fauna, and requested consideration of the tree's retention if it is in healthy condition. 
 
The proposed development team acknowledged the feedback and advised that the proposed plans 
would be reviewed to understand how they interact with the specific tree.  
 
The resident followed up, emphasising the tree's significance and providing more specific location 
details (top of the hill at the far back right -hand side of the property when facing Mamre Road). They 
expressed that retention within an allocated small gre en space would be welcome news for local 
residents and offered to take a phone call to provide further location specifics.

6.10 Further/ Future Engagement   
Pre-lodgement engagement informed an approach focused on transparent, ongoing communication 
with sensitive receivers (such as schools and aged care facilities) and clear processes for raising and 
resolving concerns throughout all project phases. 
• Assessment and Determination Phase: The proponent will maintain communication with key 
facilities, notify stakeholders of the public exhibition period, share information, respond to 
feedback, and continue consultation with Penrith City Council and government agencies. 
• Construction Phase:  Engagement will include pre -construction meetings with sensitive 
receivers, a dedicated community liaison role, advance notification of disruptive activities, 
regular construction updates, and clear complaint-handling procedures. Coordination with the 
Council and agencies will address traffic and environmental impacts, detailed within the 
Construction Environmental Management Plan. 
• Operational Phase:  The Proponent will notify stakeholders of project completion, retain 
communication channels for operational queries, respond promptly to concerns, and continue 
liaising with Council and agencies.

demand and other regulatory approvals.

7.1.9 Back-Up Power 
Proposed Back-Up Power 
 
[[page 122]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  116  
The proposal is to be supplied by 330/132kV and 132kV/33kV infrastructure. The power supply will be 
distributed within the site via the proposed underground electrical infrastructure and a substation 
serving each sub-parcel. The proposal comprises a STATCOM (Static Compensator) facility as part of 
the main substation that interfaces with the grid. This has been requested by Lumea as a load 
smoothing measure to improve the power quality impacts of the site. Ongoing development of power 
quality improvements are expected as part of the ongoing design of the HV system.  
 
The Data Centre Campus is a mission critical facility and therefore requires back up generation if there 
is interruption to the utility supply. Each building will be provided with diesel  generators and 
uninterrupted power supply (UPS) systems. The UPS system is designed to maintain supply between 
loss of utility and changeover to the generator supply , and it is not intended to maintain the full load 
on site for an extended period. For the purposes of the Plant and Equipment Systems Report, only the 
diesel generators are considered as back-up power noting that the UPS and battery system are for 
energy storage and short-term supply only. 
 
Although the energy transmission system in the area is designed to be highly reliable, there is always a 
potential (although extremely unlikely) risk of interruption to utility supplies. Any utility outage without 
backup power generation on site would impact critical sectors of the economy and community which 
rely upon cloud computing, including banking, business operations and communication. The f acility 
will play a key role in providing stable cloud infrastructure to the region. 
 
The proposal demonstrates commitment to continual improvement by establishing that each 
development stage will provide opportunities to adopt new technologies, specifically exploring 
renewable fuel sources for diesel engines and modular replacement of gene rator containers as 
alternative energy sources become commercially available in Australia, alongside pursuing power 
purchasing agreements for upstream renewable energy.

Each sub -parcel features a five (5) storey generator gantry supporting containerised generators. All 
generators discharge hot air on the façade side and intake cold air from within the gantry structure, 
with exhaust flue pipes discharging at the effective building height. Ground floor tanks provide bulk

generator engine set (2MW), fuel system with pumps, control and power panels, acoustic attenuation 
for air intake and discharge ventilation, exhaust gas silencer and flue connections, and radiator with a 
fan.

7.1.11 Data Hall Cooling System   
Proposed Data Hall Cooling System 
 
The proposal deploys direct liquid to the chip data hall cooling. Direct to chip technology allows the 
majority of heat from IT equipment to be transferred into a water network rather than into the air. This 
results in a more efficient system with reduced reliance on air, which typically requires more power per 
kilowatt of IT cooling capacity. Additional benefits of direct liquid to chip include the ability to operate 
at higher water temperatures, thereby enhancing system efficiency by reducing the workload on chiller 
compressors and or hybrid dry coolers. As the end customer’s data hall cooling strategy shifts toward a 
higher proportion of direct -to-chip liquid cooling, this in turn further improves the overall system 
efficiency. 
 
The Proponent has expertise across numerous portfolios in various data hall cooling technologies, 
including fully air -cooled systems and liquid cooling technology, with first -hand knowledge of 
operational expenditure associated with different cooling approaches. Direct -to-chip technology is 
currently among the more efficient cooling approaches aligned with end -user requirements and 
current industry trends. 
 
Power and water consumption are influenced by a range of factors, including ambient weather 
conditions, air recirculation, customer-specific design setpoints, and the final distribution between air 
and liquid cooling. Water -based chiller systems typically achieve better efficiency compared with air -
based systems, reflecting the higher efficiency of mechanical rooftop equipment. 
 
Recognising the continued evolving nature of data centre innovation, the mechanical design for the 
data halls has been developed to remain versatile and capable of accommodating or retrofitting future 
emerging, more efficient cooling solutions. 
 
Air Cooled Chillers (ACCs) are the proposed solution due to their ability to provide continuous cooling 
without water use. The configuration of this equipment allows for faster construction and provides the 
client with the option for an alternative design at lower IT density targets. This design ca n also be 
implemented alongside HDCs with water-cooled chillers if water supply is available but insufficient to 
support a full campus HDC design. The number of ACCs is determined by the end customer, with 
quantities expected to range from 24 to 28 units.  Mechanical equipment placement on the roof has 
been considered the impact of heat rejection to not affect habitable rooms and communal open 
spaces. 
 
Alternatives Considered 
 
Hybrid Dry Cooler (HDC)  were considered  however are not proposed at present . The design and 
operation of hybrid dry coolers can reduce water consumption by up to 25% at peak usage compared 
with standard open loop cooling towers of equivalent heat rejection capacity. Furthermore, HDCs can 
eliminate water use entirely under specific ambient conditions. Based on end user cooling water 
temperature requirements, the HDC ca n provide 60%of indirect free cooling on the most humid day, 
with the remaining cooling supplied by highly efficient water-cooled chillers. This approach minimises 
the power consumption of mechanical systems, with typical quantities ranging from 16 to 18 HDCs and 
six ( 6) to eight ( 8) water-cooled chillers depending on the end customer.  Mechanical equipment 
placement on the roof has been considered the impact of heat rejection to not affect habitable rooms 
and communal open spaces.

A balance between water-based and air-cooled compressor systems has been considered to optimise 
utility demands of power and water. These considerations have informed the selection of two rooftop 
system configurations, developed with careful attention to t heir placement, operation and potential 
impacts on neighbouring and public infrastructure. Mechanical rooftop equipment is arranged in 
concentrated zones, maintaining sufficient clearance from nearby intakes, to prevent re -entrainment 
of heat-rejected fluids. Discharge from these systems is generally directed vertically to mitigate wind -
driven recirculation. 
7.2 Noise and Vibrations  
Noise and Vibration Impact Assessment (NVIA) has been prepared by Renzo Tonin & Associates for the 
Proposal and is attached as (Appendix 17). The NVIA has been prepared in accordance with the NSW 
Noise Policy for Industry (NPI), the NSW Interim Construction Noise Guideline (ICNG) (DECC 2009), the 
TfNSW Road Noise Policy (RNP) and other relevant guidelines and policies. The NVIA assesses noise and 
vibration impacts during the construction and operational phases of the proposal. When required, the 
NVIA proposes mitigation and management measures to reduce impacts.   
7.2.1 Existing Environment  
The site is located within the IN1 General Industrial zone within the MRP. Population density analysis 
using 2024 census data shows that the immediate site area has "very low" density (0-500 persons/km²), 
indicating a predominantly industrial area.   
  
Identified Receiver’s  
  
Receiver locations assessed in the NVIA include residential, educational, industrial, places of public 
worship, recreation, and childcare. The nearest sensitive receivers to the site are located to the north 
and include the Mamre Anglican School, Trinity C atholic Primary School, Emmaus Catholic College, 
Emmaus Retirement Village and some rural dwellings on sites transitioning to industrial uses.  
  
The NVIA categorises sensitive receivers based on whether they are inside or outside the MRP rezoned 
area and if they are expected to exist in the long -term or only in the short term (transitioning to 
industrial uses or relocating):  
 
• Receiver catchments outside of the MRP.  
• Receiver catchments inside the MRP (long-term).  
• Receiver catchments inside the MRP (short-term).  
  
A detailed list of receivers is provided in Appendix 17 and depicted in Figure 70.  
  
Receivers outside the MRP   
This receiver group includes permanent existing residences outside the MRP (Figure 70), including in 
the neighbourhoods of   
 
• Luddenham (NCA06) (near Twin Creeks Golf & Country Club) – with the closest residences 
approximately 1,400 metres west of the Proposal.  
• Orchard Hills (NCA05) – with the closest residences approximately 1,500 metres north west 
of the Proposal.  
• Mount Vernon and Horsley Park (NCA08) – with the closest residences approximately 2 
kilometres east and south-east of the Proposal.  
• Kemps Creek (NCA07) – with the closest residences approximately 1,600 metres south of the 
Proposal.

Project construction works are generally expected to be undertaken during the NSW Interim 
Construction Noise Guideline (ICNG) recommended standard construction hours, being:   
 
• Monday to Friday 7 am to 6 pm   
• Saturday 8 am to 1 pm   
• No work on Sundays or public holidays   
  
The proposed development also involves some out-of-hours works (OOHW), including constructing the 
temporary access from Mamre Road and other necessary road works. Due to the nature of roadworks, 
some tasks must be carried out after hours. Generally, roadwo rks will occur after hours if they could 
negatively impact the road network during peak periods, pose safety risks to workers, or if a roads 
authority has mandated that the works be done during nighttime.   
  
Construction Noise Sources  
  
The likely noise emission sources associated with the construction of the proposed development are 
identified and modelled in Table 7-7 of the NVIA. The assessment was based upon noise sources levels 
from a range of sources including sound power levels based on maximum noise levels provided in Table 
A1 of Australian Standard 2436 - 2010 ‘Guide to Noise Control on Construction, Demolition and 
Maintenance Sites’, the ICNG, information sourced from past projects and/or information held in the 
internal library files.  
  
Predicted Construction Noise Levels  
  
The NVIA has modelled the potential construction noise levels at receivers for each construction stage 
of the proposed development, utilising the expected sound power levels of plant and equipment 
operating in proximity to receivers. Table 7-9 of the NVIA presents noise levels likely to be experienced 
at the nearby receiver locations during the construction phase. The noise levels are presented as ranges 
to provide an understanding of noise levels when the plant  items operate at the location furthest (or 
most acoustically shielded) from the receiver and at the location closest (or with the highest potential 
noise level) to the receiver. The upper end of the range shown in Table 7 -9 of the NVIA indicates the 
expected noise level at a receiver when constructio n work is taking place at the closest point on the 
site to the receiver. Although in practical application, noise -producing construction activities are 
carried out at different locations throughout the work area, resulting in varying noise levels at each 
receiver.   
  
The noise predictions presented in this NVIA represent a re conservative and represent the identified 
typical or high noise equipment/ plant operating continuously throughout the 15 minute period. It is  
[[page 135]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  129  
important to distinguish between high noise equipment/plant and typical construction noise scenarios 
within this assessment. High noise equipment/plant scenarios represent a worst -case condition, with 
both worst case equipment and location modelled, which is not expected to reflect the common 
construction noise emissions generated throughout the Proposal construction works for various 
receivers. Predicted noise levels from high noise equipment/plant scenarios mostly relate to short -
duration activities involving noise intrusive equipment such as saw cutting and rockhammering. These 
scenarios are modelled to ensure the assessment captures these intensive conditions but are expected 
to be infrequent and of limited duration. In contrast, typical construction wor ks involve less noise 
intrusive equipment and capture typical expected operations and usage within any given 15 -minute 
interval. As such, noise levels during the typical activities are generally lower and more representative 
of day-to-day site construction. 
 
The predicted noise levels in Table 7 -9 of the NVIA show that the surrounding receivers are not 
expected to experience noise levels above the noise management levels (NML’s) with the exception of 
the sensitive receivers located at NCA01, NCA02 and NCA03 , and generally only during periods when 
high noise equipment/plant  and loud earthmoving equipment are in use.  However, based on the 
predictions in Table 7 -9 of the NVIA , the following should be noted regarding the potential noise 
impacts on the nearest sensitive receivers:  
 
• Construction noise levels at the receivers are predicted to be above the NMLs when high noise 
activities are being undertaken (rock hammering or saw cutting) at locations closest to the 
receiver. During these works, construction noise levels are expected t o be 7-8 dB(A) above the 
NML during bulk earthworks and 1-5 dB(A) above the NML during road construction (the most 
noise-intensive construction periods).   
• During typical construction works and activities, the expected noise levels at the schools or 
retirement village in NCA01 and NCA02 are not predicted to be above the NMLs.  
• For residences in NCA01 and NCA03, during stages that are in close proximity to these receivers, 
construction noise levels are predicted to be above the NML during a range of construction 
activities.  
• For all other residences or during typical construction activities, due to the substantial distance 
from the construction works, the construction noise levels are predicted to be below the 
standard hours NMLs.  
  
Construction Noise Mitigation Measures  
  
It is expected that the predicted exceedances of the NMLs at sensitive receivers can be effectively 
reduced through the preparation and implementation of a Construction Noise and Vibration 
Management Plan (CNVMP). The following management and mitigation measures have been noted in 
the NVIA and would be implemented into the CNVMP, prepared as a post-approval matter:  
 
• General engineering controls, including screening, acoustic enclosures, silencers and 
substitution by alternative process.   
• Noise management measures listed in Table 7 -14 of the NVIA, including with consideration of 
potential cumulative impacts.  
• Mitigation measures for diesel-powered cranes.  
• OOHW evening and night works, including construction layout and planning.  
  
The NVIA also recommends noise monitoring be carried out to understand the effectiveness of the 
implementation of the CNVMP in the event of a sustained complaint. Additional noise mitigation and 
management measures would need to be employed in accordance with the ICNG to reduce noise 
impacts on nearby receivers. Through the implementation of a comprehensive CNVMP, the NVIA 
concludes that the construction noise impacts of the construction works can be effectively managed 
to protect the reasonable amenity of nearby sensitive receivers.  
[[page 136]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  130  
7.2.3 Operational Noise  
The proposed development seeks consent to operate 24 hours a day, seven days a week. The main 
operational components of the proposed development, with likely noise emission sources, are 
identified as follows:  
 
• Operation of the data centre buildings and associated noise sources, including rooftop 
mechanical plant.  
• High Voltage Substation (HVVS) and other electrical transformers.  
• Cooling systems.  
• Operation of backup generators during routine testing and emergency events.  
• Vehicle movements on site, including the deliveries and car parking.  
• Additional building services and mechanical systems.   
  
Project Amenity Noise Levels  
 
To ensure that the cumulative industrial noise level (comprising existing and new sources) remains 
within the recommended ambient noise levels for the area, the proposed development specific 
amenity noise level must be applied to each new industrial noise source entering the precinct.  The 
recommended amenity noise level from Table 2.2 of the NPfI represents the total industrial noise level 
from all sources. Adjustments to address cumu lative industrial noise are then applied to these noise 
levels to determin e the proposed development -specific amenity noise level. The NVIA includes 
adjustments to the recommended amenity noise levels to determine the proposed development -
specific amenity noise level identified for each of the three receiver groups due to the differing nature 
and potential timing of noise impacts across these receiver groups.   
  
Receivers outside the MRP  
 
DPHI’s current approach for determining acceptable amenity goals for individual industrial 
developments within the MRP is understood to use  an area-based approach consistent with Chapter 
2 of the Noise Policy for Industry (NPfI), similar to that shown in NPfI Section 2.4.2, but based upon the 
area of a development (TABLE 37).  
 
TABLE 33: MRP AREA-BASED PRECINCT NOISE GOAL ADJUSTMENT  
Mamre Road Precinct Area Proposal Area Cumulative adjustment to amenity goal 
based upon Proposal area, dB(A) 
999 hectares 52 hectares -13 
972 hectares (SP2 zoning removed) -13 
949 hectares (IN1 zone only -12 
 
The NVIA has proposed an updated approach to provide a cumulative adjustment factor based on 
additional considerations for large industrial clusters considering specifics of the MRP, including:  
 
• Separation factor – due to the large size of the MRP and the large distances between parts of 
the MRP and surrounding receivers, development areas of the MRP will be either constrained 
or unable to contribute noise up to the allocated noise levels at each receiver area using the 
current cumulative adjustment approach. Further refinement to the current area based 
approach is appropriate to consider this unused capacity. A review showed a further 1 -2 dB(A) 
adjustment could be considered. 
• Concurrency factor - the larger the development cluster and the number of sites, the less likely 
it is that all sites will be operating at worst -case noise levels concurrently. Assuming that only 
50% of the industries in the MRP would be operating at a rea sonable worst-case scenario in a 
single 15-minute period a further 3 dB(A) adjustment could be considered.  
[[page 137]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  131  
 
Based on the consideration of these factors, and an updated approach is detailed in the NVIA. 
Maintaining a conservative approach, the cumulative adjustment to the amenity goal based on the 
Proposal area has been reduced from -13 to -10.  
  
Receivers inside the MRP (long-term)  
 
The methodology for determining the cumulative correction for these receivers, including the schools 
and retirement village to the north, is provided in Appendix C.2 of the NVIA and considers the potential 
impacts, and surrounding noise environment for bot h the short -term and long term situations to 
determine appropriate PNTLs in accordance with Section 2.4 of the NPfI. This approach considers a 
short-term -5 dB(A) cumulative adjustment with minimal surrounding industrial noise as the MRP is 
developing and a correction of -10 dB(A) for the future fully developed MRP when all surrounding 
contributing industrial developments are operating.  
  
Receivers inside the MRP (short-term)  
 
The NVIA has adopted the NPfI standard approach of a -5 dB(A) reduction for short -term receivers 
within the MRP. This reduction is based on three to four other developments contributing noise 
concurrently at each receiver as the MRP is developing.   
 
Project Intrusive Noise Levels  
To minimise a noticeable difference as a result of a new development the NPfI also sets the proposed 
development intrusiveness noise level for residences. The intrusiveness of a noise source may generally 
be considered acceptable if the equivalent continuous (energy-average) A-weighted level of noise from 
the source (represented by the LAeq,15min descriptor) does no t exceed the background noise level 
measured in the absence of the source by more than 5 dB(A). The proposed development intrusiveness 
noise level has been established for managing short -term impacts at residences, but consistent with 
NPfI Section 2.4.3, is not being used to address long-term impacts due the area undergoing significant 
change and the existing noise environment likely changing. 
  
Project Noise Trigger Levels  
  
The proposed development noise trigger levels (PNTLs) have then been derived from the lower value 
of the proposed development  intrusiveness noise levels and proposed development  amenity noise 
levels, considering the cumulative adjustments detailed above for the proposed development amenity 
noise levels. The NVIA provides a summary of the PNTLs for each receiver type/area in Table 5-9.   
  
Operational Noise Assessment  
  
The NVIA modelled the expected typical operational scenarios, which included a breakdown of 
expected noise levels during each of the stages up to when the campus is fully developed (10 years):  
 
• Typical operations – all plant and equipment operating during each of the day, evening and 
night periods, in addition to maintenance testing of back -up generators during the day, with 
associated equipment under a scheduled testing regime (up to 6 generator s at a time during 
daylight hours with a run time of 60 minutes).  
 
Additional modelling of the emergency critical power failure was included to show the potential noise 
levels in this extremely rare scenario. 
 
The NVIA incorporated into the design a range of mitigation measures, in addition to proposing a 
number of mitigation and management measures to address potential exceedances of PNTLs at 
receiver’s for the proposed development due to the operation of chill ers, electrical distribution and 
[[page 138]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  132  
mechanical plant/building services. Other noise sources operating on the site would have a minor or 
negligible contribution to overall noise levels emanating from the site.   
 
The incorporated and recommended design mitigation measures are provided in Table 8-11 of the NVIA 
and include acoustic attenuation of air chillers, generators, mechanical plant and building services and 
noise barriers and enclosures. 
  
A testing regime for the diesel generators is also proposed to reduce potential noise impacts on the 
schools and retirement village to the north, including testing the nearest generators during school 
holidays/weekends and limiting the number of generators tested at any one time in areas closer to the 
receivers. 
  
The NVIA modelled the potential noise levels of the proposed development with these mitigation 
measures operating based on a number of assessment scenarios covering the phases of the proposed 
development (refer to Table 8-14 of the NVIA).   
  
The modelling predicted that under the worst-case operating scenarios, including at full development, 
noise levels at all receivers outside the MRP and long -term receivers inside the MRP would be below 
the PNTL’s. This includes the Catholic Healthcare Emmaus Retirement Village (NCA02), Trinity Catholic 
Primary School (NCA02) and Emmaus Catholic College (NCA02).  
  
Residual noise impacts (exceedances of the PNTLs) have been shown in the NVIA to occur at ten short-
term receivers (nine residences and one school) within the MRP (TABLE 38). All receivers are identified 
with residual impacts not greater than 5 dB(A) above the PNTLs and below the NPfI recommended 
amenity noise levels. The residual impacts would also be considered marginal as per the NPfI.   
  
Several of these receiver locations are dwellings ancillary to existing industrial or commercial 
operations, which would not be expected to be overly sensitive to marginal noise impacts. It is again 
noted that the Mamre Anglican School is slated to move to the Sydney Science Park in 2029; therefore, 
any residual noise impacts would either not occur or be short -lived in the case they are presented 
beyond this date.   
  
As the receiver locations within the MRP with residual impacts are on strategically identified and zoned 
land for sought -after industrial development, it is considered likely that the dwellings would be 
removed and sites transitioned to industrial development, and so residual noise impacts would either 
not occur as in many cases they are only expected during later phases or the marginal impacts would 
be short-lived.  
  
As the receiver locations within the MRP with residual impacts are on strategically identified and zoned 
land for sought -after industrial development, it is considered likely that the dwellings would be 
removed and sites transitioned to industrial developm ent, and the marginal impacts would also be 
short-lived. However, should the sensitive receivers be occupied at the commencement of operations 
of the proposed development, and predicted impacts are still expected following detailed design 
review and commissioning/noise verification, consultation with the receivers would be carried out, and 
reasonable at-receiver property treatments would be proposed.

Operational Noise Management and Mitigation   
  
The Proposal includes several management and mitigation measures, as described above and in 
Sections 8.3.2 to 8.2.6 of the NVIA. These measures would be implemented through an Operational 
Noise Management Plan (ONMP), including ongoing noise compliance mon itoring. As detailed in the 
NVIA, additional measures can be implemented to reduce noise impacts as part of the detailed design 
of the proposed development at the construction certificate stage, including the selection of plant and 
equipment with reduced sound power levels, among other measures.   
  
The ONMP would also incorporate continuous evaluations of mitigation measures and management 
practices to integrate and seize opportunities for noise emission reductions, thereby further 
diminishing the anticipated noise levels at sensitive receivers.   
7.2.4 Sleep Disturbance  
The potential for sleep disturbance resulting from maximum noise level events originating from the 
site during the night -time period must be carefully considered as per the NPfI which provides the 
following trigger levels:  
 
• LAeq,15min 40 dB(A) or the prevailing RBL plus 5 dB, whichever is the greater, and/or  
• LAFmax  52 dB(A) or the prevailing RBL plus 15 dB, whichever is the greater.  
 
The NPfI requires further analysis of sleep disturbance when project -specific noise is expected to 
exceed the screening levels.  
 
The night -time noise sources associated with the Proposal are steady -state or quasi -steady-state. 
Therefore, there is unlikely to be significant variation between L Aeq,15min values and LAFmax values, and no 
significant peak noise events are expected. The NVIA concludes that, through the achievement of the 
night PNTLs for the proposed development, the sleep disturbance L Aeq,15min assessment levels will be 
inherently satisfied. 
[[page 140]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  134  
The only potentially loud instantaneous noise events that could occur across the Proposal operations 
with the potential to disturb sleep could be loading dock or carpark related activities 
The sleep disturbance project assessment noise levels for the proposed development are provided in 
Sections 8.5.4 of the NVIA (refer to Table 8-22 of the NVIA).  
 
The NVIA concludes that, the highest predicted noise level for each of the residential NCAs for the fully 
developed campus will achieve the L AFmax values sleep disturbance assessment levels and sleep 
disturbance impacts are not predicted. 
7.2.5 Road Noise  
The potential noise impacts as a result of the operational or construction road traffic from the Proposal 
on public roads must be assessed in accordance with the TfNSW Road Noise Policy (RNP). The RNP 
road traffic noise criteria for various receiver types and a requirement that any increase in the total 
traffic noise level should be limited to 2 dB(A) above that of the corresponding ‘no build option’ before 
further feasible and reasonable mitigation measures are required. This is applicable to both operational 
and construction activities associated with the proposal.  
 
The predicted traffic noise increase on public roads based on the expected traffic (including heavy 
vehicles) during construction and operations was assessed in the NVIA based on the findings of the 
Transport Management and Accessibility Plan prepared by Ason.  
 
The NVIA concluded that the predicted change in traffic noise levels from additional operational and 
construction vehicles was less than 2 dB(A). Construction related road traffic noise is therefore expected 
to comply with the requirements of the RNP.   
7.2.6 Vibration  
The NVIA includes a construction vibration assessment based on the recommended minimum working 
distances for the most vibration-intensive plant expected, adopting a conservative approach. The site-
specific minimum working distances for construction, includ ing potential cosmetic damage and 
human annoyance for residences and schools, are shown in Figure 70. The nearest sensitive receivers 
include residences in NCA01, and Mamre Anglican School in NCA02, and the nearest warehouses in 
Kemps Creek Estate to the east and south. This assessment has concluded that the nearest sensitive 
receivers are located close to but outside of the minimum working distances for human annoyance, 
with the exception of the adjacent future warehouses when 13t vibratory rollers are in use nearby to 
these receiver during the proposed works.  
  
Considering this, to reduce the potential human annoyance impact on these receivers, the NVIA 
proposes several mitigation measures to be implemented when vibration -generating equipment is 
being utilised in close proximity, including:  
  
• Notification of vibration -intensive work periods to receivers and provision of complaint -
handling procedures.  
• Where vibration is found to be excessive, management measures should be implemented to 
ensure compliance with vibration standards. Management measures may include 
modifications to construction methods, such as using smaller equipment, establishing safe 
buffer zones, and, if necessary, imposing time restrictions on the most excessive vibration 
activities.  
• Carry out attended vibration measurements and establish site specific acceptable minimum 
working distances.  
  
The NVIA concluded that the potential for human annoyance from vibration can be reduced or 
managed to acceptable levels, subject to the implementation of the above -noted management 
measures as part of an overarching Construction Noise and Vibration Management Plan (CNVMP) and

7.3 Air Quality  
An Air Quality Impact Assessment (AQIA) has been prepared by Northstar for the proposal and is 
attached as Appendix 16 of this EIS. The AQIA has been prepared in accordance with the Approved 
Methods for the Modelling and Assessment of Air Pollutants in New South Wales (Approved Methods) 
(EPA, 2022). The assessment methodology includes modelling local meteorology and the dispersion of 
potential emissions from the proposed construction and operation to predict potential air quality 
impacts on the surrounding environment. The proposed development has the potential to generate 
air quality impacts that can be effectively mitigated and managed during construction and operational 
phases.

7.3.1 Existing Environment  
The site is located within the IN1 General Industrial zone within the MRP. Population density analysis 
using 2024 census data shows that the immediate site area has "very low" density (0-500 persons/km²), 
indicating a predominantly industrial area. Higher population densities are located as follows:  
  
• North – St Clair and Erskine Park   
• Southeast – Abbotsbury and Bossley Park   
• Southwest – Luddenham   
  
Receptor locations used in the AQIA include residential, educational, industrial, medical, recreational, 
and boundary receptors. The receptor list (provided in Appendix B of the AQIA and illustrated in Figure 
71 and Figure 72 represents a cross-section of available locations used to characterise larger areas and 
more sensitive populations susceptible to changes in air pollution. The nearest sensitive receptors to 
the site are located to the north and include the Mamre Anglican  School, Trinity Catholic Primary 
School, Emmaus Catholic College, Emmaus Retirement Village and some rural dwellings on sites 
transitioning to industrial uses.

Construction Phase 
 
The proposal will be implemented in two (2) stages over a period of 10 years. Stage 1 would occur over 
the first five years and Stage 2 over the second five years. The earthworks component of the proposal 
has the greatest potential for fugitive dust emissions, and this component of construction has been the 
subject of the majority of the assessment in the AQIA.    
 
The AQIA includes a quantitative assessment of potential construction impacts based on the proposed 
construction staging and surrounding construction activities on other sites within the Mamre Road 
Precinct (MRP). The AQIA focused on dust soiling and on the increased ambient PM concentrations 
resulting from dust from construction activities on the site.   
 
Exceedances of the 24 -hour PM 10 criteria are predicted at receptors close to the site for Stage 1 
construction works, which include bulk earthworks and roadworks. These predicted exceedances 
occur on days when elevated regional background concentrations exist. The incremental impact of the 
construction activities is minor and not shown to be heavily impacted by surrounding construction 
activities in the MRP. The AQIA does not predict any exceedances of the criterion during Stage 2 
construction works.    
 
No additional exceedances of the 24-hour PM2.5 criterion are anticipated at any receptor during Stage 
1 or Stage 2 construction activities. The annual average criteria for TSP, PM 2.5, and dust deposition are 
[[page 146]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  140  
expected to be met at all receptors throughout both construction stages, even when accounting for 
impacts from surrounding construction activities within the MRP.   
 
In terms of the annual average PM 10 criterion, an exceedance is predicted at the Mamre Anglican 
School and the Mamre Christian College during Stage 1 construction works and two industrial receivers 
during Stage 2 construction works.    
 
To reduce the potential air quality impacts of the  proposed development's construction and ensure 
exceedances of the relevant air quality criteria are mitigated, a comprehensive Construction Air Quality 
Management Plan (CAQMP) has been prepared  (Appendix 16). The CAQMP includes real -time short-
term (e.g., 1 -hour average) PM 10 monitoring as part of the T rigger Action Response Plan (TARP)  and 
implements a hierarchy of responses to ensure that impacts associated with the construction works 
are effectively managed. These responses include, among others:  
 
• Monitoring and ceasing work during adverse meteorological conditions.   
• Use of water sprays on stockpiles.   
• Limit vehicle speed limits to 15km/h.   
• Erect solid screens or barriers around dusty activities or the site boundary that are at least as 
high as any stockpiles on site.   
• Minimise drop heights from truck dumping to 1.5 m.   
• Implementation of track -out measures (e.g. wheel washing system) for trucks accessing the 
site. 
• Install hard -surfaced haul routes, which are regularly damped down with fixed or mobile 
sprinkler systems, or mobile water bowsers, and regularly cleaned.   
 
Key Performance Indicators for the construction of the proposed development are provided as triggers 
in Table 7 of the AQMP, including the established construction air quality levels for dust and PM. The 
construction activities on site would be constantly monitored, and should any triggers be met, 
additional actions and mitigation measures would be implemented.   
 
Subject to the implementation and monitoring of the effectiveness of the mitigation and management 
measures in the AQMP, the AQIA concludes that the potential construction impacts of the proposed 
development can be reduced to respond to the predicted exceedances.    
 
Operation Phase 
 
The proposed development has the potential to generate air quality impacts during routine generator 
testing and emergency power outages from generator exhaust emissions. The potential for emergency 
power outages is considered low as there has not been a sustained loss of electricity within the 
industrial precinct and main transmission lines (Sydney West BSP operated by Transgrid) since 2005. 
In the event of a full power outage, including all site feeders being offline, lithium-ion batteries would 
provide an immediate power supply until the backup generators reach full operation. The generators 
would provide continuous power until electricity is restored.  
 
To ensure the generators are operable and for maintenance purposes, regular testing of each back-up 
generator is required.  Up to six generators would be tested concurrently, between the hours of 7:00 
am to 6:00 pm. 
 
Key pollutants linked to the proposed development's operation include nitrogen dioxide (NO 2) and 
particulate matter (PM10 and PM2.5). These potential emissions result from the combustion of diesel fuel 
in the back-up generators.   
 
The proposal would be implemented in six (6) phases  over two broad construction /implementation 
stages. Potential air quality impacts during each of those six (6) phases are presented within the AQIA, 
[[page 147]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  141  
noting that the number of operational backup diesel generators will vary according to the number of 
phases in operation  (which limits the amount of generators  that could either be operated in an 
emergency scenario, or subject to maintenance testing (with a maximum of six operating at any given 
time).  
 
Detailed dispersion modelling has been undertaken as part of the AQIA for both the justified worst -
case impacts associated with emergency scenarios and regular maintenance testing scenarios. 
 
The justified worst-case scenarios have been developed to represent the potential maximum air quality 
impacts associated with the  proposed development. The proposal differs from other data centre 
developments in that a significant level of redundancy is built into the design to ensure that the use of 
diesel generators is minimised. It is noted that many other data centres may be connected to  the 
electricity supply grid by one (1) feeder only, such that any interruption to that supply would require full 
deployment of back -up power via diesel generators. In this case, three (3) feeders would supply the 
proposal, and generators would only be required for a short time, should supply from one (1) or two (2) 
feeders fail. 
 
Scenario E1 to E6 – Justified Worst Case Scenarios:  
 
The proposed development of the justified worst-case scenarios during all phases of the development 
has considered the following:  
 
• One (1) feeder failure: Some generators would be required to be operated temporarily (15 to 20 
minutes) whilst a medium voltage changeover occurs in the rings. Generators would only be 
used until the electricity supply is reconnected or whilst the changeover is in progress.   
• Two (2) feeder failures: In this scenario, further and temporary generator support would be 
initiated to maintain full site load during switching and load balancing operations. Generator 
operation would again be generally short-duration and controlled until the network has been 
reconfigured and supply redundancy is restored.   
• Three (3) feeder failures: In this event, all standby generation would operate to supply 100% of 
critical and essential loads, ensuring continuity of data centre operations for the duration of 
the grid outage, subject to available diesel fuel storage and resupply. This scenario is extremely 
unlikely.   
 
Scenario M1 to M6 – Realistic (Maintenance Testing) Scenarios  
 
The anticipated testing schedule involves either four (4) 2MW generators (during phase 1) or six (6) 2MW 
generators operating at 100% load (phase 2 onwards) within the specified testing hours. The proposed 
development of the maintenance testing scenarios during all phases of the development has 
considered the following:  
 
▪ These scenarios are likely to occur periodically, where generators would be tested 
simultaneously at any one time during daytime hours (assumed to occur at any time between 
7:00 am and 6:00 pm [11 hours]).    
▪ The combination of generators (of a total of 846), which result in the maximum incremental 
and cumulative impacts at each receptor vary, and detailed analysis has been performed to 
confirm that the impacts predicted and presented within the AQIA represent those potential 
maximum impacts.    
▪ Comparison of impacts against both short and long -term criteria is performed, providing 
another level of conservatism, as the testing schedule is modelled as occurring for every one (1) 
of the hours between 7:00 am and 6:00 pm, on each and every day of the  year (4,015 hours). 
Testing hours are anticipated to be limited to 141 hours per year (of an allowable 200 hours). 
This approach is required to be performed in this manner to account for all potential 
combinations of emissions, meteorology and background conditions. Annual average 
[[page 148]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  142  
incremental predictions are therefore weighted by actual operating hours over those modelled 
to account for annual average emission loads.
