Based on the worst -case standby generator operational scenario (Scenarios E1 to E6), a number of 
additional exceedances of the short -term air quality criteria for NO 2, PM10 and PM2.5 are predicted, as 
presented in Tables 126 to 131 in the AQIA.  
 
As noted in the AQIA, care must be taken when assessing 24 -hour average impacts, as the actual 
duration of a power outage is likely to be much shorter than 24 hours. Therefore, the assessment should 
be considered highly conservative, as the operation of al l backup generators for an entire 24 -hour 
period has been modelled (which is extremely unlikely to occur).   
  
Six scenarios have been assessed, assuming that up to 846 generators would be operational at any 
given time to provide critical IT load and sustained operations (at ultimate development). The predicted 
incremental concentrations under Scenarios E1 to E6 show exceedances of particulate matter (as PM10 
and PM2.5 and NO2) at sensitive receptor locations if a power outage occurred, and the generators are 
operating at continuous 100% load.    
  
An important aspect of the AQIA is the probability assessment which assesses the probability of an 
exceedance of the NO2, PM10 or PM2.5 criterion in any year of operation, considering a number of factors 
including the reliability of the electrical network. The probability assessment has been prepared using 
the latest information contained in the Endeavour Energy 2024 Distribution Annual P lanning Report 
(DAPR) (Endeavour Energy, 2024). The DAPR concluded that the average unplanned outage duration 
per customer per year over a 10 -year period from financial year 2013 (2013/14) to financial year 2023 
(2023/24) was approximately 82.0 minutes, or 0.016% of the time per year.  
  
The AQIA estimated the probability of 1, 2 or 3 feeders failing simultaneously leading to off-site impacts 
were calculated to occur once every  10,831 years, as an absolute maximum . To put the risk into 
perspective, the safety margin provided by three electricity feeders has been compared to industry -
standard Safety Integrity Levels (SIL). The SIL for the proposal exceeds the levels required to 
demonstrate that the risk is ‘as low as reasonably possible’ (ALARP) by a significant margin.   
  
The AQIA concluded that despite the predicted worst -case scenario exceedances of the relevant air 
quality criteria during emergency events, resulting from interruptions to the three feeders, the actual 
probability of the assessed scenarios transpiring is extremely low, and therefore the likelihood of the 
proposed development exceeding the relevant air quality criteria at nearby receivers is also extremely 
low.   
  
The AQIA concluded that the potential air quality impacts of the proposal operation in emergency 
scenarios would be acceptable, based on the extremely low likelihood of all three feeders failing, 
subject to an ongoing monitoring and reporting regime, including ongoing testing of diesel generator 
emissions.   
 
Scenario M1 to M6 Impacts 
 
Annual average PM concentrations (as TSP, PM 10 and PM2.5) resulting from maintenance scenarios are 
predicted to comply with the relevant impact assessment criteria, with no exceedances predicted.    
  
Under the maintenance scenarios, no additional exceedances of the cumulative impact assessment 
criteria for 24 -hour PM 10 and 24 -hour PM 2.5 are predicted as a result of the Proposal. While an 
exceedance of the 24 -hour PM 10 and PM₂.₅ criteria is identified, these are attributable to elevated 
background concentrations that already exceed the respective criteria and are not related to emissions 
from the Proposal.    
  
Predicted incremental and cumulative 1 (one) hour NO 2 concentrations remain below the relevant 
criteria at all considered receptor locations.    
[[page 152]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  146  
Predicted concentrations of CO, SO 2, PAHs, VOCs, and CH 2O across all assessed averaging periods are 
also below the relevant impact assessment criteria at all receptor locations under Scenarios M1 to M6.    
  
Correspondingly, it is anticipated that during the operation of the testing schedule no significant air 
quality impacts are predicted to be experienced at sensitive receptors during routine operations.   
  
Cumulative impacts  
 
Cumulative impacts during the routine operation of the Proposal have been considered in a 
quantitative manner, taking account of the routine operation of surrounding data centres and 
including regional background concentrations (for PM and NO 2). The inclusion of surrounding data 
centres does not result in any cumulative exceedances of the air quality criteria during any of the 
maintenance scenarios assessed. Based on those findings, impacts associated with other pollutants are 
not anticipated t o result in a ny significant cumulative impacts when considering surrounding data 
centre operations.  
7.3.3 Management and/or Mitigation Measures   
Construction Phase 
 
A Construction Air Quality Management Plan (CAQMP) has been developed to support the proposal to 
minimise the risk of unacceptable air quality impacts during the construction phase. The CAQMP 
includes a range of mitigation measures to be employed, an air q uality monitoring program, and a 
Trigger Action Response Plan (TARP) designed to minimise any off -site air quality impacts. The range 
of mitigation and management measures in the CAQMP includes:  
 
• Monitoring and ceasing work during adverse meteorological conditions.   
• Use of water sprays on stockpiles.   
• Limiting vehicle speed limits to 15km/h.   
• Erect solid screens or barriers around dusty activities or the site boundary that are at least as 
high as any stockpiles on site.   
• Minimise drop heights from truck dumping to 1.5 m.   
• Implementation of track -out measures (e.g. wheel washing system) for trucks accessing the 
site   
• Install hard -surfaced haul routes, which are regularly damped down with fixed or mobile 
sprinkler systems, or mobile water bowsers, and regularly cleaned.   
 
Key Performance Indicators for the construction of the proposal are provided as triggers in Table 7 of 
the CAQMP, including the established construction air quality levels for dust and PM. The construction 
activities on site would be constantly monitored, and should any triggers be reached, additional actions 
and mitigation measures would be taken.  
 
Operation Phase 
 
The AQIA concluded that, based on the results of the dispersion modelling assessment under Scenarios 
M1 to M6, the operation of the testing schedule will not cause exceedances at sensitive receptor 
locations surrounding the Proposal site. According to the assumptions in the AQIA, the Proposal's 
operation does not require any additional management strategies. The Proposal will be operated in 
accordance with the conditions of the proposed development consent and the EPL, subject to the 
approval of the development. The following operational phase air quality management strategies are 
recommended:   
 
[[page 153]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  147  
• Maintenance testing will be scheduled when nearby data centres are not conducting similar 
operations. This will require communication and coordination between data centre operators 
to minimise overlapping emissions.    
• The testing will not occur during periods of high background air quality levels. Reference will 
be made to the NSW EPA webpage reporting the Air Quality Category (AQC) for St Marys AQMS 
on the day of planned maintenance to assess background conditions.   
• Testing will also be prioritised outside of school hours, on weekends and during school 
holidays.   
7.4 Traffic and Transport  
A Transport Management and Accessibility Plan (TMAP) has been prepared by Ason and is included as 
Appendix 10. A preliminary Construction Traffic Management Plan (CTMP)  and a preliminary Green 
Travel Plan (GTP) ha ve also been prepared and are included as  appendices to the Transport 
Management and Accessibility Plan. These plans have been prepared in accordance with the relevant 
TfNSW and Austroads guidelines.  
7.4.1 Existing Environment  
The site is primarily accessed via Bakers Lane, a single-lane-each-way local road running east-west that 
provides access to rural residential properties, schools, and a retirement village . In the vicinity of the 
site, between Bakers Lane and Darrabarra Avenue, Mamre Road operates as a two-lane, two-way major 
north-south arterial road linking the Great Western Highway to the M4 Motorway and Elizabeth Drive . 
The three key signalised intersections serving the area are: 
 
▪ Mamre Road/Bakers Lane (upgraded mid-2023); 
▪ Mamre Road/Darrabarra Avenue (constructed late-2024); and 
▪ Mamre Road/Abbotts Road (currently being upgraded with signali sation works underway as 
of September 2025). 
 
Darrabarra Avenue, another east-west road, currently serves the Aspect Industrial Estate and is planned 
to function as a four  (4) lane collector road connecting to the future  Berriwerri Drive as part of the 
broader MRP network. The surrounding area is undergoing significant transformation, with ongoing 
construction works associated with the progressive delivery of Mamre Road Precinct infrastructure to 
support the region's industrial development. 
7.4.2 Assessment of Impacts  
Operational Access 
 
Once operational, all traffic will access the Mamre Road Data Centre Campus via a new four (4) leg 
roundabout on Berriwerri Drive, which will be constructed and dedicated to Council as part of the MRP 
Road Network. 
 
The site will be split into Western and Eastern campuses (divided by Berriwerri Drive) with the following 
primary access points proposed in addition to emergency and egress points: 
 
▪ Western Campus : Two  (2) way access forming the direct western leg of the Berriwerri 
Drive/Local Road roundabout. 
▪ Eastern Campus: Separate entry and exit access points from the east-west local industrial road. 
 
The access design accommodates: 
 
▪ 20m articulated vehicles (AV) for right-in and right-out movements; 
▪ 12.5m heavy rigid vehicles (HRV) for right-in, left-out, and right-out movements; and 
[[page 154]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  148  
▪ 30m a-double movements at the western campus roundabout for safe turnaround within the 
publicly accessible area. 
 
Both campuses include secure entry gates with queuing analysis showing adequate capacity . Public 
queuing areas comprise 90m queuing length across multiple lanes, accommodating approximately 13 
cars without impacting on exit movements for the data centre campus. 
 
Operational Parking 
 
In accordance with the MRP DCP 2021, the parking rates for industrial development is the greater of 
the following: 
 
1. 1 space per 200m2; or 
2. 1 space per 2 employees. 
 
Applying the above is considered an overprovision noting that industrial land uses encompass a large 
variety of operations including traditional labour intense manufacturing to modern high -technology 
businesses. This is reflected in the variability in the nominal parking requirements under the MRP DCP 
2021, varying between 550 parking spaces (under the employee rate) and 1,1 38 parking spaces (based 
on the floor space rate ). In the case of data centres, it is argued that the resultant number of staff 
required for such operations is very low. 
 
The MRP DCP 2021 floor space rate has thus been disregarded (relating to industry uses involving the 
production or storage of physical goods) to which the requirement based on the employee rate would 
be for 550 parking spaces. In response, provision for 6 19 parking spaces is provided and would 
nominally comply with this requirement. 
 
To provide further validation of the parking supply with respect to the proposed floor space, a weighted 
average parking rate was established by benchmarking six (6) established data centres in Greater 
Sydney, as set out in TABLE 42.

P a g e  149  
The proposal also includes a café, which is largely intended to service the campus, but will also be open 
to the general public. In ordinarily applying guideline parking rates to the café component, the 
Transport for NSW Guide to Transport Impact Assessments (GTIA) has been referenced in lieu of such 
rates being published in the MRP DCP 2021. For take -away food outlets that have on -site seating but 
without drive-through facilities, the applicable parking rates are either:  
 
▪ 12 spaces per 100m2 GFA; OR  
▪ The greater of: 
o 1 space per five seats (both internal and external seating); OR  
o 1 space per two seats (internal seating). 
 
For reference, the café contains approximately 400m2 GFA and has a nominated seating capacity for 
70 customers internally and 96 customers externally. Application of the seating-based rate yields a 
parking nominal parking requirement for 35 parking spaces while the floor space rate yields a 
requirement for 48 parking spaces. In response, a provision for 79 parking spaces has been proposed 
which can be accommodated 540 allocated for the sub parcels. These spaces are located within close 
proximity to the COSH building (containing the café) and include 33 customer parking spaces accessed 
securely (within the western campus) and 13 customer parking spaces accessible by the public (before 
the secure entry gates). This corresponds to a ratio of 75% internal customers vs 25% external customers 
and is considered an appropriate ratio given the café is largely intended to service a captive market. 
 
The MRP DCP 2021 stipulates the following bicycle parking rates for the following uses: 
 
▪ Bicycle Parking: 
o Industry: one ( 1) space per 1 ,000m2 of gross floor area of industrial activities (over 
2,000m2 gross floor area). 
o Office: one ( 1) space per 600m 2 of gross floor area of office and retail space (over 
1,200m2 gross floor area). 
▪ End of Trip Facilities 
o Industry: For industrial activities with a gross floor area over 4,000m 2, at least one (1) 
shower cubicle with ancillary change rooms. 
o Office: For ancillary office and retail space with a gross floor area over 2500m2, at least 
one (1) shower cubicle with ancillary change rooms. 
 
Application of these rates to the data hall (industry) and support space (office/café) components results 
in a nominal requirement for 284 bicycle spaces for the proposal. In response, the Mamre Road Data 
Centre Campus will include a consolidated bicycle e nd of trip facility that provides capacity for 30 
bicycle spaces (vertical rails) and 4 4 locker spaces and shower/ change rooms. While this represents a 
nominal shortfall relative to the MRP DCP 2021  rates, the proposed arrangements are considered 
reasonable in light of the number of spaces supporting a cycling mode share of 5% as set out in the 
target mode shares set in the GTP. The provision reflects the realistic uptake of cycling transport (given 
the inherent catchment limitations of Kemps Creek) while providing a high level of amenity to 
encourage users to use this form of transport, where possible. 
 
Operational Traffic Generation 
 
The GTIA does not stipulate any trip rates for data centre land uses. As such, a benchmark exercise was 
undertaken in accordance with the GTIA to assess the trip generating potential of six (6) established 
data centres within Greater Sydney. Based on  a 24-hour survey undertaken in May 2024, the 
benchmarked average site peaks for the data centres are set out below: 
 
▪ AM Peak: 0.07 vehicle trips per hour / 100m2 GFA  
▪ PM Peak: 0.06 vehicle trips per hour / 100m2 GFA  
▪ Daily:  0.70 vehicle trips per day / 100m2 GFA 
 
[[page 156]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  150  
The benchmarked site trip rates for data centres are appreciably lower than guideline rates for other 
industry uses, including large -format warehousing  under the GTIA. Notwithstanding, shift patterns 
were observed to vary across the survey data centres, including the number of shifts (two  (2) versus 
three (3) changeovers) and timing across the 24-hour period. In this regard, a first principles assessment 
was also undertaken for the proposal, and a 24-hour profile of vehicle trips was established by scaling 
the arrival and departure profile to the daily trip generation rate set under the benchmarking study. 
That is, the total daily trip generation of the proposal would be consistent with the benchmark daily 
trip rate surveyed across the six (6) data centres in Greater Sydney, with the AM and PM site and network 
peak trips being uniquely associated with the proposal. 
 
The resulting AM and PM trip generation and derivative trip rates for network and site peaks for the 
proposal have been adopted for modelling development volumes on the surrounding road network.  
This equates to: 
 
Network peak - AM and PM trip generation volumes of 141 vehicle trips per hour , and 171 vehicle trips 
per hour for the network, and 167 vehicle trips per hour  
 
Site peak - AM and PM trip generation volumes of 1 67 vehicle trips per hour and 17 4 vehicle trips per 
hour for the network and 167 vehicle trips per hour 
 
For comparison, the traffic volumes under each rate have been contrasted with the default MRP trip 
rates (0.23/0.24) in Figure 73, affirming that the trip-generating potential for data centres is well below 
warehouse and industry uses.

The future traffic flows on the surrounding road network have been assessed using SIDRA modelling. 
SIDRA modelling determines the average delay (AVD) that vehicles encounter, the degree of saturation 
(DOS) of the intersection and the Level of Service (LOS) of the intersection. The LOS criteria are as follows 
(for signalised intersections and roundabouts): 
 
▪ A: Good operation; 
▪ B: Good with acceptable delays and spare capacity;

▪ C: Satisfactory;  
▪ D: Operating near capacity;  
▪ E: At capacity. At signals, incidents will cause excessive delays. Roundabouts will require other 
controls; and  
▪ F: Extra capacity required.  
 
Traffic modelling using SIDRA software was undertaken for the base year of 2025, along with future 
horizon years of 2027 (expected development opening year) and 2036 (10 years post -opening). The 
modelling was conducted for both the AM and PM weekday peak periods, which were determined 
based on the analysis of observed traffic survey data.

Green Travel Plan  
 
A preliminary GTP has been prepared by Ason and is included as part of the Transport Management 
and Accessibility Plan as Appendix 10. The GTP aims to address travel demand and outlines potential 
sustainable travel initiatives for the proposal. Given the site's current industrial and rural location, travel 
patterns are heavily car-dependent, with the majority of workers driving to the area according to 2016 
Census data. The GTP aims to gradually shift this behaviour by setting a preliminary five (5) year target 
to reduce single -occupancy car travel , while increasing bus usage and introducing cycling and 
motorcycle usage. 
 
The GTP outlines potential strategies that can be adopted in achieving future transport targets. There 
are a number of actions identified in the GTP which can be employed to encourage non-car modes of 
transport to and from the site. The plan identified that sustainable transport options will improve over 
time as the precinct develops, particularly with the future Sydney Metro – Western Sydney Airport Line 
(Luddenham Station approximately 4km away) and extended bus services such as the 779 route now 
connecting to St Mary's station.  The GTP would contribute to a healthier and higher quality of life for 
building tenants, staff and visitors whilst reducing air and noise pollution. 
 
7.5 Built Form and Urban Design   
7.5.1 Design Quality   
The proposed development comprises a range of key design moves planned campus designed to 
accommodate critical digital infrastructure while responding to its environmental and urban context.   
 
The campus will include six parcels of four -storey data centre buildings, integrated with six technical 
office buildings and a dedicated campus office building, creating a cohesive and functional 
environment for high-performance operations.  
 
Supporting infrastructure includes incoming and internal electrical substations, associated services, 
and comprehensive site preparation works such as earthworks, stormwater management, sewer 
connections, internal road networks, and landscaping. The design  approach prioritises operational 
efficiency, scalability, and resilience, ensuring the campus can meet future technological demands 
while maintaining a strong commitment to sustainability and environmental stewardship.  
 
Architectural principles focus on functional clarity, modularity, and adaptability, with building forms 
and materials selected to balance technical requirements with visual integration into the surrounding 
landscape.  The proposal also incorporates strateg ies for stormwater management, energy efficiency, 
and biodiversity protection, aligning with planning objectives and best -practice guidelines for large -
scale infrastructure developments.

A full and comprehensive assessment of  MRP DCP is provided at Appendix 33 which demonstrates 
general compliance with relevant controls. It is noted that pursuant to the MRP DCP that a maximum 
building height control of 20m applies to the site. The provisions of this control and justification for the 
exceedance based on the 40m  high buildings (53.5m from the lowest point of the existing ground 
level) is provided below. 
 
TABLE 47: SECTION 4.21 OBJECTIVES - MRP DCP 
Objective Response  
a) To encourage building form that responds 
to the topography of the site and the relative 
position of the allotment to other allotments 
and the street. 
The site, as it presently exists,  is constrained in terms 
of topography due to the level difference from east to 
west.  To accommodate development for data storage 
purposes or general industrial use as per the zone 
objectives and permissible development typologies, it 
is required to substantially cut and fill throughout the 
site to create a suitable platform for development, 
which is proposed under this application.  
 
The built form proposed will be constructed on 
building pads that afford a n elevated position in 
relation to Mamre Road and the future SLR. 
 
Given the topographic constraints of the site, the 
proposal is considered to respond appropriately to 
surrounding allotments and public roads , as the built 
form transitions from east to west.

and the future Stage 2 Mamre Road upgrade.  The 
proposal is therefore not considered to unduly impact 
any views from key public spaces.

landscaping exists within the Luddenham area to the 
west and to the south.  Due to the distance from these 
areas, coupled with the transitioning nature of the 
MRP comprising a range of industrial building 
typologies, there is considered to be no undue impact. 
 
Native vegetation and riparian lands : Native 
vegetation and riparian areas exist throughout the 
MRP, however the views to and from these areas 
would generally remain the same as a compliant 
development.

the surrounding public realm, including 
areas of environmental significance, 
landscape value and residential uses.

realm due to the substantial building setbacks 
achieved to the future SLR  to be delivered by TfNSW  
and Mamre Road  and zoned RE1 public recreation 
land.  Setback distances to the future SLR range 
between 100m to 199m to the main data centre 
buildings, while the setback to Mamre Road is up to 
116m.  
 
Areas of environmental significance, being South 
Creek and Ropes Creek are in excess of 1.2km from the 
site boundaries. 
 
Setbacks, combined with existing and future built 
form, established and proposed landscaping 
ameliorate the impact of the proposed built form 
despite the exceedance of the 20m limit and create a 
desirable precedent for future development.

7.5.3 Better Placed   
The proposal achieves good design in accordance with the seven (7) objectives for good design in Better 
Placed, as addressed below and documented in the Design Report prepared by Greenbox Architecture 
at Appendix 5 of this EIS. 
 
Objective 1 - Better Fit - Contextual, local and of its place 
 
The proposal aligns with the Better Placed Policy under the ‘Better fit’ objective, as it presents a unique 
opportunity to deliver a high -quality data centre within the MRP, an area designated for significant 
industrial uses and large -scale development. Th e proposal represents a logical and appropriate 
outcome that will generate employment opportunities and support economic growth. It is consistent 
with key strategic and statutory planning frameworks, responding sensitively to its location, context, 
and social setting. Through considered design strategies, the  proposed development integrates with 
its surroundings, addresses potential impacts on nearby transitioning sensitive land uses, and 
contributes positively to the evolving local character and emerging data centre landscape in NSW. 
 
Objective 2 - Better Performance - Sustainable, adaptable and durable 
 
The proposal focuses on ‘Better performance’ through sustainability, adaptability, and durability, 
ensuring long- term resilience and minimal environmental impact. The design integrates eco -friendly 
materials and energy -efficient systems to reduce the prop osals environmental footprint and support 
future generations. Designed for flexibility, the proposed development  can evolve with changing 
technologies, market demands, and environmental conditions, ensuring it remains relevant and 
functional over time. The proposal uses high-quality, robust materials that reduce the need for repairs 
and maintenance, offering lasting performance and reliability. The proposal will incorporate several 
Ecologically Sustainable Development (ESD) principles, as detailed within Section 7.13 of this EIS. 
 
Objective 3 - Better for Community - Inclusive, connected and diverse 
 
The proposal meets the Better Placed criteria by addressing technological needs, connectivity, and 
diversity. The proposed data centre is in major cloud availability zones and well -connected 
telecommunications infrastructure. This aids the increasing deman d and adoption of cloud 
technology. The proposed development will generate high -skilled employment opportunities in the 
MRP and improve the day -to-day lives of the community , thereby strengthening the community’s 
economic well-being. 
 
Objective 4 - Better for People - Safe, comfortable and liveable 
 
The proposal aligns with the Better Placed principles, particularly in relation to the ‘Better for people’ 
criteria. The design incorporates both safety and security features throughout the  proposed 
development, with careful attention given to physical and passive surveillance, accessibility, and the 
design of the data centre. Acoustic screening is featured around the plant equipment on the roof to 
reduce and mitigate noise pollution and visual impact. Operating 24/7, the proposal ensures constant 
activity on-site, fostering a safe and secure environment. Landscaped setbacks and the street interface 
have been thoughtfully designed to activate the precinct and create pedestrian-friendly connections. 
 
Objective 5 - Better Working - Functional, efficient and fit for purpose 
 
The proposal aligns with the Better Placed principles in regard to  the ‘Better working’ criteria. The 
proposed development promotes efficient use of the site and maximise the potential output of a 
hyperscale data centre. It is designed as a state -of-the-art data storage centre facility incorporating 
campus administration office space, staff canteens, large meeting rooms, data storage halls and plant 
and equipment areas. The building has been thoughtfully designed to align with the Better Working 
principles, prioritising efficiency, functionality, and fitness for purpose. The integration of a  well-
[[page 168]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  162  
designed data centre and carefully planned landscaped setbacks not only enhances operational 
performance but also ensures these spaces meet both current and future needs. 
 
Objective 6 - Better Value - Creating and adding value 
 
The proposed scheme aligns with the Better Placed principles in regards to the ‘Better value’. Our 
society and economy have rapidly embraced digital technology, making data centres essential for 
modern life. From remote work and entertainment to streaming,  social media, online banking, and e-
commerce, these technologies rely on robust data centre infrastructure. The proposed development of 
a new data centre will: 
 
• Provide a secure location for data storage 
• within the area 
• Increase the speed of digital access for data centre users in Sydney and across NSW 
• Enhance support for local innovation and AI development, fostering economic growth and 
ensuring industry remains within Australia. 
 
Objective 7 – Better Look and Feel 
 
The proposal aligns with the Better Placed principles regarding the ‘Better look and feel’. The proposal 
carefully balances both functionality and aesthetic appeal throughout the design process. It integrates 
seamlessly with its surrounding environment, bl ending into the urban context. The activation of the 
facade along Mamre Rd and Bakers Lane creates an engaging streetscape, enhancing the overall look 
and feel of the proposed development. Landscaped setback areas further enrich the public domain, 
adding to the visual appeal of the urban space. By incorporating attractive architectural forms, along 
with landscaped areas and green spaces, the design enhances the aesthetic quality of the environment, 
creating a more inviting and visually appealing atmosphere. 
 
7.5.4 Connecting with Country  
The Designing with Country process has been formally initiated in alignment with relevant statutory 
requirements and cultural heritage obligations. Specialist consultants have been appointed to 
undertake the required assessments and provide recommendations to inform the design and delivery 
of the proposed development.  
 
While the proposed development  is in a preliminary design stage and specific Country -led and 
collaborative design engagement with the First Nations community have not yet been undertaken, 
work with Yerrabingin has begun to surface key aspects specific to exploring a Place in Country 
contextual analysis that will shape the next phases. These insights will be progressively integrated as 
the design develops to ensure the proposed development  reflects, respects, and responds to this 
Country, integrating the tho ughts, ideas, and voices of the First Nations community through 
collaboration in the next stages. The proposed development’s team is committed to embedding the 
outcomes of the Designing with Country engagement into future design development and decision -
making.  
 
This includes ensuring that guidance the First Nations community and Country informs project 
objectives, design principles, and implementation measures, in accordance with planning frameworks 
and cultural responsibilities.

as Appendix 7. The BCA Design Compliance Report provides an assessment of the current design 
proposal against the Deemed-to-Satisfy (DtS) provisions of the BCA.

the relevant accessibility requirements, subject to detailed design. This can be achieved prior to the 
Construction Certificate stage. 
7.6 Visual Impact  
Geoscapes has prepared a Visual Impact Assessment (VIA) for the proposal, which is included in 
Appendix 9.  
7.6.1 Existing Environment    
The site is zoned IN1 General Industry and SP2 Infrastructure pursuant to the Industry and Employment 
SEPP. The surrounding context comprises a patchwork of developing industrial estates, educational 
facilities, and residual rural dwellings. Recent construction along Mamre Road, Aldington Road, and 
Abbotts Road has introduced large, rectilinear warehouse buildin gs that contrast with the remaining 
agricultural lands. 
7.6.2 Assessment of Impacts

as outlined below and in Figure 78.

substantially limit and fragment long -term views toward the site. As the area continues to develop in 
accordance with pla nning objectives for the Western Sydney Aerotropolis, intervening built form is 
anticipated to further screen or obscure views from these receptors. 
 
These viewpoints illustrate the visual character of the emerging industrial interface along Aldington 
Road, demonstrating how topographic features and the ongoing transition from rural to industrial land 
uses combine to reduce visual exposure from this portion of the surrounding road network.

7.7 Hazards and Risk  
A Preliminary Risk and Hazard Analysis (PRHA) has been prepared in support of the proposal and is 
provided at  Appendix 22. The PRHA comprises a preliminary risk screening and preliminary hazard 
analysis. The Preliminary Risk Screening identifies classes and quantities of all dangerous goods to be 
used, stored or produced on site,  while the Preliminary Hazard Analysis  provides a comprehensive 
qualitative risk assessment of all identified hazards associated with these materials.  The Preliminary 
Hazard Analysis has been completed in accordance with the Resilience and Hazards SEPP and 
Applying SEPP 33 - Hazardous and Offensive Development Application Guidelines (SEPP 33).  
 
7.7.1 Assessment of Impacts 
The PRHA identified three (3) primary categories of hazardous materials proposed for the storage on 
site and is provided in TABLE 55.

Diesel fuel storage for the proposal comprises internal bulk storage tanks and associated day tanks. 
Bulk storage is located on the Ground Floor within dedicated tank rooms, with one  (1) room per shell 
containing five (5) parallel -arranged tanks. Fuel from the bulk tanks is transferred via fixed pumping 
infrastructure to day tanks, which supply the generators during operation. The proposal includes 34 -
day tanks per shell distributed within the generator enclosures from Ground Floor to Level 4, noting

Lithium Ion Battery  
 
Battery storage totalling 2,652,000 kg is distributed across Power Train Units (PTUs) on generator 
gantries (30 per shell, 102,000 kg per shell) and in -rack batteries within data halls. All batteries will be 
UL 9540A tested , demonstrating no module -to-module fire propagation. Storage exceeds the 
SafeWork NSW manifest threshold (10,000 kg), requiring notification.  The relevant aspects of the FM 
Global Property Loss Prevention Data Sheet 5 -32 – Data Centres and Related Facilities have bee n 
considered. In particular, this standard has been considered to address the risk associated with the 
lithium-ion batteries with regard to the configuration of the batteries within the server racks and the 
performance of the automatic sprinkler system. 
 
Transformer Oil 
 
The proposal includes 24 high voltage transformers (132/33 kV) within external substations serving the 
six (6) data centre buildings. Transformers contain mineral oil for cooling and insulation purposes, with 
a total site inventory of approximately 1,430,000L. While transformer oil is classified as a Class C2 
combustible liquid (flash point approximately 140°C) and is not subject to SEPP 33 dangerous goods 
thresholds, it is acknowledged that oil-filled transformers present fire hazards under fault condition s. 
All transformers will comply with AS 2067:2008 requirements, including individual bunding, blast walls 
between units, and appropriate separation distances.

7.7.2 Infrastructure Requirements     
An Infrastructure Delivery, Management and Staging Plan has been prepared in support of the proposal 
and is provided at Appendix 21. The Infrastructure Delivery, Management and Staging Plan outlines the 
sequential delivery of key enabling works, specifically focusing on earthworks, roadworks, sewer, water, 
and power infrastructure.  
7.7.3 Existing Water Supply     
Water 
 
There are a number of water services in the vicinity of the site. There is a 100mm diameter ductile iron 
cement lined (DICL) main on the eastern side of Aldington Road, a 200mm diameter DICL main on the 
western side of Mamre Road, and a 300mm diameter DICL  main located at the intersection of Baker 
Lane and Aldington Road.  
 
Sewerage 
 
There is no existing sewer infrastructure located at or adjacent to the site.  
 
Electricity  
 
There is an overhead power line (11kV and 240V) on the southern side of Bakers Lane reserve parallel to 
the site boundary, and multiple existing high voltage cables are located within Bakers Lane and Mamre 
Road.  
7.7.4 Proposed Supply    
Water 
 
[[page 182]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  176  
Local reticulation mains will be installed within the proposed road network. However, due to the large 
water needs of the data centre, an additional trunk supply main will need to be constructed to provide 
bulk water supply to the proposal. This is subject to Sydney Water approval, and at the time the report 
was written, the final route and size of this main had not yet been determined. The work will connect 
to the significant water trunk infrastructure currently in planning or under construction, including trunk 
supply mains along Mamre Road and Bakers Lane (this would be approved via a Section 73 Certificate 
and 138 Road s Act Approval ). The trunk watermain installation along Bakers Lane and Mamre Road 
frontage is identified as part of Phase 1 works, as discussed in Section 7.7.5 below. 
 
Water Demand Scenarios 
 
The water demand for cooling the data halls is entirely governed by the type of heat rejection 
technology adopted, which is in turn largely influenced by the availability of a secure water supply. 
Three operational scenarios have been assessed, reflecting different cooling strategies and their 
dependency on water availability: 
 
The 'Dry' scenario represents a zero -water approach where no water is available for heat rejection 
under any circumstances. In this scenario, water demand is limited solely to facility operations 
(amenities, landscape irrigation) and all heat rejection is managed through ai r-cooled chillers. This 
scenario requires no critical water storage or pumping infrastructure but represents the least energy -
efficient and most operationally constrained option. This scenario would only occur if Sydney Water or 
other bulk water suppliers are unable to provide a secure water supply to the site. 
 
The 'Minimum Water Use at Extreme Ambient Conditions' scenario  assumes minimal water use, 
where water is only utili sed on extreme temperature days, hot days, or when power failure occurs for 
facility operations. Heat rejection continues to rely on air-cooled chillers (not water-based cooling). This 
represents the typical estimated water use for each data centre building, with peak demand of 10.3 ML 
in January and 2 2.4 ML average annual consumption. The sizing of water storage, pipework and 
pumping infrastructure would be based on the average rate of water flow to each piece of equipment. 
 
The 'Wet' scenario  would utili se recycled water available on -site for heat rejection. Heat rejection 
systems and electrical systems are designed to operate with recycled water where available, with 
maintainable critical water storage, pumping and distribution infrastructure. This high-density cooling 
approach uses evaporative or water -cooled chillers and would result in water demand of 253.1 ML 
during peak month (January) and 2,261 ML average use over a year. This scenario will only be utili sed if 
recycled water is available to site. It reduces energy use and GHG emissions, increases density and 
provides more effective land/site utilisation. 24 hours peak water storage is required. 
 
The proposed heat rejection strategy for the Mamre Road Data Centre is Air Cooled Chillers. 
 
Sewerage 
 
Since there is no existing sewer infrastructure at the site, new sewer connections will be required. The 
proposed development will need to connect to Sydney Water's newly constructed infrastructure, 
specifically draining to pumping station 1221 and connecting to the trunk sewer gravity and rising mains 
within Mamre Road. This sewer connection work is scheduled for Pha se 1 of the infrastructure staging 
and will link into the broader regional infrastructure that Sydney Water is building to service the Mamre 
Road Precinct, including the Advance Recycled Water Centre (ARWC). 
 
Electricity  
 
The proposal requires a new bulk power supply arrangement. TransGrid will provide bulk supply from 
the TransGrid West Zone Substation, with high voltage connections to be installed primarily within 
existing and proposed road corridors. Two (2) potential ro utes were investigated with a preferred now 
chosen. The TransGrid lead -in high voltage work is identified as a critical component of Phase 1

7.8 Soils  
An assessment of potential impacts on soil resources and riparian land on or near the site has been 
undertaken as part of the  Acid Sul fate Soils Assessment (Appendix 38) prepared by JBS&G, an 
Earthworks Strategy Report (Appendix 39) prepared by AT&L Engineering and a Salinity Management 
Plan (Appendix 40) prepared by PSM. 
7.8.1 Assessment of Impacts     
 Soil Erosion 
 
While significant earthworks are expected across the site, with cuts up to 21m deep and fills up to 16m 
high, the overall impacts on soil resources are expected to be minimal. This is largely as a result of the 
implementation of appropriate erosion control measures during construction  in addition to  
stormwater systems, surface gradients, and landscaping specifically designed to control surface flows 
and minimise erosion effects. Additionally, the vast majority of the site will be sealed hardstand, with 
appropriate surface runoff collection and disposal systems incorporated into the design. Refer to the 
Erosion and Sediment Control Plan prepared by AT&L Engineering and provided as Appendix 41. 
 
Salinity 
 
The soils on site are classified as "Non-saline" to "Slightly saline" (with four (4) samples classified as "non-
saline" and two (2) as "slightly saline"), with soil electrical conductivity levels less than the upper limit of 
the "A2" exposure classification per Australian Standard AS3600-2018 "Concrete Structures." 
 
Acid Sulfate Soils 
 
There is a low risk of acid sulfate soils being present at the site, noting that the site is not located within 
the area covered by NSW Acid Sulfate Soil Risk Maps, sits at an elevation greater than 40m above sea 
level, is not situated in a coastal estuary  setting, and has underlying geology consisting of Bringelly 
Shale rather than coastal estuary deposits. Based on these factors, it is concluded that management of 
potential acid sulfate soils is not necessary and no further consideration of this issue is required.  
 
Earthworks  
 
As discussed above, the site requires significant earthworks to accommodate the proposal. The western 
portion will generate approximately 77,200m3 of excess material that could be stockpiled or exported, 
while the eastern portion will produce approximately 257,900m3 requiring export.  
 
The earthworks strategy integrates with multiple constraints including the future Southern Link Road 
to the north, Mamre Road upgrade to the west, and internal roads connecting to adjacent 
developments (Yiribana Logistics Estate, Gibb Group, and Frasers Pr operty Industrial sites). External 
roadworks are designed to stringent guidelines for large vehicles and have been adopted as fixed 
constraints for position and levels. 
 
As surrounding development are also large -scale industrial developments, property boundary 
interfaces typically create significant level differences, as each site's governing entry levels are set from 
different locations along the road network. Despite these challenges, the proposed strategy was 
developed through multiple design iterations to optimi se integration while meeting the proposals 
operational requirements. 
 
Retaining wall interfaces with the public domain have been minimised as much as possible, though 
complete elimination is not feasible given site constraints. The differing governing levels of surrounding 
sites make it impossible to eliminate retaining walls in some areas. Extensive retaining walls within and 
[[page 185]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  179  
surrounding the site are necessary to achieve level pads for each data centre parcel while balancing 
cut and fill volumes. 
 
Alternative Options Considered 
 
The bulk earthworks strategy and site grading has been developed in consideration of various site 
constraints and through design development and iteration with the proposed developments architect 
and other specialist consultants. An alternative option whereby the proposed bulk earthworks levels 
would be raised by 1 metre across the site has been considered in further detail. This alternative option 
would achieve close to a balance of cut  to fill across the site. However, this option would also result in 
final building heights 1 metre higher than the adopted design strategy and would result in a greater 
length and area of retaining walls across the site. For these reasons, this alternative option is not 
preferred. 
7.8.2 Management and/ or Mitigation Measures 
The Salinity Management Plan has been prepared to effectively manage site salinity, to minimise the 
effect of the proposal on the salinity processes and to protect the proposal from salinity damage. 
Mitigation measures regarding development components are provided below:  
 
(A) Earthworks: Grade all surfaces to prevent water ponding, establish vegetation cover on 
permanent batters, implement erosion and sediment control measures including fencing, traffic 
control, and stockpile protection. 
(B) Soil Importation: Highly saline or contaminated soils must not be imported to the site. 
(C) Gardens and Landscaped Areas: Select plant species suited to moderate salinity and clayey soils, 
use nutrient-rich non-saline topsoil, and minimize waterlogging through waterwise gardening 
practices, proper irrigation, and deep-rooted perennial species. 
(D) Roads, Footpaths and Hardstand Areas: Grade and maintain surfaces to prevent water ponding, 
design connections to restrict infiltration into underlying soils, install services at time of 
construction, and provide damp-proof course or membrane beneath slabs. 
(E) Drainage: Design stormwater and surface water systems to restrict infiltration and groundwater 
recharge, minimise disturbance to natural drainage patterns, minimi se infrastructure leakage, 
and direct runoff around exposed surfaces and landscaped areas. 
(F) Structural Durability: Design concrete members using A2 exposure classification (AS3600:2018), 
concrete piles using mild classification, and steel piles using non -aggressive classification 
(AS2159:2009). 
7.8.3 Water Management      
A Water and Stormwater Management Plan has been prepared by AT&L Engineering and provided as  
Appendix 37. A Civil Infrastructure Report has been prepared by AT&L Engineering and provided as  
Appendix 21. A Geotechnical Assessment has been prepared by PSM and provided as Appendix 18.  
7.8.4 Existing Environment     
The site is subject to undulating terrain with elevations ranging from 42 .5 m AHD to 85.5 m AHD and 
slopes ranging from 2% to 15%. There is one (1) mapped hydroline traversing the northeastern portion 
from southwest to northeast, and field validation of a first -order unnamed tributary of South Creek 
modified into farm dams connected by overland flow paths. The site comprises six (6) internal 
catchments totalling approximately 52 hectares and receives drainage from two (2) external 
catchments of approximately 17.1 hectares to the east, with existing drainage occurring through natural 
depressions and localised overland flow paths in the absence of formal trunk stormwater infrastructure. 
 
The existing groundwater conditions across the site show water levels varying between RL 58.4m AHD 
and RL 67.7m AHD, with groundwater generally encountered within the bedrock units rather than in

the overlying soil layers. At monitoring location MW01, groundwater was measured at RL 67.7m AHD

(1.2m below surface), which is influenced by the adjacent water dam . The newly installed monitoring 
wells BH101 and BH102 recorded groundwater at RL 60.3m AHD (15.2m below surface) and RL 67.7m 
AHD (10.6m below surface) respectively, both within bedrock units.

Water Demand 
 
There are three (3) primary water demand categories for the proposal are set out below: 
 
▪ Domestic water supply to meet demands typical of IN1 zoned land, including: 
o Amenities within the COSH Building (primarily kitchens and bathrooms). 
o Landscape irrigation.   
▪ Mechanical water supply for cooling towers within the data centre parcels.   
▪ Fire water supply to sprinklers and hydrants.   
 
Water usage quantities are quantified based on the heat rejection strategy adopted.  
 
Water Supply 
 
Based on investigations and ongoing discussions with Sydney Water throughout design development 
for the Summit at Kemps Creek SSDA (SSD-30628110), it has been determined that connection to the 
Oakdale West potable water infrastructure would be utilised to provide water services to the site.  As 
part of the Oakdale West Development, a Local Area Servicing Plan (LASP) was prepared and endorsed 
by Sydney Water that will provide additional trunk water infrastructure for the area.  Included within 
these works is an extension of a 300mm diameter main from Lenore Drive through to Bakers Lane. 
Subject to Sydney Water approval and further modelling, it is proposed to extend this 300mm main 
along Bakers Lane to service the site. The main will be cross - connected to the existing water mains 
and connected to the existing two (2) x 150mm diameter main within Mamre Road. This main will be 
fully reimbursable, subject to Sydney Water approval.  
 
A “third-pipe” reticulated recycled water network will supply non-potable water throughout the MRP.  
Non-potable water will be supplied from two (2) sources: 
 
• Stormwater harvested within precinct-wide wetlands/ponds, to be delivered and operated by 
Sydney Water as part of a regional stormwater management scheme.   
• Recycled water from the planned Upper South Creek Advanced Recycled Water Centre.

Sewage and Wastewater  
 
Refer to Section 7.21 for discussion regarding on-site sewerage management and wastewater. Refer to 
Appendix A of the Water and Stormwater Management Plan (Appendix 37) which includes a copy of 
the Feasibility Letter received from Sydney Water in May 2025. 
 
On-Site Detention (OSD) Capacity 
 
In advance of connection to the regional stormwater scheme infrastructure, the proposal incorporates 
two (2) OSD tanks designed to ensure post -development peak flow rates do not exceed pre -
development rates for storm events between 50% AEP and 1% AEP. The OSD east tank within the 
proposal serves a 27.2  ha contributing catchment with a storage volume of 14,600m³, designed to 
receive peak 1% AEP inflows and restrict outflows. The OSD west tank within the proposal serves a 15.4 
ha contributing catchment with a storage volume of 7,150m³, designed to receive peak 1% AEP inflows 
and to restrict outflows.  It is also proposed that the OSD west tank is temporarily utilised to store 
stormwater for the irrigation of undeveloped areas on site. This interim measure helps reduce the total 
volume of water leaving the site by reusing some of it locally. 
 
Surface and Groundwater Quantity Impacts 
 
The proposal is expected to have low and temporary impacts on groundwater quantity. During 
construction, groundwater will likely be intersected in some areas due to the varied groundwater levels, 
while the final development level will be between RL 51.0m and RL 67.0m, meaning some excavations 
will occur below the water table. However, the volumes of groundwater encountered are expected to 
be low, and any water that is intercepted is expected to drain relatively quickly without requiring water 
licensing requirements, major pumping or dewatering systems.  
 
The proposed stormwater drainage network within the Data Centre Campus has been designed to 
safely convey major and minor flows prior to discharging to neighbouring properties to the north of 
Bakers Lane and west of Mamre Road.  The following criteria have  been adopted for the proposed 
drainage system: 
• Estate road stormwater drainage: 
o Major system (pit and pipe network, overland flow paths and channels): 1% AEP 
o Minor system (pit and pipe network): minimum 5% AEP and increased where required 
to address major system design requirements. 
• Data Centre Campus stormwater drainage: 
o Major and minor system drainage: capture and convey peak flows up to and including 
the 1% AEP design storm event to ensure the safe operation of the Campus and to 
mitigate risks associated with local flooding within the site.   
 
The post-development peak discharge rates will be maintained at or below pre-development rates for 
all assessed storm events and mean annual runoff volumes will remain below MRP DCP 2021 targets. 
Accordingly, the proposal will not adversely impact downstream flooding risk, channel stability or 
waterway geomorphic function, satisfying the stormwater quantity and runoff volume management 
objectives established in the MRP DCP 2021. 
 
Surface and Groundwater Quality Impacts 
 
A comprehensive stormwater treatment train is proposed to mitigate water quality impacts from the 
proposal: 
 
[[page 190]]

Mamre Road Data Centre Campus

• Gross Pollutant Traps (GPTs): positioned upstream of both discharge points to capture litter, 
debris, coarse sediment, oils and greases, with high -flow bypass set at the 4 EY (3 -month ARI) 
peak flow rate. 
• On-Site Detention Tanks: providing water quality treatment through settlement and pollutant 
uptake processes in addition to their primary flow attenuation function.  
• Interim Stormwater Retention with Treatment:  incorporating filtration and UV disinfection 
for water stored and reused through the irrigation system during Phases 1-4. 
 
The Water and Stormwater Management Plan demonstrates this treatment train will achieve 
substantial pollutant load reductions: 
 
▪ 97.2% reduction in total suspended solids 
▪ 95.7% reduction in total phosphorus 
▪ 92.7% reduction in total nitrogen 
▪ 100% reduction in gross pollutants 
 
These reductions result in residual pollutant loads well below the allowable thresholds established in 
the MRP DCP 2021 , satisfying both Option 1 (annual load reduction) and Option 2 (allowable mean 
annual load) targets at both discharge points. 
7.8.6 Management and/ or Mitigation Measures      
Water Use Minimisation  
 
The following water use minimisation principles will apply to the proposal: 
 
▪ Avoid using water where possible, such as sweeping hard surfaces instead of washing them.   
▪ Reduce water use by installing water -efficient appliances and equipment (e.g., toilets, urinals, 
shower heads).   
▪ Reuse water from manufacturing or cooling processes to toilet flushing, landscape irrigation 
and dust suppression. 
▪ Implement best -practice cooling technologies, such as direct -to-chip cooling or immersion 
cooling.   
7.9 Flooding  
A Flood Impact and Risk Assessment has been prepared by AT&L in response to the SEARs, which is 
provided in Appendix 20. 
7.9.1 Existing Environment       
The site experiences shallow sheet flow flooding under existing conditions, with overland flows moving 
across the undeveloped land toward Mamre Road and Bakers Lane. TUFLOW modelling indicates this 
flooding is generally classified as H1 (low) hazard catego ry for storm events up to and including the 
0.2% AEP (1 in 500 year) event. The flooding is entirely from local catchment overland flows rather than 
mainstream waterway flooding. Importantly, the site is located outside the extent of mainstream 
flooding from both Kemps Creek and Wianamatta-South Creek and sits above the Probable Maximum 
Flood (PMF) level for these major waterways. The site is also positioned outside the flood planning area 
defined in the PLEP2010. 
 
Under existing conditions, the site exhibits relatively low flood risk characteristics. The eastern portion 
of the site is more susceptible to inundation during extreme events, with the local catchment PMF 
potentially causing flooding from run -on from exte rnal catchments to the east. However, for more 
frequent events (5% AEP, 1% AEP, and 0.2% AEP), the flood depths remain shallow and hazard levels 
low. The flood behaviour is characterised by rapid response times typical of local catchment flooding, 
[[page 191]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  185  
with water flowing across the site's gradual topography before discharging toward existing road 
infrastructure. Recent developments in the surrounding area have altered local drainage patterns, 
though the site itself remains in its undeveloped state with natural surface characteristics. 
 
 
Figure 97. Site Flood Affectation  
Source: AT&L 2025 
7.9.2 Assessment of Impacts        
The proposed data centre buildings have minimum finished floor levels set at 500mm above the 1% 
AEP flood level, providing appropriate freeboard and ensuring buildings remain flood -free during 
design storm events. The site does experience local overland flooding, with shallow sheet flows toward 
Mamre Road and Bakers Lane generally classified as H1 (low hazard) category. TUFLOW modelling 
demonstrates that underdeveloped conditions, overland flows up to and including the 0.2% AEP will 
be conveyed through the site without interacting with any built form, and peak flood extents will be 
generally less than existing conditions outside the site, indicating negligible adverse impacts on 
downstream flood levels. The proposal incorporates comprehensive stormwater mana gement 
infrastructure including on-site detention basins, naturalised trunk drainage channels, and enhanced 
drainage networks that ensure post -development flow rates will be equal to or less than pre -
development rates for the 50% to 1% AEP events. The enha nced infrastructure also reduces 
downstream flow velocities, provides water quality treatment, and offers ecological benefits through 
vegetated drainage channels, while fully complying with requirements that flood levels not increase 
by more than 10mm on surrounding properties and flows not be redistributed by more than 15%. 
 
The potential impacts of climate change on flood behaviour have been comprehensively assessed 
through modelling of the 0.2% AEP design event as a proxy for climate change -induced increases in 
rainfall intensity. The 0.2% AEP design rainfall intensities are  approximately 20-25% greater than the 
1% AEP design intensities, representing a reasonable projection of future rainfall increases under 
climate change scenarios . At the same time, sea level rise is not relevant for this inland location. The 
modelling demonstrates that the proposal can accommodate flows up to the 0.2% AEP event without 

[[page 192]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  186  
inundating any built form, with all buildings remaining flood -free even under increased rainfall 
intensity scenarios. The design approach of setting finished floor levels at 500mm above the 1% AEP 
flood level provides sufficient resilience to accommodate p otential climate change impacts 
throughout the operational life of the proposed development, while the comprehensive stormwater 
management infrastructure has been designed with the capacity to manage increased flow volumes 
associated with more intense future rainfall events. 
 
Localised flood level increases occur in the drainage channel north of Bakers Lane , affecting portions 
of 61-109 Bakers Lane, though this land is zoned IN1 General Industrial and allocated for future regional 
stormwater infrastructure and likely redevelopment with improved drainage. The proposal forms part 
of broader Mamre Road Precinct transformation including the Yiribana Logistics Estate, planned 
Mamre Road upgrade, and future Yiribana West development that will cumulatively enhance surface 
water conveyance throughout the precinct, representing a net improvement in flood risk management 
while fully complying with all flood planning controls and maintaining acceptable risks to the 
proposed development and surrounding properties under current and future climate conditions. 
7.9.3 Management and/ or Mitigation Measures        
The eastern portion of the site (Parcels E and F) is within the flood fringe category of the local catchment 
PMF event, and due to rapid local catchment response times, sheltering in place within the four (4) 
storey data centre buildings or within the three (3) storey COSH Building, all with second storeys located 
above the PMF level, is more practical than evacuation during extreme events. A Flood Emergency 
Response Plan will be prepared prior to occupation to address warning times, isolation periods, and 
evacuation procedures, while all critical infrastructure , including diesel fuel storage , will be located 
above the 1% AEP flood level in bunded tanks. 
7.10 Contamination and Remediation   
A DSI has been carried out on the site by JBS&G and is provided at Appendix 23. The recommendations 
of the DSI include that a Remedial Action Plan (RAP) be prepared due to identified contamination and 
to close out data gaps. 
 
A RAP has been prepared in support of the proposal and is provided at Appendix 24. 
 
The RAP has been developed to address the contamination identified as part of the DSI, with 
consideration of the future proposed land use. The specific objectives of this RAP are to: 
 
▪ Characterise and document the known extent of environmental/aesthetic impact within the 
site via presentation of a conceptual site model (CSM);  
▪ Identify the remedial strategy(ies)/framework to be adopted by an assessment of remedial 
options and development objectives; and 
▪ Document the procedures and standards to be followed in order to remove the risks posed by 
contaminated soils and aesthetic impacts to make the site suitable for the proposed 
commercial/industrial land use, while ensuring the protection of human health and  the 
surrounding environment. 
 
Based on the assessment provided in the DSI, and taking into account the data gap s identified in the 
DSI surrounding existing sewerage infrastructure and underlying the manmade bore which needs to 
occur during remedial works , the site remediation areas have been defined within the RA P. The 
remediation areas have been identified below: 
 
▪ AEC 1: Friable asbestos (AF/FA) impacted fill material at location RZ10 in the residential area of 
the property exceeding the adopted health criteria; 
▪ AEC 2: Bonded Asbestos Containing Material (ACM) impacted fill material: 
– fill within the residential zone at the site surface and to a depth of 0.3 m;

7.10.1 Management and/ or Mitigation Measures        
Subject to the successful implementation of the measures described in the RAP and the 
recommendations below, it is concluded that the site can be made suitable for the intended uses and 
that the risks posed by contamination can be managed in such a way as to be adequately protective 
of human health and the environment. 
 
The processes outlined in the  RAP will be implemented and the following documentation be 
developed and implemented to ensure the risks and impacts during remediation works are controlled 
in an appropriate manner: 
 
▪ A REMP (Remediation Environmental Management Plan) , to document the monitoring and 
management measures required to control the environmental impacts of the works and 
ensure the validation protocols are being addressed; and 
▪ A WHSP (Work Health and Safety Plan) to document the procedures to be followed to manage 
the risks posed to the health of the remediation workforce. 
7.11 Trees and Landscaping   
An Arboricultural Impact Assessment (AIA) has been prepared by Creative Planning Solutions (CPS) 
and is attached at Appendix 12. A summary is provided in the following sections.  
7.11.1 Existing Environment         
A total of 215 trees and groups of trees were observed within and adjoining the site. Of those trees 
assessed 212 trees and groups of trees fell within the boundaries of the site. The remaining three (3) 
trees were observed outside the site boundaries and within the neighbouring allotment to the east at 
1-22 Bakers Lane.  
 
The site presents as a greenfield allotment with highly disturbed conditions following localised 
demolition of built structures from its former use.  
7.11.2 Assessment of Impacts         
The assessed trees comprised predominantly locally indigenous species, along with other Australian 
native and exotic species. Many of the trees are species consistent with the Critically Endangered 
Ecological Community (CEEC): Cumberland Plain Woodland in the Sydney Basin Bioregion. 
 
A total of 215 trees (within the site and adjoining) are proposed to be removed due to project impacts. 
This comprises: 
 
▪ 156 trees and groups of trees due to falling directly within the footprint of the proposed bulk 
earthworks.

respective Notional Root Zones (18-35%) and Structural Root Zones (0-15%).

A Tree Management and Protection Plan is not applicable to this development application as no trees

removed to facilitate the proposed development.  
7.12 Climate Change   
A Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Assessment Report has been prepared by E-LAB Consulting in support of the 
proposal and is provided in Appendix 36 . The GHG Assessment Report has been prepared in 
accordance with EPA’s NSE Guide for Large Emitter s, the National Greenhouse and Energy Reporting 
(NGER) Measurement Determination, and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) 
2006 Guidelines 
7.12.1 GHG Emissions Assessment         
The assessment boundary for the GHG Assessment Report considers all  major operational activities 
across the campus  excluding particular minor activities . Emissions sources include direct (Scope 1) 
emissions from on-site back up fuel use, indirect (Scope 2) emissions from purchased electricity, and 
indirect (Scope 3) emissions from supply chains, waste, and employee travel. The emissions assessment 
establishes a comprehensive quantification of the proposal’s GHG emissions across S copes 1, 2, and 3 
throughout the operational lifecycle, providing the evidence base required to demonstrate compliance 
with NSW large emitter guidelines and applicable legislative frameworks. The assessment forms the 
foundation for identifying targeted mitigation measures and offset requirements to support the 
proposal's net zero commitments in alignment with national and state climate policy objectives.

important to note that these emissions have been identified for the peak emissions year  which as per 
staging plans and assumptions, this is expected to be 2032. Additionally, cumulative total emissions 
have been identified below.

7.12.2 Offset Strategy  
To achieve net zero commitments, the below offset strategy is proposed to be implemented: 
 
▪ Residual Emissions and Offset Requirements : Despite best -practice design, efficiency 
measures, and renewable electricity procurement, residual greenhouse gas emissions will 
remain primarily from backup diesel generation, fugitive refrigerants, and embodied carbon in 
construction materials. Prelimi nary modelling indicates residual Scope 1 emissions requiring 
offsetting are approximately 1,623.0 tCO₂-e/year during peak operations at maximum capacity, 
with cumulative requirements estimated at 79,091 tCO₂-e, over the proposed development’s 
operational life. 
 
▪ Offset Selection and Integrity Principles : The proposed development  will prioritise high -
quality Australian Carbon Credit Units (ACCUs) that deliver measurable environmental 
outcomes and co-benefits to local communities, including native forest regeneration projects 
in NSW and Indigenous fire management projects in the No rthern Territory. Where practical, 
international Verified Carbon Units (VCUs) from Asia -Pacific conservation projects may 
supplement the strategy. All offsets comply with the Commonwealth Carbon Credits Act 2011 
integrity requirements, ensuring credits are  real, additional, permanent, unique, transparent, 
verifiable, and conservatively accounted for. 
 
▪ Implementation Framework: The offset strategy integrates into the Principal’s broader Net 
Zero Carbon Strategy targeting residual Scope 1, including the following:  
o Integration: Applying offsets only to unavoidable residual emissions, including diesel 
back-up generation where renewable diesel is not utilised. 
o Offset Selection & Due Diligence:  All offsets are sourced from accredited schemes 
with priority for NSW -based ACCUs, then ACCUs and then VCUs. Evaluation criteria 
include permanence and verifiable emission reductions, additionality beyond 
business-as-usual scenarios, social and biodiversit y co -benefits, and alignment with 
the Principal's broader regional sustainability priorities. 
o Procurement and Certification: Carbon credits are procured through certified 
registries and reputable intermediaries ensuring  transparent ownership and 
traceability. Credit retirement is undertaken annually to offset verified emissions. 
 
The GHG Assessment Report has identified mitigation and offset strategies to support and actively 
combat GHG emissions  from the construction and operation of the proposed development . This 
approach ensures credibility, transparency, and alignment with national and state climate policies 
while supporting biodiversity protection and regional economic development.

7.13 Ecologically Sustainable Development    
An Ecologically Sustainable Development (ESD) Report has been prepared by E -Lab Consulting  in 
support of the proposal and is provided at Appendix 13. The ESD Report presents a summary of the 
ESD strategies proposed and commitments made for the proposed development. The Proponent 
intends to deliver a sustainable outcome for the proposed development by demonstrating a strong 
commitment to sustainability through design, construction, and operation. 
 
The ESD Report addresses the following matters as required by the SEARs for the proposal: 
 
▪ Identification of how ESD principles (as defined in section 193 of the EP&A Regulation) are 
incorporated in the design, construction and ongoing operation of the proposed development.  
▪ Demonstration of how the proposed development will meet or exceed the relevant industry 
recognised building sustainability and environmental performance standards, including a 
maximum Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) of 1.3 and a minimum NABERS rating of five stars.  
▪ Identification of the projected Water Usage Effectiveness (WUE) for the proposed 
development, compared against recognised ‘best practice’ benchmarks.  
▪ Demonstration of how the proposed development minimises greenhouse gas emissions 
(reflecting the Government’s goal of net zero emissions by 2050) and consumption of energy, 
water (including water sensitive urban design) and material resources.  
▪ If Chapter 3 of State Environmental Planning Policy (Sustainable Buildings) 2022  applies, 
include:  
o Demonstration as to how the proposed development has been designed to address 
the provisions set out in in Chapter 3.2(1)  
o A NABERS Embodied Emissions Material Form to disclose the amount of embodied 
emissions attributable to the proposed development in accordance with section 35BA 
of the EP&A Regulation.

7.13.2 ESD Design Initiatives   
ESD design initiatives for the proposal include: 
 
Energy 
 
The design in the first instance must seek to remove the demand for energy consumption where 
possible through reducing the load. Beyond this, energy consumption can be made more conservative, 
through efficient lighting, mechanical systems, and other associated active services. Once the energy 
system has reduced all possible energy -consuming elements and made the remaining systems as 
efficient as possible, renewable energy sources will be prioriti sed through both on -site and off -site 
generation. Only after a ll the above steps have been implemented should carbon offsets be used to 
achieve operational neutrality 
 
The following initiatives are to be implemented across the proposed development: 
 
▪ Detailed Modelling – Energy modelling of the proposed development will be undertaken early 
during subsequent design stages to understand the opportunities and identify risks to overall 
energy performance and compliance with identified performance requireme nts. This will be 
used to inform and coordinate the wider project team to best align systems to reduce energy 
consumption as applicable.  
 
▪ Renewable Energy – The pro posal will implement a Renewable Electricity Procurement 
Strategy as part of the proposed development. This strategy forms a key component of the 
Proponent’s broader commitment to achieve Net Zero (Scope 1 and 2) greenhouse gas 
emissions by 2030 and will be applied throughout the design, delivery, and operation of the 
proposed development . Under this strategy, the pro posal will progressively match all 
operational electricity consumption with renewable energy through a combination o f long-
TABLE 63: ESD PRINCIPLES 
Philosophy Project Response 
generations have equal opportunity and access to 
the environment. 
Conservation of Biological Diversity and Ecological Integrity: 
Conservation of biological diversity and 
ecological integrity should be a fundamental 
consideration 
The proposal is located largely within bio-certified 
land under the Cumberland Plain Conservation 
Plan to minimise biodiversity impacts. All residual 
impacts from the removal of 1.36 hectares of 
native vegetation will be offset through 
retirement of 84 biodiversity credits, achieving 'No 
Net Loss' under the Biodiversity Conservation Act 
2016. 
Improved Valuation, Pricing and Incentive Mechanisms: 
Environmental factors should be included in the 
valuation of assets and services. The users of 
goods and services should pay prices based on 
the full life cycle costs of providing goods and 
service. 
The proposal is committed to minimising waste 
throughout development and operation by 
improving circularity and diversion rates. This will 
be achieved through data collection, identifying 
reuse opportunities, and exploring take -back 
programs with equipment suppliers to  enable 
second-life use of equipment. These actions will 
help conserve resources, support decarbonisation, 
and reduce environmental impact.
