Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  194  
term Power Purchase Agreements (PPAs), renewable energy certificates (RECs), and 
collaborative renewable energy procurement with tenants. The Pr oponent’s current 
operational portfolio already achieves a 74% renewable electricity matching rate, providing a 
strong foundation for extending these measures to new developments. A Tenant Renewable 
Electricity Uptake Program will also be implemented to facilitate customer participation in 
renewable energy sourcing. This program will include mechanisms to verify renewable energy 
procurement, provide contractual  flexibility to enable tenant -led renewable energy 
purchasing, and support joint renewable energy projects where feasible. 
 
▪ Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) - Lead the industry with best -in-class operational Power 
Usage Effectiveness (PUE) and 100% renewable energy matching at all sites by 2030, 
supporting the transition towards 24/7 clean energy. Continue to drive down PUE towards a 
long-term goal through innovation projects and deployment o f novel technologies such as 
liquid cooling. Continual usage of optimisation programs will aid in the reduction of energy 
draw. Use of effective lighting controls and strategies in conjunction with low consuming 
lighting will contribute to the PUE.  
 
▪ Mechanical HVAC – The heating, cooling and ventilation strategy for the campus will be 
designed to maximise energy efficiency. Operating schedules and control of the various spaces 
served will be designed to optimi se efficiency by matching occupational profiles to ensure 
HVAC is not running unnecessarily and increasing energy demand. The data centre campus 
cooling system is supported by a high-reliability cooling system, comprising multiple closed -
circuit cooling towers per building or air -cooled chillers, and dynamic pressure units with 
process water make-up and expansion tanks, integrated with condenser and evaporator loops. 
This configuration provides redundancy and operational flexibility, ensuring precise 
temperature control and uninterrupted performanc e of critical infrastructure. The system 
demonstrates energy-efficient operation through staged deployment of cooling towers  and 
aligning equipment operation with real -time cooling demand. Closed -circuit cooling towers 
minimise water loss and mitigate foul ing, maintaining optimal heat transfer efficiency. 
Dynamic pressure regulation optimises pumping energy, while air -cooled chillers provide 
supplemental cooling under controlled energy conditions. Furthermore, the adoption of 
water-efficient technologies, with integration of rainwater harvesting to supply the mechanical 
cooling system reduces reliance on potable water and enhancing overall environmental 
performance.  
 
▪ Commissioning and Tuning – To ensure optimal operation of building services, commissioning 
to a recognised industry standard shall be undertaken during construction and before practical 
completion.  
 
▪ Equipment – Evaluated all strategic equipment suppliers through the Supplier Relationship 
Management (SRM) program using ESG criteria. Partner with contractors and suppliers to cut 
embodied carbon by replacing high -carbon materials and optimising supply ch ains, 
manufacturing, and transport. Promote circularity by designing data centres to minimise 
waste, use recycled and low -carbon materials, enable take -back programs, and ensure 
equipment longevity and recyclability, building more resilient and sustainable infrastructure.  
 
▪ Low Emission Transportation – Monitoring business travel, employee commuting, and supplier 
transportation will guide the proposed development to reduce any additional emissions. These 
activities form a key part of the embodied carbon reduction strategy  for the proposed 
development, helping identify opportunities to cut emissions and drive reductions across every 
stage of the value chain. 
 
Water 
 
[[page 201]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  195  
To achieve water resilience, the proposed development is committed to minimise water withdrawal, 
optimise water productivity through the deployment of measures to increase efficiency and enhance 
WUE, reducing energy use and carbon emissions. WUE for the proposed development will be identified 
and calculated, which will be compared against the benchmark for best practice. The client’s portfolio 
has achieved a WUE of 0.89 L/kWh with 100% of campuses meeting design-defined limits. The Mamre 
Road Data Centre Campus is expected to achieve a better WUE performance of 0.1 L/kWh by deploying 
a series of measures. 
 
▪ Water Stress – Using water for cooling increasingly dense computing deployments has the 
potential to save energy and reduce carbon emissions. To make sure water resilience is 
achieved, water in water stressed areas is monitored, measured by WSL.  
 
▪ Recycled Water – The proposed development is committed to water recycling initiatives to 
supply the proposal or replenish local basins. As well as working with authorities and utilities 
to develop new sources and pushing to be an off -taker of existing recycled water schemes, 
reducing reliance on freshwater. Rainwater and recycled water infrastructure will be int egral, 
with potential to supply at least 80% of non -potable demand, initially with rainwater tanks 
until recycled water schemes are operational.  
 
▪ Optimise water usage – Deployed cooling solutions that maximi se the use of free air cooling 
while maintaining optimal PUE performance. The proposed development  will consistently 
track and report the WUE as a key metric to ensure responsible water usage, a commitment 
reflected in the SLL targets.  
 
▪ Integrated Water Cycle Management – An integrated water cycle management strategy will 
ensure sustainable water use and protection of the South Creek catchment. The strict 
stormwater quality targets will be met, including a 90% reduction in total suspended  solids 
and gross pollutants, 80% reduction in phosphorus, and 65% reduction in nitrogen, with flow 
targets enforced during both construction and operation. The strategy will incorporate MUSIC 
modelling, lifecycle costing, and maintenance plans.  
 
▪ Water Metering and Monitoring –The ratio of wastewater to water that comes into site is 
estimated or metered by the local water authority. OSD tanks (eastern and western) will be 
provided in the design of the campus with initial studies considering wastewater discharge 
from cooling plant will be minimal, reducing concerns on both volume and pollutants. This 
includes Gross pollutant traps upstream of discharge points will capture litter, debris, coarse 
sediment, oils and greases before stormwater enters the estate-wide drainage system. 
Furthermore, a Interim stormwater retention systems will include automated irrigation 
controls with flow meters, moisture sensors and weather stations to monitor and manage 
water reuse across the site. 
 
▪ Fire Protection – Reticulation of fire system test water will be investigated to minimise 
additional water loss. 
7.13.3 Power Usage Effectiveness    
The requirement of the MRP DCP states the building is only to be designed to meet a 4.5-star NABERS 
energy rating. However, in particular the SEARs requirements call for a PUE of 1.3 and a minimum 5 -
star NABERS energy. This will be achieved through working with the tenants when available to ensure 
the highest efficiency. Current  site managed by the operator achieves an average PUE of 1.32, which 
aligns with a NABERS rating of 5 Star NABERS Energy. This will be aimed to be achieved above and 
beyond to meet the PUE requirement of 1.3 and the minimum 5-star requirement.

7.13.4 Water Usage Effectiveness    
To optimise water consumption reduction, the following preliminary water consumption estimates 
have been conducted for the proposed development to inform the design. The methodology for these 
estimates rely on the client’s estimated WUE of 0.1 L/kWh, which  is an improvement on the portfolio 
wide WUE of 0.97 L/kWh. 
 
As the methodology of this water consumption estimate is based on WUE of existing similar projects, 
it is necessary that the consumption of electricity use for the proposed development is estimated. This 
has been conducted as part of the GHG emissions assessment which has been undertaken in line with 
the NSW Guide for Large Emitters, the National Greenhouse and Energy Reporting (NGER) 
Measurement Determination, and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) 2006 
Guidelines. 
 
Underlying assumptions include: 
 
▪ Peak IT load of ~40MW per building within the campus. With 26 shells it is expected that the 
site has a total capacity of ~1.2GW (1200 MW) with an IT load of ~1.04GW  
 
▪ Annual operational load factor is varied as per historical tenant utilisation. Provided by the 
Client the following identifies the load factor per shell once operational: End of Year 1 – 50% - 
End of Year 2 – 75% - End of Year 3 and onwards  
 
▪ 90% ▪ Diesel back -up generators: 846 total generators for the proposed development. 
Estimated testing of all generators once annually with 60 minutes per test and 6 generators 
tested simultaneously. Projected 18,360 litres per shell per year.  
 
▪ Assumed power usage efficiency (PUE) of 1.3 as per SEARs requirements and the principal’s 
average PUE reporting of portfolio which includes similar projects.

7.13.5 State Environmental Planning Policy (Sustainable Buildings) 2022    
All new non -residential developments must report on embodied emissions, where the NSW 
government's Sustainable Buildings SEPP applies. Chapter 3 – Standards for non -residential 
development of the Sustainable Buildings SEPP applies to the proposal.  
 
Clause 3.2 - Development consent for non -residential development of the SEPP prescribes the 
following: 
 
▪ In deciding whether to grant development consent to non -residential development, the 
consent authority must consider whether the development is designed to enable the 
following— 
o the minimisation of waste from associated demolition and construction, including 
by the choice and reuse of building materials, 
o a reduction in peak demand for electricity, including through the use of energy 
efficient technology,

706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  197  
o a reduction in the reliance on artificial lighting and mechanical heating and cooling 
through passive design, 
o the generation and storage of renewable energy, 
o the metering and monitoring of energy consumption, 
o the minimisation of the consumption of potable water. 
▪ Development consent must not be granted to non -residential development unless the 
consent authority is satisfied the embodied emissions attributable to the development have 
been quantified. 
 
The ESD Report prepared in support of the proposal includes a number of target initiatives in relation 
to minimising waste, reduction in electricity, natural light and ventilation, storage and generation of 
renewable energy, extensive metering, and reduction in water use as described in Section 7.13.4 above. 
The proposal is considered to be designed in a manner that is consistent with the requirements of 
Clause 3.2(1).  
 
It is noted that pursuant to Clause 3.2(2) of the Sustainable Buildings SEPP, proponents for non -
residential development types must complete the NABERS Embodied Emissions Material Form, 
including calculation of material quantities (and later embodied emissions) at the proposed 
development application stage, and again at the construction certificate stage. The embodied 
emissions attributed to the proposal are provided in the NABERS Embodied Emissions Material Form 
provided in Appendix 14.  
7.14 Aboriginal Cultural Heritage    
An Aboriginal Cultural Heritage Assessment Report (ACHAR) (Appendix 26) and an Addendum ACHAR 
(Appendix 27) have been prepared by Biosis to inform the proposed development . Noting SSD-
30628110 on the site, which is currently under assessment, the requirement for an addendum ACHAR 
was established following discussions with Heritage NSW, Department of Climate Change, Energy, the 
Environment and Water (Heritage NSW) o n 2 September 2025 and 2 October 2025. Heritage NSW 
advised that an addendum report in the for m of a letter was sufficient to satisfy SSD-92743706 
submission requirements and provided the following advice: 
 
o No requirement to restart the consultation process outlined in the Aboriginal Cultural 
Heritage. 
o Consultation Requirements for Proponents 2010 (DECCW 2010) (consultation requirements). 
o Use the existing Registered Aboriginal Parties (RAPs) associated with the original ACHA R 
and send the addendum ACHAR to this group. 
o The addendum report would serve as an update to the RAPs, which would detail the 
proposal and recommendations. 
o The addendum report would be sent out to RAPs for a 28 -day consultation period, prior to 
being submitted as part of the SSD application.  
 
Heritage NSW was supportive of this approach and confirmed the use of existing RAPs for the SSD  
application. As a result of this consultation with Heritage NSW, Biosis has prepared an addendum to 
the ACHAR to assess the new proposed works under SSD-92743706. 
7.14.1 Existing Environment     
An Aboriginal Heritage Information Management System (AHIMS) register search conducted on 3 June 
2021 identified 102 Aboriginal archaeological sites within a 1.7km by 1.7km area centred on the site. Of 
these, nine (9) sites were recorded as being located within the study area itself. However, background 
research revealed that two (2) of these sites are located approximately 1km north of the site and have 
already been included in a Consent to Destroy Permit (#2188). 
 
[[page 204]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  198  
An archaeological survey was conducted on 11 June 2021, though its effectiveness was significantly 
limited by poor ground surface visibility due to extensive grass coverage, which hindered the 
identification of Aboriginal sites on the surface. During the survey, the locations of previously identified 
AHIMS sites were visited to confirm their current condition, but the artefacts recorded in the site cards 
could not be located due to the grass coverage. The inspection of the two (2) AHIMS sites described 
above confirmed that the GPS locations in the AHIMS database did not match the site descriptions in 
the site cards or associated reports. While no Aboriginal sites were identified during the survey, areas 
of minimal disturbance were assessed as containing Potential Archaeological Deposit (PAD). 
 
Based on the survey results, a test excavation program was undertaken from 5  to 29 October 2021 to 
characterise the extent, nature, and archaeological value of Aboriginal cultural heritage within the 
identified Aboriginal sites and areas of PAD. This archaeological assessment identified an additional 
five (5) Aboriginal sites within the site.

7.14.2 Assessment of Impacts  
The identification and assessment of cultural heritage values include the four (4) values of the Burra 
Charter: social, historical, scientific and aesthetic values.  
 
Historical Significance 
 
The historical significance refers to historic values and encompasses the history of aesthetics, science 
and society, and therefore to a large extent underlies all of the terms set out in this section. The site is 
not known to have any historic associations. 
 
Social Significance 
 

[[page 205]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  199  
The social significance refers to the spiritual, traditional, historical or contemporary associations and 
attachment that the place or area has for the present -day community.  During consultation limited 
information was provided by RAPs in regards to the cultural values of the site, it was however indicated 
that the site has cultural significant to the RAPs. 
 
Scientific Significance 
 
The scientific significance refers to the importance of a landscape, area, place or object because of its  
archaeological and/or other technical aspects. An archaeological scientific assessment was undertaken 
for the site which identified four (4) sites that had been disturbed by the creation of a dam. The 10 
others were relatively undisturbed with the only disturbance in the past 50 years in those areas 
identified as land clearing and cattle grazing. Five (5) sites contained sub-surface artefact scatters while 
the other nine were surface artefacts or scatters. It was determined that four sites have low scientif ic 
significance and nine have moderate scientific significance. 
 
Aesthetic Significance 
 
The aesthetic significance refers to the sensory, scenic, architectural and creative aspects of the place. 
The site has been disturbed through land clearance of remnant native vegetation. However, the overall 
landscape is a typical example of sloping landforms of the Bringelly Shale geological unit. The 
landscape of the site is linked with Aboriginal cultural values and provides context for Aboriginal sites 
that gives a strong sense of place. The vegetation clearance and other development surrounding the 
site indicates that the aesthetic values have been altered from the original landscape before European 
settlement and therefore has moderate aesthetic values. 
 
The current proposed works within the site include activities which could impact Aboriginal heritage 
sites or objects. If not mitigated the impact may include: 
 
o Bulk earthworks of the entire site, which will involve the removal of topsoil and subsoil. 
o Construction of warehouses and associated services and infrastructure, which would involve 
subsurface excavations for foundations and services. 
 
Left unmitigated, these activities have potential to completely remove or disturb archaeological 
deposits and Aboriginal objects. A summary of potential archaeological impact has been undertaken 
in TABLE 65 below.

7.14.3 Consultation 
Consultation with the Aboriginal community was undertaken by Biosis in accordance with the 
Aboriginal Cultural Heritage Consultation Requirements for Proponents 2010  (DECCW 2010a) 
(consultation requirements).  The appropriate government bodies were notified and advertisements 
placed in the Western Weekender newspaper (4 June 2021). 
 
A search conducted by the National Native Title Tribunal (NNTT) listed no Registered Native Title Claims, 
Unregistered Claimant Applications or Registered Indigenous Land Use Agreements within the site. 
The Office of the Registrar, Aboriginal Land Rights Act 1983 did not provide a response. Heritage NSW, 
Department of Premier and Cabinet (Heritage NSW) provided a list of Aboriginal stakeholders for the 
region, which were invited to register an interest in the proposed development. 
 
Upon registration, the registered Aboriginal parties (RAPs) were invited to provide their knowledge on 
the site and on the proposal provided in Methodology. The responses identify the site as significant due 
to the continued occupation and the complex sites located within close proximity. Responses from the 
RAPs are included in Appendix 32. 
 
The outcome of the consultation process was that the RAPs considered the site to have a high level of 
cultural significance, although that significance was not clearly defined. The recommendations that 
resulted from the consultation process have informed the management and mitigation measures 
described in Section 7.14.4 below.  
7.14.4 Management and/ or Mitigation Measures     
Detailed management options and recommendations are included as part of the ACHAR and 
Addendum ACHAR which include: 
 
▪ Develop a Cultural Heritage Management Plan (CHMP)  to be prepared by a suitably qualified 
archaeologist in consultation with the RAPs for the proposed development. 
▪ Arrange community collection  to a llow Aboriginal community members to collect surface 
artefacts from 7 identified AHIMS sites before development begins.  
▪ No further assessment needed . Two (2) AHIMS sites (45 -5-3036 and 45-5-3030) are outside the 
site and marked as destroyed or relocated.  
▪ Establish long -term care agreement  with Aboriginal groups for ongoing care of recovered 
artefacts, potentially returning them to the community.  
[[page 207]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  201  
▪ Continue consulting Aboriginal groups including, informing RAPs throughout the proposal and 
involve them in decisions about artefact management.  
▪ Create Aboriginal interpretation elements  through native gardens, Aboriginal artwork, and 
cultural designs.  
▪ Protocol for unexpected Aboriginal objects  will be employed  in the vicinity and if Aboriginal 
objects are found; qualified archaeologist and potentially Heritage NSW  would be immediately 
contacted.  
▪ Protocol for unexpected historical relics  will be employed in the vicinity  and if historical relics 
are discovered; qualified archaeologist would be contacted and notif ication to the  Heritage 
Council if assessed as significant.  
▪ Protocol for human remains – Stop all work in the vicinity immediately, protect the remains, and 
notify Coroner's Office, NSW Police, Aboriginal parties, and Heritage NSW.

impacts on non -Aboriginal cultural heritage  either on the site or within the vicinity of the site. An 
Archaeological Assessment was also carried out for the site and is provided at Appendix 29.

The Statement of Heritage Impact notes that there are no heritage items or heritage conservation areas 
listed in the Industry and Employment SEPP  or PLEP2010 located on the site  or in the vicinity of the 
site. The site is situated within the broader vicinity of the following heritage listed  items of local 
significance: 
 
▪ The Fleurs Radio Telescope site (Item No. 832), which is approximately 1.7km south-west 
of the site. 
▪ Leeholme Horse Stud Rotunda (Item No. 232), which is approximately 2.3km north-west 
of the site. 
▪ Brick Farmhouse (Item No. I4/104), which is approximately 2.2km south-east of the site. 
▪ Gateposts to Colesbrook (Item No. I3/105), which is approximately 1.7km south of the site. 
▪ Bayley Park, house (Item No. I2/106), which is approximately 1.4km south of the site.

inspection based on background research undertaken for the site. These are set out below:

foundations, footings, building materials, demolition debris and construction debris. 
▪ Residential service pipes: Possible construction date of pre-1947 and the probable features relate 
to ceramic pipes and cut and fill deposits.

7.15.2 Assessment of Impacts      
There are no heritage items or conservation areas within or near the site of the proposal. The nearest 
heritage items are over 1.4 km from the site and are separated by residential and industrial 
developments. There are no known historical associations or current visual connections between the 
site and any heritage item. As such, there are no items of heritage significance within or adjacent to the 
site that will be impacted by the proposal.  
 
From an Archaeological perspective, the archaeological resources associated with the large shed would 
not contribute further to an understanding of building techniques from this time. The building 
techniques and materials, and pastoral and agricultural processes are typical of this region and are 
thoroughly researched and well understood. There were no features of these structures to differentiate 
them from similar structures of this nature built during this period. An assessment of the readily 
available historical information has not identified a strong or special association with the life or works 
of a person or group of persons. The structural remnants of the large shed as observed during the 
physical inspection do not indicate they hold aesthetic or technical significance. While the official date 
of construction of the large shed cannot be definitively determined, the background research and 
physical inspection has concluded that the buildings were constructed prior to 1947. Given the known 
evidence of th e structure and the wider occupation of the site, the resources of this structure would 
not demonstrate the use of the site. The archaeological resources associated with the large shed do 
not hold heritage significance at a local or State level. 
 
The archaeological resources associated with the residential service pipes would not contribute further 
to our understanding of water and sewer management from this time. The infrastructure and materials 
are typical of this region and are thoroughly researched and well understood. There were no features 
of these structures to differentiate them from similar structures of this nature built during this period. 
An assessment of the readily available historic al information has not identified a strong or special

706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  203  
association with the life or works of a person or group of persons. The residential service pipes as 
observed during the physical inspections do not indicate they hold aesthetic or technical significance. 
While the official date of construction of the residential service pipes cannot be definitively determined, 
the background research and physical inspection has concluded that the buildings wer e constructed 
prior to 1947. Given the known evidence of the structure and the wider occupation of the site, the 
resources of this structure would not demonstrate the use of the site. The archaeological resources 
associated with the residential service pipes do not hold heritage significance at a local or State level. 
7.15.3 Management and/ or Mitigation Measures     
The Archaeological Assessment recommend s the implementation of an unexpected find protocol. 
Relics are historical archaeological resources of local or State significance and are protected in NSW 
under the Heritage Act. Should unanticipated relics be discovered during the course of the proposal, 
the following steps should be taken: 
 
▪ If a find is identified, works should stop immediately around the find and the area should be 
protected. 
▪ The find should be reported to the project manager or supervisor. 
▪ A historical archaeologist should be contacted to identify the find. 
▪ If the find is a relic, a mitigation strategy must be developed and implemented. This could include 
avoidance of the find, test excavation or other mitigation measures.  
▪ If the find is a relic, notification to Heritage Council under Section 146 of the Heritage Act will be 
required. 
7.16 Biodiversity     
A BDAR has been prepared to inform the proposed development by Biosis and is provided in Appendix 
15 of this EIS. 
7.16.1 Existing Environment      
Native vegetation on the site is largely in fragmented patches, occurring as scattered trees over native 
grassland, with minimal shrubs in the mid -storey. The site appears to have been subject to historical 
clearing and currently features large areas of non -native grassland and exotic shrubs and tree species 
that have been planted around the site of a dilapidated human -made housing structure  which was 
demolished during remediation works in September 2025. The site includes three (3) dams, one (1) 
large and two (2) small that have native fringing vegetation around the edges. The large dam in the 
east of the area of non-certified land connects to an unnamed non-perennial watercourse to the south 
that runs through the proposed development site to the east. 
 
The site occurs within the Cumberland Plain (SB Cumberland) and Sydney Basin Diatremes (SB 
Volcanics) Mitchell Landscapes, with the former covering the majority of the site. The occurrence of 
Sydney Basin Diatremes is restricted to an area surrounding a dam/wetland along an unnamed Strahler 
Order 1 system within the site.  
 
[[page 210]]

Non-Native Vegetation 
 
A total of 5.36 ha of the site is mapped as exotic grasses, shrubs or trees that are not native and therefore 
meet the definition of non-native vegetation 
 
Plant Community Types (PCTs) 
 
The following PCTs were recorded within the site: 
 
▪ PCT 3320 – Cumberland Shale Plains Woodland 
▪ PCT 3975 – Southern Lower Floodplain Freshwater Wetland 
 
In addition, PCT 4023 in low condition was recorded within the site outside of the area of non-certified 
land. As PCT 4023 is in the certified land under the  Cumberland Plain Conservation Plan (CPCP) land, 
it is not the subject to this BDAR and this PCT is not discussed further. 
 
Threatened Ecological Communities (TECs)

which are set out below: 
 
▪ Cumberland Plain Woodland  
▪ Sydney Freshwater Wetlands

listed under the EPBC Act.

7.16.2 Assessment of Impacts 
Avoid and Minimise Impacts  
 
The measures undertaken to avoid and minimise impacts on biodiversity values, including prescribed 
impacts, are to be in accordance with the Biodiversity Assessment Method (BAM). The primary 
approach to reducing impacts on biodiversity values within the proposed development site has been 
through avoidance and/or minimisation of native vegetation and fauna habitat removal. The key 
measures have been set out below: 
 
▪ The site has been strategically selected within the M RP, which predominantly comprises 
certified land identified for development as urban capable land under the Cumberland Plain 
Conservation Plan. 
▪ The small area of non -certified land contains fragmented, disturbed patches of vegetation 
exhibiting limited connectivity within a highly modified landscape. 
▪ Direct impacts to non -certified native vegetation have been limited to 1.35 hectares, with the 
majority of vegetation removal (18.03 hectares) occurring within certified land areas of the 
proposed development site. 
▪ Access roads have been designed to utilise the existing Mamre Road corridor and new roads 
being delivered to the south of the site , thereby avoiding any exacerbation of existing vehicle 
strike risk to native fauna currently associated with adjacent roadways. 
▪ Built infrastructure has been positioned centrally within the proposed development site to 
minimise edge effects.  
▪ Landscaping has been strategically positioned along the external boundaries (northern, 
eastern, western, and southern perimeters) of the proposed development site.  
▪ Post-construction landscaping will establish connectivity between the proposed development 
site and surrounding vegetation within the assessment area, including vegetation located 
north of Bakers Lane.  
▪ The final development footprint has been designed through iterative planning to minimise 
impacts on biodiversity values throughout construction and operational phases.  
▪ Prescribed impacts and indirect impact zones have been identified, mapped, and will be 
managed through targeted mitigation measures. 
 
The proposal has sought to avoid and minimise biodiversity impacts through strategic site selection 
within CPCP -certified urban capable land and through careful development design that positions 
infrastructure to minimise impacts to adjacent biodiversity values. Whilst the proposal will result in the 
removal of the entire non-certified land area, including 1.35 hectares of native vegetation, 
comprehensive consideration has been given to avoiding and minimising indirect and prescribed 
impacts to biodiversity  values in the broader assessment area. The mitigation and management 
measures detailed in Section 7.16.3 will be implemented throughout pre -construction, construction, 
and post-construction phases to adequately address residual impacts associated with the proposal. 
 
Direct Impacts 
 
Direct impacts include vegetation clearing, dam infill and non -native vegetation removal calculated 
from the area of the proposal footprint in the area of non-certified land. Direct impacts arising from the 
proposed development include: 
 
▪ Removal of 1.36 ha of native vegetation comprising: 
o 0.63 ha of PCT 3320 in Moderate (Regrowth) condition of which all is consistent with 
the BC Act listed CEEC Cumberland Plain Woodland . One patch of PCT 3320 in 
Moderate - regrowth condition (0.19 ha) is consistent with the EPBC Act listed CEEC 
Cumberland Plains Shale Woodlands as well as the BC Act CEEC. 
o 0.30 ha of PCT 3320 in Low condition consistent with the BC Act listed CEEC 
Cumberland Plain Woodland. 
[[page 212]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  206  
o 0.01 ha of PCT 3320 in DNG condition consistent with the BC Act listed CEEC 
Cumberland Plain Woodland. 
o 0.40 ha of PCT 3975 in Moderate condition consistent with the BC Act listed EEC 
Freshwater Wetlands on Coastal Floodplains. 
▪ Removal of Southern Myotis foraging habitat, consistent with PCT 3975 and PCT 3320 within 
200 metres of dams including: 
o 0.01 ha of PCT 3320 in DNG condition. 
o 0.26 ha of PCT 3320 in Low condition. 
o 0.63 ha of PCT 3320 in Moderate - regrowth condition.

(i) Removal of 0.40 ha of PCT 3975 of potential habitat for Maundia triglochinoides and 
Persicaria elatior (assumed present).

Serious and Irreversible Impacts 
 
Cumberland Plain Woodland in the Sydney Basin Bioregion is considered to meet the principles for 
Serious and Irreversible Impacts (SAII) and will be impacted by the proposal. The CEEC meets SAII 
principles due to its rapid rate of decline, severe degradation through fragmentation, weed invasion, 
and structural changes, and restricted geographic distribution. The community has undergone large 
reductions in both geographic dist ribution and ecological function, with remaining patches typically 
small, isolated, and subject to ongoing threatening processes. 
 
The proposal will impact 0.94 ha of this CEEC in the area of non-certified land, representing 0.004% of 
the community's total NSW extent. While this is a small proportional impact, the community's critically 
endangered status and demonstrated susceptibility to ongoing threats means any removal contributes 
to cumulative impacts on this highly threatened ecosystem. The impacted vegetation occurs in varying 
conditions and is already highly fragmented and edge affected. A detailed SAII assessment addressing 
all four (4) SAII principles has been included in Appendix 4 of the BDAR. 
[[page 222]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  216  
7.16.3 Management and/ or Mitigation Measures     
Construction and operational management plans will contain an adaptive management component. 
Adaptive management strategies will be receptive to any new and relevant data that may arise through 
ongoing assessment and monitoring and are key to the successful implementation of crucial objectives 
yet also allow flexibility to changing dynamics and ongoing feedback and results. This includes 
measures to monitor predicted and uncertain impacts in the proposed development site which will 
trigger adaptive manageme nt actions and allow for effective and quick responses.  Measures to 
mitigate or manage impacts have been set out below: 
 
▪ Native vegetation removal and displacement of resident fauna including: 
o Implement Construction Environmental Management Plan (CEMP) with vegetation 
clearance, hollow-bearing tree removal, dam infill, and fauna injury protocols; 
o Two-staged hollow -bearing tree removal: ecologist inspection immediately before 
removal; and 
o Check all vegetation for fauna before clearing; implement unexpected species find 
procedure, if detected;  
▪ Direct impacts to waterways and waterbodies including: 
o Appoint qualified Project Ecologist to oversee waterway/waterbody works; 
o Stop works if unexpected threatened species identified; 
o Install erosion and sediment controls; and 
o Implement dam dewatering plan. 
▪ Introduction and spread of weeds including: 
o Implementation of the Weed Eradication and Management Plan (WEMP) (Appendix 44) 
directly after construction and considered during operation and construction works. 
▪ Indirect impacts on native vegetation and habitat including: 
o Install stormwater and erosion controls throughout construction;  
o Install Tree Protection Zone (TPZ) exclusion fencing around retained trees; 
o Use "No Go Zone" signage; identify protected areas in site inductions; 
o Restrict stockpiles, parking, and machinery to cleared areas only; and 
o Mulch cleared native vegetation for re-use in landscaping. 
▪ Mitigating prescribed biodiversity impacts including: 
o Prepare plans showing cleared vs. protected areas, exclusion zones, and habitat features ; 
and 
o Develop clearing protocols for all vegetation removal. 
▪ Adaptive management strategies proposed to monitor and respond to impacts on biodiversity 
values that are uncertain including: 
o Implement CEMP during works; and 
o Prepare Tree Retention Plan and Landscape Plan.  
7.17 Human Health     
A Human Health Impact Assessment (HIA) was prepared by E -Lab Consulting  (refer Appendix 45 ), 
which integrates the findings of the relevant technical assessments in the EIS and has been prepared 
in accordance with the relevant NSW and national health guidance, including:  
  
• enHealth Health Impact Assessment Guidelines (2018); 
• enHealth EHRA Guidelines (2012); and 
• Relevant EPA and NEPM air and noise criteria and other relevant legislation.   
  
The purpose of the HIA is to evaluate potential human health impacts associated with the construction 
and operation of the proposed development. The HIA highlights that the potential impacts of the 
proposed development on community health and wellbeing include air quality, noise, and urban heat.   
[[page 223]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  217  
The HIA integrates issues raised via the statutory EIS consultation process, including concerns from 
adjacent educational facilities, the Emmaus Retirement Village, and other neighbouring industrial 
users.  
  
The HIA focuses on the plausible exposure pathways of environmental detriments on surrounding 
populations, including:  
 
• Air quality: construction dust emissions and diesel generator emissions.; 
• Noise and vibration: construction equipment, operational mechanical plant, and 
emergency/maintenance generator noise; and 
• Urban heat: altered microclimate from large impervious surfaces and building mass.  
7.17.1 Existing Environment  
The site is located within the IN1 General Industrial zone within the MRP. Population density analysis 
using 2024 census data shows that the immediate site area has "very low" density (0-500 persons/km²), 
indicating a predominantly industrial area.   
  
Identified Receivers  
  
Receiver locations assessed in the NVIA, AQIA and Urban Heat Island Effect (UHIE) assessment include 
residential, educational, industrial, places of public worship, recreation, and childcare. The nearest 
sensitive receivers to the site are located to the north and include the Mamre Anglican School, Trinity 
Catholic Primary School , Emmaus Catholic College, Emmaus Retirement Village and some rural 
dwellings on sites transitioning to industrial uses.  
  
The HIA notes that the NVIA categorisations for sensitive receivers are based on whether they are inside 
or outside the MRP rezoned area and if they are expected to exist in the long-term or only in the short 
term (transitioning to industrial uses or permently relocating):  
 
• Receiver catchments inside the MRP (long-term).  
• Receiver catchments inside the MRP (short-term).  
• Receiver catchments outside of the MRP.  
  
The HIA also notes that the AQIA receptor locations are representative rather than exhaustive, chosen 
to reflect typical exposure conditions within their surrounding land uses. In areas where multiple 
sensitive uses occur close together (for example, a school adjacent to a retirement village), the receptor 
nearest to the emission source was selected to represent potential impacts for all nearby sensitive uses.  
  
In terms of the potential for urban heat island effect (UHIE), the HIA notes that the site and the 
surrounding environment have the following characteristics:  
 
• Local topography is generally flat to gently undulating.  
• Prevailing winds are predominantly from the east during warmer months and from the 
west to northwest during cooler months.  
• Land uses within the surrounding area are primarily industrial and semi -rural, with 
large impervious areas such as roads, hardstands, and warehouse roofs interspersed 
with sparse vegetation, which contributes to localised heat retention.   
 
Overall, the HIA suggests the baseline microclimate of the Mamre Road site reflects a heat -prone 
environment.  
[[page 224]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  218  
7.17.2 Assessment of Human Health Impacts Associated with Air Quality      
The HIA included a detailed review of the Air Quality Impact Assessment (AQIA) prepared by Northstar 
Air Quality. The potential air quality human health impacts associated with the proposed development 
are considered to be:  
 
• Short-term respiratory effects (e.g. irritation, coughing, exacerbation of asthma) 
associated with elevated short -term concentrations of particulates and NO₂ during 
dust-generating construction activities or emergency generator operation.  
• Nuisance impacts (e.g. visible dust deposition) near the site during construction if 
dust is not adequately controlled.  
• Low long -term health risk from chronic exposure, as operational emissions under 
normal generator testing scenarios are limited in duration and predicted annual 
average concentrations comply with relevant NSW EPA and NEPM criteria.  
• Negligible health risk from toxic air pollutants (BTEX, PAH, formaldehyde), which are 
predicted or assumed to be present at negligible background concentrations and 
assessed against conservative criteria.  
 
The HIA includes an air quality risk assessment to determine the potential for the impacts mentioned 
above to adversely affect the health of sensitive receptors during construction, operation – emergency 
scenarios, and operation – maintenance testing. The results of the risk assessment are provided in 
(TABLE 68) and show that the inherent risks related to air quality impacts range from low to high; 
however, once the probability of occurrence and the proposed management and mitigation measures 
in the AQIA are taken into account, the residual risk levels are considered low or very low.

7.17.3 Assessment of Human Health Impacts Associated with Noise      
The HIA included a detailed review of the Noise and Vibration Impact Assessment (NVIA) prepared by 
Renzo Tonin & Associates. The potential construction phase noise -related human health hazards 
associated with the proposed development are considered to be:  
 
• Airborne construction noise from high-noise plant.  
• Out of hours work.  
• Construction traffic noise.  
• Cumulative construction noise if works overlap with other major project construction 
in the locality.   
  
The potential operational phase noise -related human health hazards associated with the proposed 
development are considered to be:  
 
• Continuous noise emissions from cooling systems, transformers and other mechanical 
plant.  
• Noise associated with the regular testing of diesel generators  
• Noise associated with emergency events (power failure).  
• Low frequency noise (LFN)  
 
These construction and operational noise hazards could cause human health impacts, including low -
level annoyance, reduced outdoor or indoor acoustic amenity, reduced speech intelligibility outdoors, 
temporary stress responses during generator testing and m ild stress responses among noise-sensitive 
individuals (e.g., children, elderly residents).   
  
The HIA includes a noise risk assessment to assess the potential impacts of the above -noted hazards 
on the health of sensitive receptors during construction and operation. The results of the risk 
assessment are provided in TABLE 69 and show that the inherent risks associated with noise impacts 
range from low to high, however, once the probability of occurrence and the proposed management 
and mitigation measures in the NVIA are considered, the residual risk levels are all low-medium or low.

The HIA concludes that while the facility will introduce continuous and intermittent noise sources 
typical of a data centre, the majority of sensitive long -term receptors are not expected to experience 
adverse health effects due to the significant mitigation incorporated into the design. Minor, short-term 
impacts may occur for transitional residential receivers within the MRP, primarily in the form of 
annoyance, reduced amenity, or temporary stress responses during generator testing or emergency 
scenarios. These impacts are limited in duration, scope, and intensity, and do not pose significant long-
term health risks.  
7.17.4 Assessment of Urban Heat Island Impacts       
UHIE refers to the phenomenon where urbanised areas experience higher ambient temperatures than 
surrounding rural or vegetated regions. UHIE mainly results from heat -absorbing materials like 
concrete and metal surrounded by limited vegetation. The gradual release of heat from these surfaces 
can increase nighttime temperatures, resulting in higher cooling energy requirements and affecting 
occupant comfort.   
  
The HIA used a microclimate simulation that integrated geometry, building features, and 
meteorological data to estimate surface heat fluxes and radiative balance related to the proposed 
development. The model details for the proposed development noted that post-development, the site 
would contain the following characteristics:  
 
• Footprint density 24%  
• Façade to site ratio 61%  
• Vegetation cover 36%  
  
The simulation found that the maximum daily air temperatures across the site would remain essentially 
unchanged, but the proposed development would contribute to minor localised nighttime warming.  
 
To better understand the potential human health impacts associated with the UHIE, the HIA included 
a Universal Thermal Climate Index (UTCI) assessment. Indicative views of the UTCI are provided in 
Figure 102, which shows that the proposed landscaping of the site reduces urban heat at the 
boundaries and around the buildings, but hardstand areas, including internal roads and carparks and 
the future Southern Link Road would cause increased temperatures. Overall,  the UTCI concluded that 
the potential UHIE of the proposed development has been effectively mitigated through site layout 
and landscaping but recommended some additional measures to further reduce the urban heat 
caused by hardstand areas with little shading.   
  
[[page 239]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  233  
 
Figure 102. Typical Summer Midday UTCI  
Source: Blackash, Bushfire Consulting, 2025 
7.17.5 Management and Mitigation Measures       
The HIA reviewed the comprehensive management and mitigation measures proposed in the AQIA 
and NVIA and concluded that these measures would effectively reduce air and noise impacts to 
acceptable levels, thereby decreasing the potential for human health impacts in the surrounding area. 
The HIA did not recommend any additional mitigation measures related to air and noise impacts.   
  
Based on the outcomes of the UHIE and UTCI analysis, the HIA concluded that some minor additional 
mitigation measures can be integrated into the design to minimise the pro posed development's  
contribution to local heat build-up. The proposed mitigation measures and strategies include:  
  
• Incorporation of high reflectance roofing materials to reduce surface heat gain.   
• Utilisation of permeable and lighter-coloured paving materials for roads and hardstand areas.  
• Providing shading structures in high-activity outdoor zones.  
  
These measures would be implemented as part of the detailed design of the proposed development 
carried out as part of the construction certificate documentation.   
  
The HIA concluded that implementing these measures would improve local thermal comfort and 
reduce the proposed development’s overall UHI impact, supporting long -term environmental and 
operational performance objectives.  
7.18 Social      
Social impacts are generally experienced when a new project brings change. The social impact of a 
proposal includes the direct and indirect effects on people and their communities throughout the 
proposed development’s lifecycle. A Social Impact Assessment (SIA) is an independent and objective 
study that identifies, analyses, assesses, manages, and monitors a project's social impacts, both positive 
and negative. These impacts have been assessed in accordance with the DP HI’s Social Impact 
Assessment Guidelines (2025).  
 

[[page 240]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  234  
Within the context of the site and its surroundings, the transformation of the Mamre Road Precinct 
from rural to industrial land use represents one of the most significant cumulative development 
programmes in Western Sydney. The concurrent delivery of mult iple industrial developments, 
including data centres, warehousing facilities, and supporting infrastructure, requires coordination 
among private developers, state agencies, and local governments. As such, the technical analysis of key 
issues such as air quality, noise, stormwater and traffic issues has taken the wider context into account.  
A key aspect in evaluating the significance of impacts has been the consultation with, and 
consideration of, the schools to the north of the site as sensitive receptors. As noted in the supporting 
Engagement Report, these considerations included reorganisi ng the Site Plan to facilitate larger 
setbacks to the schools, as well as reducing traffic impacts on Bakers Lane to focus access to the site 
from the emerging road network south of the site. Open communication has been successfully 
established to facilitate ongoing consultation throughout construction and operation periods.  
 
Overall, the proposal will deliver significant positive social impacts aligned with strategic planning 
objectives, including: 
 
• Economic Investment in Digital Infrastructure: With an estimated development cost for the 1.2 
GW facility of approximately $9 billion, this proposal will deliver hyperscale digital 
infrastructure, contributing to the economic use of land in the Mamre Road Precinct.   
• Activation of land in the Mamre Road precinct: Contributing to the vision for the globally 
competitive Western Sydney, and approximately  10,570 construction jobs and 800 ongoing 
jobs 
• Transport Connectivity: Delivering land dedication and new roads supporting the delivery of 
the precinct; and 
• High-quality industrial development:  Contributing to regional stormwater, tree canopy and 
amenity in the precinct. 
 
The assessment identified impacts during the construction period, whilst these are temporary in 
nature, due to the size of the site, there is a ten -year construction timeframe with distinct phases 0-7. 
In this context, it is important to consider the staged development of each of the six land parcels within 
the site and the ability to manage construction impacts in a staged way. These impacts are assessed as 
manageable and rely on the implementation  of a wide range of measures, including the following 
mitigation measures identified for impacts of medium to very high significance.  
 
Construction Phase 
 
Recommendation 1: Prior to construction commencement, prepare and implement a comprehensive 
Construction Environmental Management Plan (CEMP), to: 
 
• Ensure effective environmental and social management of the site during all construction 
phases, including bulk earthworks, road construction, and infrastructure delivery. 
• Address the social impacts identified in this SIA, particularly those relating to noise and 
vibration, dust and air quality, and traffic, to minimise disruption to the amenity, health and 
wellbeing of surrounding residents (including adjacent schools) and other stakeholders. 
• Implementation of staged management of stormwater throughout the construction phases to 
meet Sydney Water requirements. 
• Include construction schedules, sequencing of works, approved hours of operation, 
environmental control measures, and a communications protocol for nearby schools, residents 
and businesses. 
• Coordinate dust suppression and air quality monitoring protocol in response to construction 
activity on site, noting concurrent activities with adjacent developments 
• Establish construction noise coordination protocols requiring notification to neighbouring 
sites before high-noise activities and staggered scheduling of impact equipment operation. 
[[page 241]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  235  
• Implement the Construction Air Quality Management Plan (Northstar) measures, including 
water carts, solid screening barriers, progressive stabilisation, and speed limits on sealed 
surfaces. 
 
Recommendation 2: Develop and implement a Construction Traffic Management Plan in conjunction 
with the CEMP, to: 
 
• Identify designated heavy vehicle routes and manage construction-related traffic movements. 
• Minimise disruption to road users by scheduling deliveries outside peak hours. 
• Incorporate traffic control measures at key intersections and access points to ensure safety for 
all road users. 
• Include site induction and training protocols on traffic safety for all workers. 
• Provide consistent, accessible communication to local residents and stakeholders about 
anticipated traffic conditions, access limitations, and construction updates. 
• Coordinate heavy vehicle movements with concurrent nearby projects to avoid cumulative 
impacts during network peak hours  
 
Recommendation 3: Implement Aboriginal cultural heritage management measures as outlined in 
the Aboriginal Cultural Heritage Assessment (Biosis) through: 
 
• Development of a Cultural Heritage Management Plan in collaboration with Registered 
Aboriginal Parties prior to ground disturbance. 
• Coordination with adjacent developments to prevent consultation fatigue and ensure 
consistent engagement protocols. 
• Provision of community collection opportunities for the seven identified AHIMS sites before 
bulk earthworks commence. 
• Integration of heritage interpretation elements into the landscape design in partnership with 
local Aboriginal stakeholders. 
 
Recommendation 4: Implement the measures outlined in the Landscape Plan (Geoscapes), and 
Arboricultural Impact Assessment (Creative Planning Solutions), to: 
 
• Offset for the non-certified land as has been identified 
• Appropriate planting and landscaping vegetation to create the desired local character  as 
envisioned in the DCP for Mamre Road Precinct 
• Create a cohesive, resilient, and locally responsive environment within the site in line with the 
DCP requirements as well as the  proposed development requirements (as Data Centre 
Campus require specific functional and security requirements ) 
• Street trees to planted that align with the Mamre Road Penrith Council’s Street Tree 
Masterplan to create a cohesive public domain outcome 
 
Recommendation 5: Maintain effective community and stakeholder relations to ensure effective long-
term environmental management and integration: 
 
• Establish a program for community liaison meetings addressing construction / operational 
impacts and emerging concerns. 
• Participate in precinct -wide infrastructure planning where relevant to address cumulative 
impacts/demands of infrastructure/servicing. 
 
Operational Phase 
 
Recommendation 6: Operational noise impacts across the data centre campus are to be managed by:

706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)

• Implement the acoustic attenuation measures as specified in the Noise and Vibration Impact 
Assessment (Renzo Tonin  and Associates ), including acoustic screening, attenuators and 
enclosed plenum spaces. 
• Maintain a complaints register to address any noise -related queries from neighbouring 
properties 
• Restrict concurrent generator testing to a maximum of six units and coordinate with adjacent

Recommendation 7:  Urban heat island effects and climate resilience to be addressed through 
implementation of measures outlined in the ESD Report (E-Lab Consulting), including: 
 
• Achievement of increased tree canopy coverage, prioritising western and northern boundaries. 
• Installation of high -albedo surfaces on all rooftops and extensive paved areas to reduce heat 
absorption. 
 
Recommendation 8: Once the data centre is fully operational, the infrastructure in the precinct will 
continue to be delivered, including the  future Southern Link Road, the potential Western Sydney 
Freight line and the associated Intermodal and regional stormwater systems. To ensure coordinated 
delivery, it is recommended that the proponent continue: 
 
• To engage with TfNSW, considering the realisation of the complete road network envisaged 
for the Mamre Road Precinct  
• To engage with Sydney Water to coordinate matters regarding the realisation of the Precinct’s 
complete stormwater management and detention systems 
 
Recommendation 9:  Maintain effective community and stakeholder relations by recalibrating the 
program established during construction, as needed, for community liaison to address operational 
matters, such as testing of backup power systems. 
 
The long-term success of the Mamre Road Precinct is dependent on precinct transformation, which 
requires ongoing coordination of State and local Government and the private sector over the coming 
decades. In particular, support for the relocation of schools  should be considered as part of the wider 
transformation of the precinct, particularly as the precinct -level regional stormwater system and 
regionally significant future freight line and associated intermodal impacts are progressed.

mechanisms; and

Plume Rise Assessment  
 
Any plume rise that exceeds the critical velocity of 4.3 m/s at the point of exit must be assessed by 
CASA. The AQIA at Appendix 16 details that CASA has reviewed the proposal and confirmed that “the 
plume from this development will not be a hazard to aircraft operations and no CASA mitigation are 
required. CASA has no objection to the proposal as detailed below.” 
 
Lighting  
 
The proposal is situated outside the light control zones and outside the 6km assessment trigger radius, 
as such there is no further action required. 
 
Prescribed Airspace  
 
The site is within the WSI OLS and at a maximum elevation of 107.5 m AHD would remain below the 
OLS. The PANS-OPS airspace protections are not yet published for the airport however it is expected 
the OLS would be lower. 
 
Construction Impacts  
 
Any p enetrations of prescribed airspace by construction plant and equipment during construction 
constitutes a controlled activity under the Airports (Protection of Airspace) Regulations 1996 . 
Construction equipment and methodologies will need to be assessed, and any penetrations of 
prescribed airspace will require approval under the Airports (Protection of Airspace) Regulations 1996.

in detail it should be submitted to WSI for approval.

7.20.1 Assessment of Impacts       
The Bushfire Hazard Assessment recommends the following bushfire measures for the proposal which 
are based upon the relevant provisions of the PBP, as set out below: 
 
▪ Asset Protection Zones: At the commencement of building works and in perpetuity, the site 
shall be maintained as an Asset Protection Zone (APZ). The APZ shall be established and 
maintained as an inner protection area as outlined within PBP and the NSW RFS document 
‘Standards for Asset Protection Zones’. 
▪ Fire Hydrants: Fire hydrants are provided in accordance with Building Code of Australia E1.3, 
AS2419.1:2005, including the ring main requirements for large, isolated buildings and those 
identified in Section 9. 
▪ Compliance with Relevant Standards: The part of buildings affected by BAL (Figure 8) is to be 
constructed to comply with the National Construction Code (2019), Australian Standard AS 
3959:2018, Construction of buildings in bush fire -prone areas  and/or NASH Standard (1.7.14 
updated), National Standard Steel Framed Construction in Bushfire Areas – 2014, and Section 
7.5 of PBP on a prescriptive (deemed to satisfy and/or acceptable solution) basis and/or 
performance basis. 
▪ Roads: All proposed roads within the site must comply with section 5.3.2 of PBP, as appropriate.

In relation to temporary, e -waste and bulky waste storage, the waste storage strategy establishes a 
hierarchical system from point-of-generation to final collection, ensuring operational efficiency across 
all waste streams. Temporary storage at source (daily -capacity bins in offices, kitchens, and back -of-
house areas) feeds into centrali sed main bin storage areas through daily cleaning staff collection 
rounds. Specialised streams, including e-waste (660L bins with flexible collection frequencies during 
[[page 255]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  249  
maintenance or upgrades) and bulky waste (ground -level WSRA storage with prompt contractor 
removal), are managed separately to prevent cross-contamination and maintain storage capacity. This 
integrated approach provides adequate segregation, convenient access points, and scalable capacity 
responsive to operational fluctuations while preventing overspill and maintaining campus amenity. 
7.21.2 Management and/or Mitigation Measures        
The Waste Management Plan has identified internal management protocols to ensure waste is suitably 
managed as part of the proposal , broken down into the various collection waste streams expected to 
be generated at the site: 
 
▪ General Waste: General waste will be placed within a tied plastic bag prior to transferring into 
collection bins. For collection purposes, general waste shall be stored within a mobile garbage 
bin. 
▪ Commingled Recycling: All recyclables will be stored in commingled bins (mixed plastic, 
paper, cardboard, glass, aluminium, steel). All recyclables should be decanted loose (not 
bagged) with containers un-capped, drained and rinsed prior to disposal into the recycling bin. 
Paper should be flattened and placed in paper and cardboard bin if applicable. 
▪ Paper and Cardboard: Should large quantities of paper and carboard waste be generated from 
proposed site uses a separate service may be suitable for application at the site. The contracted 
waste service provider may be able to provide separate paper and cardboard bins for the 
source separation and collection of paper and cardboard waste. 
▪ Garden Waste: It is expected that landscaping at the site will be maintained by an external 
contractor who will remove all vegetation waste from ongoing maintenance activities. 
▪ Food Waste: Arrangements for separate collection of food organics can be made with the 
waste management contractor. Food waste can be stored in 240L sealed bins or refrigerated 
waste storage prior to collection.  
▪ Secure Documents: Separate bins for secure document waste may be retained in office spaces, 
to be serviced by a specialist secure document destruction contractor. 
▪ E-waste: E-waste generated from the data centre buildings and inventory warehouse will be 
managed through a dedicated e -waste collection and recycling contract, or by returning 
materials directly to product suppliers. 
▪ Plastic packaging waste:  Soft plastics and expanded polystyrene (EPS) can be consolidated 
and sent to specialist soft plastic and EPS recyclers.  
▪ Wooden packaging waste: Bulky packaging waste such as wooden pallets and timber crates 
shall be managed through either internal reuse, return to supplier or sent to pallet recovery 
companies. 
▪ Other (Problem) Waste: The disposal of hard, bulky, liquid or potentially hazardous wastes 
shall be organised by the site user as necessary. 
 
The above measures would ensure that the proposal is consistent with the aims, objectives and 
guidance in the NSW Waste and Sustainable Materials Strategy 2041. 
7.22 Cumulative Impacts       
In accordance with DPHI’s Cumulative Impact Assessment Guidelines for State Significant Projects, 
projects and land uses within proximity to the site have been considered in the potential cumulative 
impacts of the proposal. Key cumulative impacts include t raffic, noise and vibration, air quality, visual 
amenity, and construction works have been assessed, and mitigation measures have been 
recommended.

It is considered that Roads 1 and 2 may be delivered through a Works In Kind Agreement (WIKA) as 
both roads are identified in the Mamre Road Precinct Development Contributions plan to be dedicated 
to Council.  The WIKA may be negotiated prior to determination of the proposal.  
 
In terms of state contributions , further negotiation is required to determine if there is opportunity for 
Works in Kind or a similar agreement can be entered into for land dedication to provide for state 
infrastructure such as Mamre Road and the future SLR. However, only projects listed in the 
Infrastructure Opportunities Plan will be eligible.  As the framework and mechanisms are unclear at 
this stage further investigation is to be carried out.

P a g e  251  
PART 8: PROJECT JUSTIFICATION 
8.1 Overview 
The proposal is justified on environmental, social and economic grounds and is compatible with the 
locality in which it is proposed. The propos al would enhance the site from an otherwise vacant 
landholding to a productive employment generating facility.  
 
This EIS is submitted on the following basis. 
8.1.1 Supports State, Regional and Local Planning Objectives 
The proposal is consistent with the objectives, provisions and vision contained within A Metropolis of 
Three Cities – Greater Sydney Region Plan; the Western City District Plan; and the Industry and 
Employment SEPP . The proposal would contribute to employment generation in an area already 
earmarked for employment through both State and Regional planning policies.  
 
Data centres are critical infrastructure to support  secure regional and national government and 
businesses. The proposed development aligns with the Greater Sydney Commission's standards, 
objectives, and priorities. Additionally, it offers several advantages, including facilitating a distributed 
work model , reducing travel distances and associated costs for local workers, creating new 
employment opportunities, and contributing to the local economy through job creation near 
residential areas. 
8.1.2 Demonstrates an Appropriate Use of a Permissible Development 
The proposed development would retain and contribute to the growth of new industry for the 
immediate locality and the wider region. The proposed development would be a highly appropriate 
and compatible (given its contiguousness to other existing industrial developments) response to the 
strategic goals and objectives of the whole region as set out in A Metropolis of Three Cities – Greater 
Sydney Region Plan and the Central District Plan, which all envisage employment-generating land uses 
at this location. 
 
The permissibility  of development for six (6) data centre buildings, and associated infrastructure is 
prescribed by the Industry and Employment SEPP  which describes such development as permitted 
with consent in the IN1 General Industrial zone. Furthermore, pursuant to Section 2.31 of the Transport 
and Infrastructure SEPP, development for the purposes of data centres is permitted with consent in 
the IN1 General Industrial zone which is a prescribed zone for the purposes of this section. 
8.1.3 Minimises Environmental Impacts 
Specialist consultants have assessed the potential impacts of the proposed development, determining 
that it could be undertaken with minimal environmental impacts. The commissioned reports have 
collectively concluded that no significant risk to the localit y would result from the proposed 
development. Where impacts have been identified, these fully-developed strategies are set out in detail 
for management and mitigation. 
8.1.4 Addresses the Likely Impacts of Development  
Where impacts are identified, this EIS and accompanying technical reports explain why they are 
acceptable and how they can be mitigated. Environmental impacts on both the natural and built 
environments, and social and economic impacts in the locality have been considered and addressed. 
[[page 258]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  252  
8.1.5 Delivers Ecologically Sustainable Development 
The principles of ESD as outlined in Clause 193 of the EP&A Regulation have been carefully considered 
in the formulation of this proposal and are addressed in Section 7.13 of this EIS. 
8.1.5.1 The Precautionary Principle 
The proposal will employ relevant environmental protection measures and risk assessments to ensure 
no irreversible environmental harm is acted as a result of the construction or operation of the proposed 
development. Energy reduction, water consumption reduction and waste reduction are all project 
commitments to reduce environmental impact.  
8.1.5.2 Inter-Generational Equity 
The proposal will ensure no irreversible environmental harm as a result of the construction or operation 
of the proposed development. The design team will address key elements such as energy, water and 
material use to ensure intergenerational equity is achieved. 
8.1.5.3 Conservation of Biological Diversity and Ecological Integrity 
The proposal is supported by a BDAR  to support removal of some vegetation that falls outside of the 
certified land . Offset credit obligations will be satisfied prior to the construction of the proposal  to 
ensure suitable management of impacts.  
8.1.5.4 Improved Valuation, Pricing and Incentive Mechanisms 
The proposal is committed to minimising waste throughout construction and operation by improving 
circularity and diversion rates. This will be achieved through data collection, identifying reuse 
opportunities, and exploring take-back programs with equipment suppliers to enable second -life use 
of equipment. These actions wil l help conserve resources, support decarbonisation, and reduce 
environmental impact. 
8.1.6 Investment into Australia’s Digital Economy 
The proposal is a multi -billion-dollar investment in NSW’s digital economy that is necessary to ensure 
Australia continues to remain competitive in the data storage sector within the APAC region. The global 
market value is estimated at US$384 billion (AU$582.4b) , and looking forward, the demand for data 
centre capacity is expected to require almost US$7 trillion in capital investment.  Whilst Australia is a 
smaller player in the global data centre market (accounting for 3% of total supply), it is poised to rapidly 
grow in response to consumer, industry and government -led demand. By 2030 , it is projected to 
require an almost doubling in its capacity (MW), necessitating AUD$26 billion in data centre 
investment. Data centres are critical to advanced economies and modern cities. Cities and countries 
with lower levels of data centre infrastructure and investment will undoubtedly struggle to remain 
competitive, attract investment and grow. 
 
The proposal is committed to aligning with the strategic goals and planning priorities within the MRP 
and WSEA whilst ensuring environmental impacts and planning considerations are adequately 
addressed when delivering the data centre campus. The proposal w ill harness large-scale processing 
and storage to remain competitive and boost the data storage sector in Western Sydney. 
8.1.7 Suitability of the site 
The proposals consistency with applicable regional and local strategies is demonstrated in the 
comprehensive environmental assessment, provided in Part 7 of this EIS, which includes an analysis of 
all potential impacts, which has been informed by the relevant consultant reports. Accordingly, the 
[[page 259]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Mamre Road Data Centre Campus 
706-752 Mamre Road, Kemps Creek NSW 2187 (Lot 10 DP 1280592)                                                                               
 
 
 
P a g e  253  
environmental assessment prescribes recommendations and mitigation measures (where necessary), 
to account for all identified potential impacts, by the proposal.  
 
Having regard to the characteristics of the site and its location, the proposal is suitable for the site for 
the reasons outlined in Section 3.3. 
 
The technical assessments prepared in support of the proposal have concluded that the site is suitable 
for the proposal. 
8.1.8 Public Interest  
The proposal is in the public interest as: 
 
▪ It provides employment opportunities in a zone that is intended for such development. 
▪ It can suitably co-exist with other land uses. 
▪ The proposal provides a significant economic injection as the EDC of the proposed 
development is $9 Billion. 
▪ All environmental impacts can be suitably managed, thus ensuring the zone objectives are 
satisfied. 
▪ The proposed development will support IT computing requirements for businesses and 
households, thus providing greater efficiency in how we work and live.

council, or other drainage or water authority (i.e. 
Sydney Water), provide full hydraulic details and 
detailed plans and specification of proposed works 
that have been prepared in consultation with, and 
comply with the relevant standards, the local council 
or other drainage or water authority 
▪ details of provisions for supplying potable water and 
disposal of wastewater including any agreements entered 
into with relevant authorities such as Sydney Water and 
Penrith City Council.    
Section 7.8 
15. Flooding 
▪ identify any flood risk on-site (mainstream and overland) 
having regard to adopted flood studies, the potential 
effects of climate change, and any relevant provisions of 
the NSW Flood Risk Management Manual (2023)   
Section 7.9 
▪ assess the impacts of the development, including any 
changes to flood risk on-site or off-site, and detail design 
solutions and operational procedures to mitigate flood risk 
where required 
Section 7.9 
▪ identify flood behaviour, flood constraints and risks on the 
site and adjoining areas including the potential impacts of 
climate change for the full range of events up to and 
including the probable maximum flood (PMF) event, 
supported by suitable mapping   
Section 7.9 
▪ include details of proposed management measures and 
controls to:   
o effectively address flood constraints to ensure the 
flood risks to the development and its users are 
acceptable 
o manage flood and associated emergency 
management impacts due to the development on 
existing communities and individual property owners / 
occupiers.   
Section 7.9 
16. Contamination 
▪ characterisation of the nature and extent of any 
contamination on the site and surrounding area 
Section 7.10 
▪ a Detailed Site Investigation (DSI) and a Remedial Action 
Plan, if the Preliminary Site Investigation indicates 
contamination is present and a DSI is required. 
Appendix 23 
17. Landscaping 
• an arborist report, tree protection plan and vegetation 
management plan 
Appendix 13 
▪ detailed plans showing suitable landscaping which 
incorporates endemic species, heights of trees at maturity 
Appendix 13
